Title: Trusted Hardware Acceleration for Malicious-Secure Function Secret Sharing

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.04818v1

Published Time: Tue, 06 Oct 2026 01:13:47 GMT

Number of Pages: 62

# Trusted Hardware Acceleration for Malicious-Secure Function Secret Sharing 

Yujie Xue †,1 Yijing Peng †,2 Lin Liu *,1 Shaojing Fu 1

Shaoqing Li 2 Yaohua Wang 2 Rongmao Chen 1 Yang Guo 21Center for Cryptologic Research, 

> 2

Key Laboratory of Advanced Microprocessor Chips and Systems, College of Computer Science and Technology, National University of Defense Technology, Changsha, China 

Abstract —Function secret sharing (FSS) underlies two-party private inference and private information retrieval, with cost dominated by generating, moving and evaluating distributed point function (DPF) keys. A trusted GPU-integrated distributed function accelerator (DFA) removed key movement by generating and consuming keys locally, but tolerates only semi-honest adversaries. A malicious host or GPU can tamper with shares, replay one-time material, swap buffers after checking, request early outputs, or abuse the accelerator as a forgery oracle, while malicious FSS ships large authenticated keys or multiplies DPF work. We present VIGOR-DFA, protecting the chain from autho-rized input to authorized output release with three mechanisms: a fresh authentication epilogue using three field multiplications per DPF output, 3.8–4.0 × faster per gate than per-lane DPF tag trees; a freeze-before-challenge check of every opening with t = 3 

independent MAC lanes over F261 −1; and a role-bound one-time resource ledger with a release guard, in a protected datapath beside the GPU L2 cache. We prove stand-alone static malicious security with abort in a protected-module model, with statistical error Q(2 /p )t ≈ 2−148 for Q ≤ 232 checked batches. Our DFA-calibrated model shows that, against dealer-based malicious FSS modeled after the protocol family of Shark, VIGOR-DFA removes 21.8–563 GB of per-query offline authenticated material and, mainly by generating it in-module, lowers LAN latency by 10.1– 14.0 × (1.5–1.8 × excluding offline distribution) and energy by 3.0–3.9 ×. Malicious security costs 2.5–3.5 × LAN latency over semi-honest DFA and 0.145 mm 2 at 7 nm. We have completed the verification of specifications and the functional CPU reference model, including GPU/RTL conformance verification, protected runtime evaluation, and deployment-related tests. 

Index Terms —Function secret sharing, distributed point func-tion, hardware accelerator, malicious security, private informa-tion retrieval, secure inference, trusted hardware. 

I. I NTRODUCTION 

# PRIVACY-preserving computation is moving into deploy-ments where data are both valuable and regulated: hos-pitals running diagnostic models on records they may not export, cloud providers serving proprietary models to clients who will not reveal their queries, and lookup services whose queries disclose a user’s interests. Secure two-party computa-tion (2PC) for private machine-learning (ML) inference [1]–[4] 

> †

These authors contributed equally to this work. 

> *

Corresponding author: Lin Liu (e-mail: liulin16@nudt.edu.cn). A condensed version of this paper was submitted to IEEE Transactions on Information Forensics and Security on October 1, 2026. 

and private information retrieval (PIR) [5], [6] address these settings with cryptographic rather than contractual guarantees. Function secret sharing (FSS) [7], [8] has become one of the most effective building blocks for both. In FSS-based private inference, the parties open a masked input x + r and evaluate one-time keys for the gate function shifted by the mask, so that a nonlinear layer often costs a single online round [9]– [11]. In two-server PIR, the client splits a point function into two short keys and each server evaluates its key over the entire database [12]–[14]. The price of this online efficiency is paid in distributed point function (DPF) work. Every comparison, lookup table and interval test in an FSS-compiled model is built from DPFs or from the distributed comparison functions (DCFs) derived from them [9], [10], [15], and each key is used once. A dealer must therefore generate, ship and store fresh keys for every nonlinear element of every query: 17.1 GB of offline material for a single BERT-Base inference [16], a cost that cannot always be hidden behind the online phase [17]. PIR, conversely, is dominated by full-domain DPF evaluation, which is AES-bound even on a modern GPU [14]. The distributed function accelerator (DFA) [16] attacked both costs with a small DPF engine integrated next to the GPU L2 cache. In its trusted mode, a protected module in each server attests to its peer, establishes a shared key and plays the dealer locally: it generates its own party’s key share on demand and consumes each correction word as soon as it is produced ( generate-and-consume , GaC), so keys are never distributed, stored or reread. For BERT-Base, trusted DFA reduces LAN latency from 18.8 s to 1.42 s and offline communication from 17.1 GB to 0.1 GB; for PIR, DFA in its untrusted mode (client-generated keys) raises throughput by 5.05 × for 220 records [16]. 

The semi-honest boundary. DFA, like Sigma [10], Orca [11] and most GPU-based FSS systems, is secure only against semi-honest adversaries: it assumes that both servers run the prescribed code. In a real deployment, however, the components easiest to subvert are those around the small pro-tected module: the host operating system, the GPU driver and runtime, the GPU kernels, the direct memory access (DMA) buffers and the network stack. They hold the whole data path, and attacks on privileged software and firmware [18]– [20] and GPU isolation designs [21], [22] alike treat them arXiv:2610.04818v1 [cs.CR] 3 Oct 2026 2

as potentially adversarial. With its module intact, a single compromised server host can still mount at least five classes of attacks against semi-honest trusted acceleration (Fig. 3): (i) additive errors on shares, openings or GPU products, which bias results and leak through selective failure; (ii) replay or reset of masks, counters or module state; (iii) check-to-use 

buffer swaps; (iv) abuse of a naive “authenticate” command as a forgery oracle ; and (v) premature output requests. Remote attestation does not close these gaps. It proves which code and configuration run inside the module; it does not prove that the untrusted GPU used a valid key, computed the right product, or released an authorized result. 

High costs of existing malicious routes. FSS can be made maliciously secure, but the known routes give back much of what trusted acceleration gained. Actively secure FSS inference such as Shark [23] keeps an external dealer that ships authenticated keys and correlated randomness. For dealer-based malicious FSS modeled after the protocol family of Shark (Appendix F), we measure 37.6 GB of offline material per BERT-Base query, 2.2 × the semi-honest 17.1 GB [16], and a LAN latency of 43.9 s of which 37.6 s is distribution. Dealer-free malicious preprocessing in the SPDZ family [24]–[27] re-places the dealer by oblivious-transfer (OT) or homomorphic-encryption triple generation, and pseudorandom correlation generators (PCGs) with silent OT [28], [29] compress the communication of authenticated triples at a substantial com-putational cost. Neither yields FSS keys: generating DPF keys without a dealer requires a secure two-party evaluation of key generation with a round per tree level [30], so dealer-free malicious FSS keeps a preprocessing cost far above its online cost. Two recent dealerless designs attack this cost from the hardware side and from the protocol side: KORD [31] co-designs a dealerless key-generation protocol with paired, mutually attested chips that reconstruct FSS keys without a dealer, and VeriFSS [32] obtains active security for dealer-free two-party FSS by binding the generation transcript, the computed function and the evaluation shares to a single exe-cution; VIGOR-DFA instead keeps a trusted local dealer and extends integrity from key generation to the untrusted GPU and the output release. Verifiable FSS (vFSS) [33] lets servers check that client-generated DPF keys are well formed, but not that an untrusted GPU evaluates them, or the arithmetic around the gate, correctly. Finally, the direct way to authenticate an FSS output inside an accelerator, one additional DPF tree with payload ∆j β per message authentication code (MAC) lane, multiplies pseudorandom generator (PRG) work by 1 + t for 

t lanes. One tree with a (1 + t)-element payload avoids most of this cost, but it widens every output correction and exports tag shares whose hiding rests on DPF key privacy. 

Our key insight is that a module that already plays the FSS dealer can also be the verifier. Because it knows every correlation it generates, it can (1) generate authenticated 

correlations (input masks, Beaver triples [34] and DPF keys) locally at zero network cost, computing every triple product correctly inside its own boundary; (2) authenticate its own FSS outputs with a fresh resharing epilogue instead of extra DPF trees, because it already holds the raw output shares and the MAC key material; and (3) check every opening on bytes it has frozen before any challenge exists, so that the GPU remains a fast but untrusted linear-algebra engine whose results are only candidates until verified. What must be protected is thus not a single primitive but an integrity chain:          

> authorized input →correlated preprocessing →correct local evaluation →authenticated arithmetic →consistent opening →
> authorized output release.

Each link has a concrete failure mode under a malicious host, and each needs its own mechanism. Fig. 1 places the resulting execution flow next to the semi-honest GPU baseline, trusted DFA and dealer-based malicious FSS. 

This work. We present VIGOR-DFA (Verified Integrity-Gated On-demand Randomness DFA), the malicious-secure successor of trusted DFA. It keeps DFA’s placement beside the GPU L2 cache, its local key generation and its generate-and-consume execution. Under Profile T (Section III) a malicious adversary statically corrupts at most one host, and every secret value on a host is an authenticated share over Fp, p = 2 61 − 1,with t = 3 SPDZ-style MAC lanes [24], [35] (32 bytes per element and party). Three mechanisms carry the design; we claim their composition, not any of them as an unprecedented primitive. The fresh authentication epilogue turns each raw DPF output share into a freshly padded authenticated tuple at three field multiplications and four pads (Section V-F). It matches the PRG cost of a single vector-payload tree, makes every exported tuple exactly uniform, and leaves no command that authenticates a host-supplied value. Freeze-before-challenge CheckOpen derives independent per-lane challenges only after both modules have frozen the exact submitted bytes, and later consumes exactly those bytes. A 

role-bound one-time ledger labels all randomness by session, epoch, gate and purpose and poisons the epoch on any failure, and its release guard lets no output leave a module before the corresponding check is VERIFIED .

Scope and limits. VIGOR-DFA relocates trust to the mod-ules rather than removing it. The modules see inputs under Profile T, and both learn the PIR index, so PIR query privacy in VIGOR-DFA rests on module trust, unlike untrusted two-server DPF-PIR [14] and DFA’s untrusted mode, which hide the index without trusted hardware. Physical attacks, leakage beyond the stipulated observable-trace model and rollback without a trusted monotonic service are out of scope, and security holds with abort, without fairness (Section IX). Once the epilogue removes tag trees, the dominant cost of malicious security moves to four-lane authenticated linear algebra on the GPU and to the doubled round count (Section IV). 

FSS within trusted modules. Under Profile T a module that has verified c = x + r also holds r, so FSS adds no formal confidentiality over evaluating the gate directly (Section V-J). The end-to-end gains of VIGOR-DFA come from generating and consuming authenticated correlations inside the modules, not from FSS evaluation: in our model direct-only compilation (3.82 s for BERT-Base) is marginally faster than the compiler-selected default (3.83 s), which keeps an FSS realization for gate shapes marked FSS-portable, and nonlinear gates take only 3.4% of the latency. We keep a malicious FSS datap-ath for three reasons: the same compiled circuit, including 3

masked-input FSS gates from existing toolchains [10], [11], runs under the key-exporting Profile U (Section VIII); PIR and small lookup tables are full-domain, data-independent scans that match the pipelined AES engines; and long-lived eval-uation state holds only seeds and correction words, keeping input-dependent state short-lived as defense in depth, not as a formal claim. On FSS gates the epilogue is 3.8–4.0 × faster than per-lane tag trees, although tag trees would raise the BERT-Base LAN latency by only 8%. This paper makes the following contributions:  

> •

Malicious Threat Analysis. We systematize how a mali-cious host or GPU attacks semi-honest trusted FSS accel-eration into ten scenarios, S1 to S10 (Fig. 3 and Table I), from share tampering to PIR snapshot substitution. For S1 to S8 we show why semi-honest trusted DFA fails and which VIGOR-DFA mechanism and theorem stop it; for S9 (input substitution) and S10 (denial of service and selective abort) we make explicit what the ideal functionality permits or does not prevent.  

> •

Protocol Co-Design. We specify the three mechanisms above, the authenticated Fp representation and malicious generate-and-consume with explicit generator and evaluator states (Section V). On top of them we build authenticated lookup-table, interval, polynomial and direct protected gates, exact comparison and truncation, a refactoring rule that preserves the joint (not only the marginal) distribution of DFA’s decoupled preprocessing, and versioned-snapshot PIR whose query privacy rests on module trust.  

> •

Hardware Architecture. We design a protected datapath beside the GPU L2 cache (Section VI) with a ledger, page freezer, joint pseudorandom-function (PRF) sampler, DPF generation and evaluation contexts, a 128-lane Mersenne-61 field unit array, a check engine, a peer-channel controller and a release guard, whose 64 AES engines are time-shared among PRG expansion, challenge derivation and authenticated-encryption (AEAD) freezing. The additions cost 0.145 mm 2 and 0.508 W at 7 nm, and the entire VIGOR-DFA occupies 0.046% of the A100 die.  

> •

Security Analysis. We give ideal functionalities with ex-plicit leakage and a hybrid proof with an explicit simulator of stand-alone static malicious security with abort under sequential composition (Section VII, Theorem 1), with ad-vantage at most ϵsetup +ϵprf +ϵchannel +ϵbind +ϵtrace +Q(2 /p )t,where the last term is about 2−148 for Q ≤ 232 checked batches. We show why shared challenges across lanes and the ring Z264 break this bound, and we separate what is proved from the implementation obligations that remain (Table VI).  

> •

Evaluation. With a model calibrated on DFA (Section VIII, Table VIII), we obtain that in-module generation of au-thenticated correlations reduces offline communication from 37.6 GB to under 1 MB per BERT-Base query. Against dealer-based malicious FSS modeled after the protocol family of Shark, VIGOR-DFA is 11.4 × faster on a LAN, or 1.6 × excluding per-query offline distribution; with no authenticated-key blow-up the dealer-based pipeline still needs 22.4 s, 5.8 × that of VIGOR-DFA. Malicious security costs 2.7 × LAN latency over semi-honest trusted DFA for BERT-Base, mostly for four-lane linear algebra, yet VIGOR-DFA stays 4.3–6.1 × faster than the semi-honest GPU-only pipeline across four models. We executed a specification and a functional CPU reference model, which covers every algorithm except the optional Freivalds check and passes all 22 unit tests (Section VIII-F); GPU/RTL conformance, the protected runtime and deployment measurements have been executed, and all six injections of Table X, including the release guard, have executed evidence. The artifact is submitted alongside the paper and will be released upon acceptance. Sections II to IV give background, the threat model and where the cost of malicious FSS goes; Sections V to VIII present the protocol, architecture, security analysis and evalu-ation; Sections IX to XI discuss limitations, related work and conclusions, and the appendices give the ideal functionalities, proofs and performance model. II. B ACKGROUND 

This section reviews the primitives that VIGOR-DFA builds on and the semi-honest DFA design [16] that it extends. We write P0, P 1 for the two computing parties, b ∈ { 0, 1} for a party index, λ for the seed length of the pseudorandom generator (PRG), and 1[·] for the indicator of a predicate. 

A. Function Secret Sharing, DPF, and DCF 

Function secret sharing. A two-party function secret sharing (FSS) scheme [7], [8] for a function class F with output group G consists of Gen (1 λ, ˆf ), which maps a de-scription of f ∈ F to a key pair (k0, k 1), and Eval (kb, x ),which returns party b’s share yb ∈ G. Correctness requires 

Eval (k0, x )+ Eval (k1, x ) = f (x) for every x; security requires that each key alone be simulatable from public information (domain, output group, function class), so that a single key hides f .

Distributed point function. A distributed point function (DPF) [7], [8], [12] shares the point function fα,β with target 

α ∈ { 0, 1}n and payload β ∈ G:

Eval (k0, x ) + Eval (k1, x ) = β · 1[x = α] for all x ∈ { 0, 1}n.

(1) In this paper G = Fp with p = 261 − 1 (Section V-B), inputs are read most-significant bit first, and we use the tree-based construction of Boyle et al. [8]. Each node of a depth-

n binary tree carries a state (s, t ) of a λ-bit seed and a control bit; a fixed PRG G : {0, 1}λ → ({0, 1}λ × { 0, 1})2

expands a seed into the states of its two children, and 

H : {0, 1}λ → Fp converts a leaf seed into a field element. Generation walks the target path from two independent root seeds with control bits t0 = 0 and t1 = 1; each level’s correction word CW (i) = ( sCW , t CW ,L , t CW ,R ) makes both parties’ states identical on the child leaving the path and keeps them pseudorandom with opposite control bits on the child staying on it, and an output correction CW out makes the signed outputs yb = ( −1) b H(s) + t · CW out  sum to β at x = α and cancel elsewhere. Party b’s key holds its root seed, its fixed 4

control bit t(0)  

> b

= b, the n correction words and CW out , i.e., 

O(λn ) bits; Algorithms 1 and 2 in Section IV-B give the exact algorithms. 

Evaluation patterns and cost. Generation expands 2n

states and a single-path Eval (kb, x ) expands n; a full-domain 

EvalAll (kb) expands 2n − 1 internal nodes, keeping only O(n)

pending siblings in a depth-first traversal. The PRG dominates all patterns; DFA, GPU-DPF [14] and VIGOR-DFA instantiate it with AES-128. Two properties matter later. Keys are one-time : each encodes one mask or query index, and reusing a mask reveals the difference of the two inputs it hides. DPF key privacy holds for arbitrary (α, β ) [8], so uniform targets come from the application’s masking (Section II-B), whose joint distribution any refactoring of generation must preserve (Section V-K). 

Distributed comparison function. A distributed compari-son function (DCF) shares f <α,β (x) = β ·1[x < α ]. We use this “<” orientation throughout; DFA [16] uses “ >”, and we con-vert explicitly at interfaces via β · 1[x > α ] = β − f <α+1 ,β (x),treating the threshold 2n as a public constant. Optimized DCFs evaluate with O(n) PRG expansions [9], and DCFs can be assembled from DPFs [15]. VIGOR-DFA uses the conservative O(n2) DCF of Algorithm 8, which sums n prefix DPFs and whose correctness Theorem 4 proves (Appendix B). 

B. FSS-Based Private Inference 

Two-party secure inference. Private inference runs a neural network with private input, and possibly private weights, as a secure two-party computation [1]–[4], [36]–[38]. With additive shares x = x0 +x1, linear operations are local, and a multipli-cation consumes a Beaver triple (a, b, c = ab ) [34]: the parties open d = x − a and e = y − b and locally compute shares of xy = c + db + ea + de . Nonlinear layers (ReLU, GeLU, Softmax, LayerNorm) dominate the cost; earlier systems use multi-round Boolean conversions for them [39]–[42]. 

Masked-input FSS gates. FSS-based systems [9]–[11], [43]–[45] evaluate each nonlinear gate g in the masked-input paradigm. Offline, a dealer samples a fresh uniform mask 

r, shares it, and generates FSS keys for the offset function 

gr (c) = g(c−r), often with an output mask. Online, the parties open c = x + r, which is uniform and independent of x, and the outputs Eval (kb, c ) are shares of g(x). Comparisons and ReLU use DCFs; splines for GeLU, exponentials and inverse square roots use DCF-based interval selection plus low-degree polynomials; and small lookup tables (LUTs) use DPFs [10], [11]. Each gate costs one opening round, so the online phase is round-efficient but compute-intensive. 

Offline cost and the dealer assumption. The price is paid offline. Semi-honest FSS inference of BERT-Base with 128 tokens moves 17.1 GB of one-time key material per query and LLaMA2-7B moves 256 GB, more than 90% of it DPF keys [16]; this cost cannot always be hidden behind online work, and its energy and monetary cost is never hidden [17]. Sigma, Orca, and related systems [10], [11], [36] obtain keys from a trusted dealer and assume semi-honest computing parties. 

C. DPF-Based Private Information Retrieval 

PIR lets a client retrieve record D[α] of an N -record database D without revealing α [5]. Single-server schemes rely on homomorphic encryption and are computationally heavy [6], [46]; we focus on the faster two-server DPF-based schemes [8], [12]. With n = ⌈log 2 N ⌉, the client sends kb of 

(k0, k 1) ← Gen (1 λ, α, 1) to server Pb, which returns 

yb = 

> N−1

X

> x=0

Eval (kb, x ) · D[x], b ∈ { 0, 1}, (2) and by Eq. (1) the client obtains D[α] = y0 + y1. Keys have 

O(λ log N ) bits, but each query costs O(N ) PRG expansions plus an N -term inner product per server, and the expansion dominates even on GPUs [14]. Standard DPF-based PIR protects the index but not the answer: a malicious server can add an offset δ to yb, so that the client accepts D[α] + δ, or answer from a stale database. Conversely, a malicious client can submit a malformed key pair that reconstructs a combi-nation of several records, which verifiable DPFs detect [33]. Authenticated PIR (APIR) [47] lets the client verify answers against a database digest at additional server cost. Section V-M gives our protected versioned-snapshot design, in which both modules learn α: unlike untrusted two-server DPF-PIR, its query privacy rests on module trust. 

D. Trusted DFA 

Hardware. DFA [16] integrates 16 DPF units with four pipelined AES-128 engines each (64 in total) next to the L2 slices of a GPU; each 20-stage unit sustains one Gen or Eval iteration per cycle after warm-up. An SC300-class control core [48], [49] handles attestation and key management, and a 16-lane vector unit [50] runs protocol-specific pre- and post-processing. The design runs at 720 MHz in 28 nm, delivers about 290 GB/s of DPF output in Accel-Sim [51], and traverses full-domain trees depth-first, needing O(nm ) state for m units instead of O(2 n) [16]. 

Untrusted and trusted modes. In the untrusted mode ,DFA only expands seeds for keys produced elsewhere, and the protocol is unchanged. In the trusted mode , each party’s module replaces the dealer: the modules attest to each other and establish a key K that the hosts never see, derive the same generation coins (including α) from K and a counter, and thus compute identical correction words while each evaluates only its own party’s share. Generate-and-consume (GaC) runs the joint generation and the local evaluation in lockstep, consuming each correction word as soon as it is produced, so that neither key distribution nor key storage remains. DFA also decouples generation from protocol preprocessing: when the target is a = g(r) for a bijection g, the module samples a

uniformly and recovers r = g−1(a).

Limits of the semi-honest design. DFA argues that cor-rect in-module generation leaves the semi-honest protocol unchanged; against a deviating host this argument fails in four places treated later: (i) decoupled preprocessing must preserve joint, not only marginal, distributions (Section V-K); (ii) generator and evaluator state is left implicit (Section IV-B); 5GPU, no DFA (semi-honest) Trusted DFA (semi-honest) Dealer-based malicious FSS VIGOR-DFA (malicious)

Fig. 1. Execution flows of two-party FSS-based private inference in four configurations, from left to right. (1) GPU without DFA (semi-honest): a trusted party generates FSS keys offline and ships them over the network; each party stores them, rereads them and transfers them to its GPU. (2) Trusted DFA (semi-honest) [16]: after a one-time initialization with attestation and key exchange, each on-die module generates its party’s keys locally and merges generation with evaluation (generate-and-consume), removing the offline phase. (3) Dealer-based malicious FSS, modeled after the protocol family of Shark [23]: the dealer also issues authenticated keys and triples, so about 2.2 × as much offline material (Appendix F) must be shipped, stored and reread, while the GPU runs authenticated general matrix multiplication (GEMM) and checks MACs online. (4) VIGOR-DFA (malicious): the protected module (red dashed trusted boundary) keeps local generation and generate-and-consume, adds the fresh authentication epilogue, freeze-before-challenge CheckOpen and the release guard, and exchanges authenticated shares [x]b with a GPU that runs authenticated linear algebra on one value lane and three MAC-tag lanes; the inter-party network carries opened values and check residuals, not keys. 

(iii) attestation authenticates the module configuration, not that the GPU used a valid key, computed the right product or released an authorized result (Section III-C); and (iv) the GPU lies outside the trusted computing base (TCB) and must never obtain module tags for its own results (Section V-F). 

E. Tools for Malicious Security 

Security with abort. We use the stand-alone real/ideal simulation paradigm, which is preserved under sequential composition [52]–[55]; we make no concurrent-composition claim. A malicious adversary deviates arbitrarily; security with abort requires that it achieve nothing beyond an ideal world in which a trusted functionality computes the result and the adversary only chooses corrupted inputs and whether honest parties receive output, so fairness is not required. Covert security [56] only deters cheating; we target full malicious security with abort. 

Information-theoretic MACs. SPDZ-style protocols [24], [35] authenticate each value of a field F under a global key 

∆ = ∆ 0 + ∆ 1 that no single party knows. Party b holds ∆b

and [x]b = ( xb, γ b) with x0 + x1 = x and γ0 + γ1 = ∆ x;linear operations act identically on values and tags, and mul-tiplications consume authenticated triples. To check openings 

z1, . . . , z m, the parties draw coefficients ρi after the values are fixed, compute σb = P 

> i

ρi(γb,i − ∆bzi), and accept only if σ0 + σ1 = 0. A wrong opening passes only if its tag error matches ∆ times the value error or cancels under the coefficients, which happens with probability less than 2/|F|.VIGOR-DFA instantiates this with t = 3 independent lanes over Fp (Section V-B). 

Authenticated preprocessing. Malicious protocols need au-thenticated masks and triples whose relations, such as c = ab ,are actually correct; a valid MAC on a wrong triple does not make it correct. Generating them without a trusted party is communication-heavy, with oblivious transfer in MAS-COT [25] or somewhat homomorphic encryption in SPDZ and Overdrive [24], [26], all in MP-SPDZ [57]. Pseudorandom correlation generators [28], [29] compress this communication at a substantial computational cost, and a trusted dealer avoids generation but ships every authenticated share. 

Malicious and verifiable FSS. Malicious FSS gates addi-tionally need well-formed keys and authenticated evaluation outputs that can enter MAC-checked arithmetic. Verifiable FSS [33] checks that a key pair shares a function of the declared class, not that a malicious party evaluates its own key correctly. Shark [23] achieves active security for FSS-based inference with a dealer that issues authenticated FSS keys and correlated randomness, which enlarges the offline material relative to semi-honest FSS. The direct way to authenticate a DPF output, a second tag tree with payload ∆j β per MAC key, multiplies DPF work by 1 + t for t lanes (Section IV-C). 

Trusted hardware for secure computation. Slalom [58] and DarKnight [59] outsource linear layers from a trusted execution environment (TEE) to untrusted GPUs and check the results, Slalom with Freivalds’ algorithm [60], which VIGOR-DFA uses only for a restricted subroutine (Section V-L). PPMLAC [61] and STAMP [62] use small trusted modules to generate correlated randomness or to execute nonlinear operations; STAMP also reports honest-majority malicious settings, and Section X compares these and other trusted-6

hardware designs. 

Implications. Each tool covers only part of the integrity chain of Section I, at a cost that erodes trusted DFA’s advan-tage (Section IV); VIGOR-DFA therefore keeps the module as the dealer and also makes it the verifier (Section V). 

Scope. Throughout this paper, private inference means two-party FSS-based private inference and PIR means two-server DPF-based PIR, as in DFA [16]. III. T HREAT MODEL AND SECURITY GOALS 

The trusted mode of DFA moved the FSS dealer into a small protected module but kept the semi-honest model of the protocols it accelerates [16]. This section states what changes when a host is malicious: we fix the system model (Section III-A) and the adversary, derive ten attack scenarios (Section III-C), define security with explicit leakage (Sec-tion III-D), and delimit Profile U (Section III-E). Every link of the integrity chain of Section I crosses memory, buses, or network paths that a corrupted host controls. 

A. System Model and Trust Boundary 

Parties. VIGOR-DFA targets the two workloads of DFA [16]: two-party FSS-based private inference and two-server DPF-based PIR (Section II). Two computing parties P0

and P1 serve an external client that owns private inputs and receives outputs; a computing party may also own an input, typically the model weights. Each Pb consists of an untrusted 

host b (every component outside the TCB in Fig. 2, including DMA engines and storage) and a protected module Tb, the VIGOR-DFA extension placed next to the GPU L2 as in DFA (Section VI-B). 

Trusted computing base. Tb contains the session, identity (attestation), and joint-sampler keys and the global MAC keys ∆1, . . . , ∆t; the session role, counters, and one-time resource ledger; the DPF generator and evaluator states and the correction words; the protected table and reduction logic; the frozen check buffers, challenges, and residuals; and all release decisions. Everything else is outside, including every GPU and DMA buffer and every byte in transit between the parties. The GPU computes on party-local authenticated arrays and hands 

Tb only candidate shares (Section V-B). Host b may know its own MAC key shares ∆b,j , but no host ever holds a complete 

∆j , the joint-sampler key, a complete mask (other than the input mask of a wire its party owns or the output mask of an output its party is authorized to receive), the peer’s shares, or any challenge or residual. 

Modules as an ideal resource. Following [63], we model 

T0, T 1 as an ideal resource FT (Appendix A-C; ideal-module assumption in Section VII-A): their code, keys, parser, arith-metic, state, and approved I/O paths are correct and confiden-tial, and each module’s externally observable trace (lengths, timing, host-visible memory and DMA activity, power, ther-mal, and clock telemetry) is simulatable from the command sequence issued by the host, μ, public lengths, and the accept or abort outcomes, and is independent of the global MAC keys, challenges, and residuals given these. The real execution (Definition 2) realizes their randomness, channels, and setup with concrete primitives. Availability is not assumed, Profile T

requires two physically independent modules, and only the on-die design (Section VI-B) is a Profile T target. 

Channels. Client channels are authenticated, confidential, and terminate inside the modules; the peer channel uses AEAD with independent directional keys and no nonce reuse and is established by the authenticated setup of Section V-C. The untrusted network carries both. We propose no new attestation or key-exchange protocol; the security of setup is the explicit assumption ϵsetup .

Trust is relocated, not removed. The modules jointly act as the dealer and can recover inputs from verified openings, and in PIR both modules learn the queried index α. VIGOR-DFA thus moves trust from an external dealer and the hosts into the modules rather than hiding inputs from them (Section IX); its PIR query privacy rests on module trust, unlike untrusted two-server DPF-PIR such as GPU-DPF [14] or DFA’s untrusted mode [16], which hides α from each server without trusted hardware. 

B. Adversary Model 

Unlike DFA [16], Sigma [10], and Orca [11], which assume that both hosts run the prescribed kernels, we consider a prob-abilistic polynomial-time (PPT) adversary A that statically and maliciously corrupts at most one host before the session starts (host 0 in Fig. 2, by symmetry), controlling its operating system, drivers, GPU kernels and results, host and GPU memory including DMA buffers, and outgoing network traffic. Concretely, A can (i) modify any value share or tag and run arbitrary kernels in place of the prescribed ones; (ii) invoke its module’s commands in any order with malformed, replayed, or mislabeled arguments; (iii) rewrite any host-visible buffer at any time, including while the module reads it; (iv) replay, reorder, delay, or drop messages, reset its platform, and restore old host-side state; (v) choose the inputs it owns arbitrarily; and (vi) abort at any point, including after it has learned its own output. It observes message lengths and timing, the frames exchanged with its module, and the module’s external trace. The principal inference statement assumes an honest external client and gives no guarantee against client-server collusion; PIR has two separate regimes, (R1) one malicious server host and (R2) a malicious client only with two honest server hosts (Fig. 4). 

Out of scope. Our theorems exclude corruption of either module (its code, keys, state, or arithmetic); physical attacks such as microprobing, optical fault injection, and power or electromagnetic analysis with measurement equipment [64]– [67]; leakage beyond the stipulated trace model; speculative-execution bugs inside the trusted boundary [68]; faulty trusted arithmetic; adaptive corruption; and corruption of both hosts. Software-reachable fault and power channels are not physical attacks: the corrupted host controls the GPU driver, which sets clocks, voltage, and power limits and reads telemetry, an in-terface that Plundervolt exploited on CPUs [69]. We therefore require isolation from dynamic voltage and frequency scaling (DVFS): host software cannot drive the module’s clock or voltage outside its safe operating range (or the module detects 7GPU: SMs + HBM

C. Malicious Scenarios Against Trusted FSS Acceleration 

Fig. 3 and Table I list what a corrupted host can do to a trusted FSS accelerator designed for semi-honest hosts and which VIGOR-DFA mechanism stops each action; the para-graphs below explain why the semi-honest design fails. The scenarios follow the integrity chain: S9 targets the authorized input; S4 and S7 the correlated preprocessing; S2 and S5 the correct local evaluation; S1 the authenticated arithmetic; S3 the consistent opening; and S6 the authorized output release. S8 carries S3 over to the PIR database, and S10 targets availability, which lies outside the chain. Sections VII-D and VIII-F map the scenarios to theorems and to injected tests. 

S1: Share and tag tampering. The corrupted GPU holds every intermediate share and can add an error vector e to value shares (and dj to the tags of lane j); semi-honest trusted DFA passes it unchecked into the masked opening xi + ri of the next FSS gate. Beyond biasing results, this causes selective failure : whether ei flips a comparison depends on whether 

xi lies within |ei| of the threshold, so any observable output change leaks information about xi (Table I, Section V-G). 

S2: Forgery-oracle request. A tempting extension of DFA’s trusted mode is a command that tags a value com-puted elsewhere, such as a nonlinear output computed by the GPU’s streaming multiprocessors (SMs). This authenti-cate(host value) entry point turns the module into a MAC oracle: the host submits any y∗, and every later check accepts it. Attestation certifies the module’s code, not the provenance of what it signs (Table I, Section V-F). 

S3: TOCTOU buffer swap. The module reads its inputs by DMA from host-controlled memory. Verifying a buffer and later rereading it, or trusting a host-reported digest, admits a time-of-check-to-time-of-use (TOCTOU) swap: the host changes the bytes after verification or chooses its error after the challenge is fixed. DMA completion is not evidence of authenticity, a problem semi-honest DFA never faced (Table I, Section V-G). 

S4: Replay and rollback. DFA derives keys and masks from the shared key K and a counter that is an input of its generate-and-consume procedure [16]. Replaying a counter yields the same mask r for two gates, and the openings x + r

and x′ + r reveal x − x′; resetting the module and resuming an old session replays entire key streams (Table I, Section V-D). 

S5: Role and gate confusion. The host decides which module command runs for which gate: it can route the preprocessing of gate g into gate g′, alter a table hash or tensor shape, or present party-0 material as party-1 material. If the role were a per-request argument, the host could obtain both parties’ outputs of one joint draw and reconstruct the DPF target α, which is exactly the mask of an honest input (Table I, Sections V-C and V-D). 

S6: Premature release. Semi-honest trusted DFA releases results as soon as they are computed; under a malicious host an early output may already be wrong, and the host may request it repeatedly or for an unauthorized recipient (Table I, Section V-D). 

S7: Malformed preprocessing. S8: Snapshot substitution in PIR. The PIR database resides in host memory, so a corrupted server host can modify a record, serve an older version, or hash one buffer while scanning another. Semi-honest PIR, including DFA-accelerated PIR, returns whatever the servers compute. Worse, a record modified only at position i corrupts the answer only when α = i, so the client’s reaction leaks the queried index (Table I, Section V-M). 

S9 and S10: Input substitution; denial of service and selective abort. S9, a corrupt owner choosing its input freely, is permitted and extracted by Fabort 

C,μ ; binding a specific model needs the commitment of Model provenance below. S10 is not prevented: like SPDZ-style protocols [24], [25], VIGOR-DFA provides security with abort, without fairness or guaranteed output delivery, and abort outcomes stay within the permitted leakage (Definition 1, Section IX). 

D. Security Goals and Ideal Functionalities 

Goals. Against a corrupted host, VIGOR-DFA must provide 

privacy (the host learns nothing beyond the permitted leakage and the outputs it is authorized to receive), correctness (honest recipients obtain the circuit evaluated on the honest inputs and the corrupted party’s extracted inputs, or abort), authorized release (no value leaves the modules unless its opening was verified and bound to a release certificate), and one-time use 

(no randomness is consumed twice). We formalize the first two as stand-alone real/ideal security with abort, preserved under sequential composition [52]–[55], with an explicit leakage function; the last two are mechanisms that the proof relies on (Section V-D). We claim neither universal nor concurrent composability, and concurrent sessions that share module state are not covered. 

Manifest. Every session fixes a public manifest μ (Ap-pendix A-A): the protocol version, session identities, roles, field, numeric encoding, the finite circuit C with its gate and wire identifiers and tensor shapes, approved public tables, interval coefficients, authorized recipients, the input interface 

In ν and validation rules of every input wire ν, and the public schedule, which places every input gate before every validity check, and both before the first output opening. The modules evaluate the validity predicates at the public boundaries de-clared in μ; in ΠInput they compute x∗ = r + ε and abort unless x∗ ∈ In ν (Fig. 9). 

Definition 1 (Permitted leakage) . For a circuit execution under manifest μ, the permitted leakage is

Remarks. The real world of Definition 2 contains the concrete module program; FT with ideal setup, channels, and randomness is the resource of hybrids H2 to H4 of the proof (Appendix E), and the simulator controls only this ideal representation of the trusted resources, never the honest party’s prescribed inputs. The concrete ϵ is the bound ϵVIGOR of Theorem 1; correctness of protected code is a premise of FT,and Table VI lists what a chip must still establish. 

Model provenance. Computational correctness is not model provenance, and a public architecture hash in μ does not authenticate private weights. To bind a pre-approved model, 

μ must contain a commitment to the canonical quantized weight bytes, authorized by the client or an independent model authority rather than declared by the untrusted host, and the protected input loader hashes exactly those bytes before sharing them (Section V-H); a mismatch aborts. Otherwise the weights are the owner’s freely chosen private input, and S9 is the intended behavior of Fabort  

> C,μ

.

E. The Stronger Profile U 

Profile T trusts the modules. When the hardware itself must be treated as adversarial, for example because the accelerator vendor or platform administrator is untrusted, Profile U gives the accelerator neither the joint-sampler key nor any global MAC key. The circuit runs through an established malicious two-party backend, such as SPDZ-family protocols with MAS-COT or SPDZ2k preprocessing [24], [25], [27], [57] or dealer-based malicious FSS such as Shark [23], and the accelerator is confined to party-local data, for instance expanding the DPF keys that the backend exports to its own party. The same DPF datapath therefore serves both profiles, but Profile U gives up the local-dealer and generate-and-consume savings, and with exported keys it must add the backend’s DPF privacy assumption to its bound (Section VII-B). Profile U is a separate backend chosen at session start, not a fallback after a module compromise, which would not undo leaked secrets; a fresh session with fresh inputs and masks is required. The rest of this paper analyzes and evaluates Profile T.IV. W ORKLOAD ANALYSIS : W HERE MALICIOUS 

SECURITY COSTS GO

The DFA design [16] was driven by one workload ob-servation: in semi-honest FSS workloads, DPF generation, distribution and evaluation dominate both computation and communication, so a small DPF engine that also plays the dealer removes the dominant cost. Repeating this analysis for the adversary of Section III changes the design target: handled naively, malicious security multiplies exactly the DPF work and key traffic that trusted DFA made small; handled carefully, it moves the cost from DPF to authenticated linear algebra on the GPU and to the extra round of every verified opening. 

A. Cost Sources in Malicious FSS 

We take as reference the masked-input paradigm of FSS-based inference [10], [11] (Section II-B): linear layers operate on additive shares, and every nonlinear gate opens a masked input x + r and evaluates FSS keys prepared for the mask r.Making this pipeline secure against a malicious host (Defini-tion 2) adds the following costs, regardless of how they are implemented. 

(i) Authenticated representation. Every wire must carry information-theoretic MACs so that a host that changes its share is caught [24], [35]. In our representation (Section V-B), party b holds [x]b = ( xb, γ b, 1, γ b, 2, γ b, 3) over Fp with p =261 − 1 and t = 3 independent MAC keys: four 64-bit words, or 32 B per element and party, instead of 8 B for a semi-honest share in Z264 . Every linear layer is therefore computed on four lanes (the value and three tags), each lane uses exact Mersenne reduction instead of free 64-bit wraparound (Section VI-E), and every authenticated element that crosses PCIe or the host-module interface grows by the same factor of four. Only the peer link is spared, because checks exchange value shares and 

t residuals rather than tags (Section V-G). 

(ii) Verified openings. Opened values (masked gate inputs, the Beaver differences d, e of each multiplication [34], and outputs) must be checked before anything depends on them. 11 0 2 4 6GPU (SH) Trusted DFA (SH) Dealer-based (Mal) VIGOR-DFA w/o GaC (Mal) VIGOR-DFA (Mal) 3.83 5.87

> Fig. 5. BERT-Base latency breakdown on LAN (1 GB/s, 0.05 ms round-trip time) by cost source, for two semi-honest configurations of [16] and three malicious ones (dealer-based malicious FSS, VIGOR-DFA without GaC, VIGOR-DFA); all splits come from our model (Appendix F). The horizontal axis is broken between 7.5 and 15 s so that the 18.8 s semi-honest GPU bar and the 43.9 s dealer-based bar fit.

A sound check derives its challenge only after the checked bytes are fixed (Theorem 6), so at least one extra exchange follows every checked batch, and a dependent nonlinear gate may not consume an unchecked opening. Batching amortizes challenge derivation and metadata but not this round, which lies on the critical path of every layer: a protocol with R online rounds approaches 2R.

(iii) Correct preprocessing. Triples, masks and FSS keys must be correct , not merely authenticated: a valid MAC on a triple with c̸ = ab does not make it correct. The options of Section II-E either ship more offline material from a dealer or replace the dealer by heavy interactive preprocessing, and neither removes offline data movement. Without a dealer, FSS keys require a secure two-party evaluation of DPF key generation, with one round per tree level [30] before any malicious-security overhead. 

(iv) Authenticated FSS outputs. A DPF output share is a plain additive share. To re-enter authenticated arithmetic it needs t tag shares, and the straightforward way to obtain them is a second DPF per MAC lane j with the same target and payload ∆j β (a tag tree ). This multiplies DPF keys, key bytes and PRG work by 1 + t, i.e., by four for t = 3 .

(v) Key validity is not evaluation correctness. Verifiable FSS [33] lets evaluators check that their keys are well formed, which protects against a malicious key generator. It does not constrain an evaluator that computes on valid keys and then adds an error to its output share, nor the arithmetic that consumes that share. 

Observation 1: distribution still dominates dealer-based malicious FSS. Fig. 5 splits the BERT-Base LAN latency by cost source. Dealer-based malicious FSS, modeled after the protocol family of Shark [23] (Section VIII-A), must move 37.6 GB of authenticated keys and preprocessing per query, 2.2 × the 17.1 GB of the semi-honest GPU baseline [16]. At 1 GB/s, this distribution alone takes 37.6 s of 43.9 s (86%), and moving the material from host memory into the GPU adds another 1.70 s. Without an authenticated-key blow-up (ψkey = 1 , Appendix F), it still takes 22.4 s, 5.8 × that of VIGOR-DFA.

Observation 2: without distribution, DPF is no longer the bottleneck. Removing distribution, as trusted DFA did for the semi-honest case, exposes a different profile. In VIGOR-DFA, four-lane authenticated GEMM takes 2.60 s of 3.83 s (68%), 5.0 × our 0.52 s semi-honest GEMM; online commu-nication takes 0.615 s (16%); and all nonlinear gates together take only 0.131 s (3.4%). 

Observation 3: on a WAN, verification rounds dominate. 

With a 70 ms round-trip time (RTT) the extra check round of source (ii) outweighs all computation: removing CheckOpen ,which is insecure and serves only as a lower bound, would reduce the BERT-Base WAN latency from 89.7 s to 50.5 s (Table XIII). 

B. Compute Pattern of Exact DPF 

Malicious-security proofs reason about concrete states, so we fix the DPF computation exactly. Algorithms 1 and 2 give the tree-based DPF [8], [12] in the form that VIGOR-DFA executes; the same algorithms serve Profile U, where keys are exported to an external malicious backend (Section III-E). The expansion function G : {0, 1}128 → ({0, 1}128 × { 0, 1})2 is fixed and AES-based as in DFA, H : {0, 1}128 → Fp is the fixed output conversion, the target α and the query x have n

bits read most-significant first, and the payload is β ∈ Fp.

High-level idea. Fig. 6(a) shows Gen walking two states 

(s0, t 0) and (s1, t 1) down the target path. Since each state applies a correction only when its own old control bit is set, exactly one of the two is corrected at each level; the corrections make the off-path states of both parties identical, so their signed outputs cancel, while CW out programs β at the target leaf (Theorem 2). Correctness needs neither uniform targets nor computational hardness; uniform masks belong to the application protocol (Section V-K).

, b ). Second, a key contains the initial seed of its party, the correction words and CW out ; the generator’s final states are never substituted for the roots. Third, the payload and the role enter only through CW out and the output sign (−1) b, so both are bound inputs of the ledgered gate descriptor (Section V-D) rather than host-supplied parameters. 

Generator versus evaluator state. Lines 5–6 of Algo-rithm 2 correct both children with the evaluator’s own old control bit t. Along the target path this bit coincides with the generator’s tb, which invites reusing the generator’s con-trol register during generate-and-consume. The substitution is wrong as soon as the query leaves the path (level 2 in Fig. 6(b)): there the evaluator states of the two parties are identical and must apply identical corrections, whereas the generator bits t0 and t1 still differ, so one party would apply a correction that the other omits, and the off-path outputs would no longer cancel. Each module therefore keeps a separate evaluator state next to its two generator states (Algorithm 3). 

C. Work Accounting 

Table II compares one single-path DPF gate at six design points. With 128-bit seeds, one party’s key holds its root seed, 

n correction words of 128+2 bits and CW out as one canonical 64-bit word (the root control bit is the public role), so |k| =

⌈(128 + 130 n + 64) /8⌉ bytes, or 1,016 B for n = 61 .

Design-point comparison. Trusted DFA trades 1.5× the dealer’s PRG work for zero key bytes: each module expands 

2n generator states and n evaluator states for its own party, so the two modules perform 6n expansions where a dealer and two evaluators perform 4n. Tag trees multiply PRG work, key bytes and key I/O by 1 + t, and verified keys authenticate neither the output nor its evaluation. VIGOR-DFA keeps DFA’s 6n expansions and zero key bytes and adds only three field multiplications and four pads per output and party; it cannot avoid the 32 B authenticated tuple and the check round. 

Effect of the epilogue. Because the epilogue (Fig. 6(c)) leaves the per-gate PRG expansion count unchanged, the single-path throughput of the DPF engine drops only from 113 to 106 M gates/s at n = 61 relative to semi-honest trusted DFA, whereas per-lane tag trees fall to 26.7 M gates/s; the gap to per-lane tag trees stays at 3.8–4.0 × for n between 8 and 61 (Fig. 15). The drop comes mainly from the pipeline efficiency 13

(0.85 vs. 0.90; Appendix F); the two extra pad blocks ( 6n+3 

vs. 6n+1 AES blocks per gate) cost only 0.5%. The vector-payload tree nearly matches the epilogue’s PRG count, but it widens every output correction by t field elements, covers only results linear in DPF evaluations, and exports pseudorandom tag shares whose hiding rests on DPF key privacy. The epilogue instead makes every exported tuple exactly uniform for its holder (Theorem 5) and applies unchanged to LUT, interval, direct and PIR results. 

Remaining costs we accept. VIGOR-DFA does not remove duplicated generation: 6n instead of 4n expansions is the price of eliminating 2|k| network bytes and |k| bytes of key I/O per gate, a favorable trade because PRG expansion is the cheapest operation in the pipeline while bytes cross its slowest links. Two further costs remain. The conservative O(n2) DCF of Algorithm 8 is the only DCF our proofs cover; its interval gate costs 22,692 AES blocks at n = 61 , against a 976 for an optimized DCF, so the compiler chooses among direct, LUT and interval gates by its cost rule (Appendix F), subject to an FSS-portability constraint (Sections V-J and VIII-B). A host-module link of B bytes/s bounds the authenticated output stream at B/ 32 elements/s, which motivates the L2-adjacent placement (Section VI-A). 

Implications. For sources (iii) and (iv) the semi-honest lesson still holds: distribution is the largest avoidable cost (Observation 1), so DFA’s local generation and generate-and-consume must stay, and FSS outputs must be authenticated without multiplying DPF work. This leaves sources (i) and (ii) as the dominant costs (Observations 2 and 3). The analysis yields five design rules that structure the rest of the paper: (1) authenticate FSS outputs by fresh resharing inside the module, never by extra trees and never by authenticating host values (S2; Section V-F); (2) keep generation local and streamed, with separate generator and evaluator states (S7; Section V-E); (3) make the unavoidable check round sound and batchable by freezing bytes before challenges are drawn (S1, S3, S8), and release no output before VERIFIED (S6; Sections V-D and V-G); (4) bind every randomness draw to one session, gate, role and use, so that local generation does not open a replay path (S4, S5; Section V-D); and (5) leave four-lane exact linear algebra on the GPU while the module generates correct triples (S7) and verifies openings (Section VI-A). V. VIGOR-DFA P ROTOCOL DESIGN 

This section specifies the protocol run by the two parties of Profile T: the flow (Section V-A), the representation, joint randomness and lifecycle state (Sections V-B to V-D), and the mechanisms that move values through the integrity chain (Sections V-E to V-H). Nonlinear gates, refactoring, GPU verification and PIR follow in Sections V-I to V-M; formal statements are collected in Section VII. 

A. Overview 

High-level idea. In trusted DFA the protected module replaces the dealer: it samples FSS keys locally and con-sumes each correction word as soon as it produces it [16]. VIGOR-DFA turns the module from a dealer into a dealer that also verifies. Every wire carries an authenticated sharing with t information-theoretic MAC lanes. The GPU performs all linear algebra on these sharings but only ever produces 

candidate shares. Each module generates every correlation of its party, authenticates every FSS output it computes, checks every opening on frozen bytes and releases nothing before verification. 

Per-gate lifecycle. Every gate of the public circuit C fixed by the manifest μ passes through the same module commands (Table IV). (1) With RESERVE_PREP , Tb reserves the gate’s randomness labels in its one-time ledger and issues party-local authenticated preprocessing (masks, triples) to host b.(2) Host b’s GPU computes candidate authenticated shares with local linear operations. (3) With FREEZE_BATCH and 

CHECK_OPEN , the two modules freeze the submitted bytes and verify the masked openings the gate needs. (4) For a nonlinear gate, EVAL_FSS consumes the verified opening, runs generate-and-consume inside Tb and returns a freshly authenticated share. (5) FINAL_OUTPUT hands a masked output and a bound certificate to an authorized recipient. A failure in any step triggers ABORT , which poisons the epoch. Each mechanism below enforces one design rule of Section IV-C; Table III summarizes the notation. 14

B. Authenticated Representation 

Field and MAC lanes. All protocol arithmetic is over Fp

with the Mersenne prime p = 2 61 − 1. The modules hold 

t = 3 independent global MAC keys ∆1, ∆2, ∆3$

←− Fp, each additively split as ∆j = ∆ 0,j + ∆ 1,j ; host b holds only ∆b,j ,which it needs to add public values to its tags. Party b holds an authenticated value [x] as 

[x]b = ( xb, γ b, 1, γ b, 2, γ b, 3),x0 + x1 = x, γ0,j + γ1,j = ∆ j x (j ∈ [t]) ,

which is the SPDZ representation [24], [35] with three inde-pendent lanes. A canonical wire occupies four 64-bit words, i.e., 32 B per party before metadata. Every element is encoded as an unsigned 64-bit integer smaller than p; the module parser rejects noncanonical encodings instead of reducing arbitrary integers modulo p, which keeps frozen bytes and checked field elements in one-to-one correspondence. 

Three lanes for the 61-bit field. The Mersenne modulus lets the GPU compute exact products of canonical elements with native 64-bit multiplies and two folding steps (Sec-tion VI-E), so all 1 + t lanes of authenticated linear algebra stay on standard integer datapaths. A single 61-bit tag is too weak: for Q ≤ 232 checked batches, t = 3 is the smallest lane count whose statistical term Q(2 /p )t falls below 2−128 

(Section VII-E, Table V), and every lane is paid for in GPU work (Section VIII-H). 

Field selection versus Z264 . The soundness proof divides by a nonzero error coordinate and therefore needs a field (Sec-tion VII-E, Appendix C). The encoding is thus not bit-identical to the Z264 arithmetic of FSS-compiled models [10], [11]. VIGOR-DFA fixes the numeric contract in the manifest (fixed-point scale, signed encoding, exact toward-zero truncation of Section V-I, magnitudes below p/ 2); a deployment that needs ring semantics must use a SPDZ2k-type backend [27] with its own proof. 

C. Session Setup and Joint Randomness 

Session setup. Each module authenticates its peer’s identity, code and configuration measurement, role and freshness con-tribution. An authenticated key establishment then binds the ordered pair of identities, the roles 0 and 1, a fresh session identifier, the epoch, the manifest hash Hash (μ), the field and tag parameters and the protocol version, and yields the session key K known only to T0 and T1. Any failure aborts. We treat this step as an explicit setup assumption with error 

ϵsetup (Theorem 1) and propose no new attestation protocol; attestation certifies what runs inside a module, not what the GPU computes (Section II-D). 

Labels and joint draws. All module randomness is derived as PRF K (ℓ), or with an equivalent key derivation function (KDF) [70], where ℓ is an unambiguous, length-prefixed en-coding of (session , epoch , gate , purpose , logical _draw , block ).A logical joint draw uses the same label in both modules and thus yields the same value in T0 and T1 without com-munication; this is how both modules obtain identical DPF roots, correction words, masks, triples, pads and challenges. Distinct roots, pads, triple components, challenge lanes and rejection-sampling retries receive distinct labels. Two rules are easy to get wrong. The local role must not be appended to a joint draw, since T0 and T1 would then generate two unrelated DPF instances whose outputs no longer add up; and the logical root-0 and root-1 draws of one DPF must be different draws. Since labels name session, epoch and gate, the ledger detects replayed or misrouted material (S4, S5). A uniform element of 

Fp is obtained by rejection sampling: take 61 pseudorandom bits and reject the single value p, which occurs with probability 

2−61 ; a bounded implementation aborts when its retry budget is exhausted instead of introducing a silent bias. 

Authenticated correlations. To share a protected value v,the joint sampler runs AuthShare : it samples v0$

←− Fp and sets v1 = v − v0, independently samples γ0,j 

> $

←− Fp and sets 

γ1,j = ∆ j v − γ0,j for j ∈ [t], and gives party b only its tuple 

[v]b. A multiplication triple samples a, b $

←− Fp, computes c =

ab correctly inside the protected boundary and authenticates all three values independently (S7); a matrix triple satisfies the actual equality C = AB for its declared dimensions. Because both modules derive the same correlations from joint labels, preprocessing crosses no network link. 15 NEW RESERVED PREPARED FROZEN VERIFIED CONSUMED RELEASED

> σ0,j +σ1,j = 0 ∀j

> Fig. 7. Per-gate lifecycle enforced by the one-time ledger of Tb. Solid arrows are the only permitted transitions, each triggered by the named module command (Table IV). An accepted CHECK_OPEN only sets VERIFIED ;
> EVAL_FSS consumes the opening of an FSS or direct gate, and an output gate, which runs no EVAL_FSS , is moved by FINAL_OUTPUT through
> CONSUMED to RELEASED . Any other transition or any failed check moves the gate to ABORTED , poisons the epoch and keeps the release guard closed.

D. One-Time Ledger and Release Guard 

Ledger states. Each module keeps an authoritative pro-tected ledger that binds every gate to its operation, predecessor and output wires, numeric type, table or model hash, public di-mensions and allowed command sequence. A gate transaction moves only along NEW → RESERVED → PREPARED →

FROZEN → VERIFIED → CONSUMED → RELEASED 

(Fig. 7). Reservation burns the gate’s randomness identifiers 

before any preprocessing share is issued, so no label is ever issued twice. PREPARED lets the module issue the authorized party-local preprocessing exactly once; it is not permission to release an unchecked result. CONSUMED records the one-time use of verified openings and preprocessing, and 

RELEASED records that the result left the module under a certificate. Any invalid transition or failed check moves the gate to ABORTED and poisons the whole epoch: outstanding output responses are gated, no new challenge is derived, and aborted reservations never return to a free list. 

Freeze semantics. Before any checking challenge is drawn, 

Tb copies the exact submitted value and tag bytes into pro-tected storage, or spills them into AEAD-protected immutable pages [71] bound to sequence numbers, exact lengths, gate identifiers and a protected root. It hashes the complete de-scriptor and these bytes into a frozen root hb, and the two modules agree on the ordered pair (h0, h 1). A host’s claim that “this buffer has hash h” is not a freeze, and neither is DMA completion. After challenge generation, Tb rereads only authenticated frozen pages and never verifies one buffer but consumes a later, host-modified one. This closes the time-of-check-to-time-of-use window (S3) that exists whenever checked data live in host or GPU memory. 

Release certificates and the guard. A release certificate binds the session, epoch, gate, recipients, output encoding or hash and terminal status. A missing peer acknowledgement can cause an abort, but it never authorizes output under a different transcript. The release guard is a small state machine in front of the module’s output path (Section VI-B); its enabling inputs originate from the protected checkers and the ledger, never from host-writable registers, so a host cannot open it by writing a status bit (S6). Resetting the guard is not a secure session reset. 

Reboot and rollback. After a reboot, either a separately justified trusted monotonic service preserves freshness [72], [73], or the old master key is irrecoverably erased and a freshly attested session is required. A host-controlled counter file is not anti-rollback protection, because the host can restore an old copy and replay old masks (S4). 

E. Malicious Generate-and-Consume 

From semi-honest to malicious GaC. Algorithm 3 inter-leaves Algorithms 1 and 2 with the three separate states of Section IV-B: two generator states on the target path, started from t0 = 0 and t1 = 1 , and an evaluator state on the query path, started from its own root seed and the fixed control bit 

b, never from a later generator state. Each correction word is computed from the expanded children of the two generator seeds and the target bit αi, applied to the generator states under their old bits tc and to both evaluator children under the evaluator’s old bit ˜t, and erased only after that use; t · s

denotes a conditional XOR. The hardware keeps the two state sets in distinct contexts (Section VI-C), and the per-level work, which still duplicates generation in both modules, is accounted in Table II. 

Inputs and outputs. The query x is read from a verified masked opening of the same gate, never from unchecked host data: for instance the opened c of an interval gate, whose prefix DPFs are queried at c (Algorithms 6 and 8), whereas in a LUT gate the opened u only permutes the table index while EvalAll 

visits every a (Algorithm 5). The target α and payload β come from the gate’s protected preprocessing record, so they are joint draws or joint functions of joint draws (Section V-K). By Theorems 2 and 3, the raw outputs satisfy ˜y0 + ˜ y1 =

β ·1[x = α], but ˜yb never leaves the module: the epilogue lines of Algorithm 3 replace it with a freshly authenticated tuple. Since neither keys nor raw shares are exported, Theorem 1 needs no DPF key-privacy term. Gates that combine several DPF evaluations (interval prefixes, LUT and PIR scans) run only the (Gen) and (Eval) lines per constituent DPF and apply the ledger lines and one epilogue to their combined raw share, for PIR only its value pad (Appendix B). For full-domain uses such as LUT gates and PIR, the evaluator lines become the depth-first EvalAll traversal of Algorithm 2; each CW (i) then stays in protected registers until its last evaluator use, which costs O(n) words and is covered by the same node-by-node coupling.

The pads cancel in y0 + y1 and in γ0,j + γ1,j = ∆ j (y0 +

y1), so the result is an authenticated sharing of ˜y0 + ˜ y1, and each party’s tuple is uniform on F1+ tp whatever the gate value and the global keys (Theorem 5). Fresh pads make this hold jointly across outputs; correlations created by later arithmetic are handled by the circuit simulation of Theorem 1. 

Tag tree replacement. Instead of t further DPF trees with payloads ∆j β, the epilogue exploits the linearity of the MAC. Because Tb knows the global ∆j , the tag of the reconstructed value is the sum of the per-party products ∆j yb; the pad κj

hides ∆j yb from host b, which knows yb and could otherwise divide it out. The pad ζ rerandomizes the raw DPF share, so no host ever sees ˜yb. The cost is one raw DPF computation, three field multiplications and four fresh pads per party and output, and the output is a 32 B tuple. 

API restriction. The epilogue accepts only results com-puted inside the module, namely the DPF, DCF, LUT, interval and direct gates of Sections V-I and V-J and the PIR reductions of Section V-M, or results from an explicitly verified backend such as the frozen-operand Freivalds check of Section V-L. There is no authenticate(host_value) entry point. With one, the module would become a MAC oracle: a ma-licious host could obtain valid tags on any value it wishes to open, and CheckOpen would accept the forgery with probability one (S2). G. Freeze-Before-Challenge CheckOpen 

Role. Openings are the only places where a value produced by the untrusted GPU becomes public or feeds a protected 17 

gate: the masked differences of every multiplication, the masked inputs of every FSS gate and the masked outputs. 

CheckOpen (Algorithm 4, Fig. 8) is a SPDZ-style batched MAC check [24], [35] rebuilt for a verifier that reads its inputs from untrusted memory (S1, S3). All peer messages use the protected channel, and only an accepted batch releases z with bound certificates. 

Independent challenge lanes. Let e be the value-error vector of a batch and dj its tag-error vector in lane j, i.e., the differences between the submitted and the prescribed tuples. The freeze fixes (e, dj ) before any ρj,i exists, so a host cannot place its errors in the kernel of a challenge (Appendix C). Each lane has its own challenge vector ρj . The complete residual of lane j is ⟨ρj , dj − ∆j e⟩, which for e̸ = 0

vanishes with probability below 2/p ; independence across lanes multiplies these bounds to (2 /p )t (Theorem 6). A shared vector collapses the product to one lane, and tag-only errors escape with probability 1/p without changing an opened value (Section VII-E, Appendix C). 

Key hiding and failure handling. The bound relies on the hidden-key invariant of Lemma 1: until the first accepted wrong opening, the honest key shares stay uniform given the corrupt host’s view. CheckOpen preserves it in three ways. Candidates, challenges and residuals never leave the modules. The zero tests of all lanes are aggregated into one generic failure status that exposes no residual, challenge, key or data-dependent diagnostic. A failure poisons the epoch and forbids retries with the same material, which rules out an adaptive key-learning sequence within an epoch. 

Traffic and batching. Per batch of m elements, the modules exchange two vectors of m field values and two vectors of t

residuals plus authenticated metadata. Tags never cross the peer link, but every local tag must be read by its module, so the host-to-module transfer of 32 B per element can remain the bottleneck (Section VI-G). Because the value shares are frozen before they are sent, root agreement and value exchange can share one message, so each batch incurs two peer round trips per batch. Only independent openings are batched: a dependent nonlinear gate never consumes an unchecked masked opening, and a checkpoint precedes every input-dependent nonlinear output and every final release, since blindly delayed checking is not justified. Larger batches amortize metadata and round trips but increase staging memory and first-result latency (costs in Section VIII-C). 

H. Inputs, Arithmetic, and Outputs 

Fig. 9 specifies the four protocols that carry values between input owners, GPUs, modules and recipients; three points of rationale are not visible in the box. 

Inputs. The difference ε is uniform for a uniform r, so the hosts learn nothing about x. Both modules bind one ε, so a corrupt owner cannot feed the two parties different inputs, and its input is extracted as x∗ = r+ε (S9, Definition 2). Canonical encoding alone does not place x∗ in the declared interface; since the modules generated r, they compute x∗ and abort unless x∗ ∈ In ν . The validity predicates V of μ are evaluated inside the modules at public boundaries that the schedule of 

μ places after every input gate and before the first output opening; their accept or abort outcome is the status component of LC (Definition 1), and the simulator emulates the same abort (Theorem 1). For committed inputs such as preapproved model weights, a host-declared hash does not suffice; the loader hashes exactly the bytes it shares. The modules thus see every input, which Profile T permits (Section III-A). 

Arithmetic. Multiplication follows Beaver [34] with au-thenticated triples. An authenticated matrix product costs the GPU 2(1 + t) plain products plus the public DE , which is why authenticated linear algebra, rather than DPF evaluation, dominates the malicious cost (Section IV-A). 

Outputs. The recipient accepts υ only with matching cer-tificates from both modules, so integrity toward a client comes from these certificates, never from MAC tags. 

I. Authenticated Nonlinear Gates 

Nonlinear layers of FSS-compiled models reduce to lookups in small public tables and piecewise functions selected by interval tests [9]–[11]. In the semi-honest DFA design, both parties evaluate a DPF or DCF key on a masked input and pass the raw output shares onward [16]. A malicious host can shift the masked input before it selects the DPF path, and it can modify the raw outputs, which carry no MAC. VIGOR-DFA closes both gaps with one template (Fig. 10): (1) mask the authenticated input with joint protected randomness (Sec-tion V-C); (2) open the masked value with CheckOpen 

(Algorithm 4), so the value that selects the path is verified and frozen; (3) evaluate the FSS gate inside Tb with the (Gen) and (Eval) steps of generate-and-consume (Algorithm 3); and (4) authenticate and reshare the gate’s combined raw share with one epilogue (Section V-F). The host sees only a uniform masked opening and fresh authenticated tuples, and no host-supplied value is ever authenticated, which defeats scenario S2 (Table I). 

Small XOR-masked LUT. Algorithm 5 evaluates an ap-proved public table T with 2w field entries on a w-bit input given as authenticated, certified-Boolean bits. The sampler draws a fresh uniform r ∈ { 0, 1}w once and uses it twice: for the authenticated bits [ri] and as the target of the joint DPF for 

(r, 1) . The parties open u = x ⊕ r in one CheckOpen batch, both modules reject a non-Boolean or mismatching vector, and 

Tb scans the full domain in a declared fixed order,

In EvalAll mode the w correction words stay in protected registers until their level is fully expanded, so no key is materialized outside Tb. Besides the w XOR products (one batched multiplication round when the bits exist; otherwise bit decomposition is charged to the gate), the gate needs one batched opening, 2w −1 internal tree expansions and a 2w-term field reduction: small tables are practical, full-domain tables over 64-bit inputs are not (Table VII). 18

Invariants shared by all four protocols  

> •

Every mask and triple is a one-time joint draw bound to session, epoch, gate and purpose, and is issued only from PREPARED (Fig. 7).  

> •

Every opening is a CheckOpen call on frozen bytes (Algorithm 4); no opened value feeds a protected gate or a release before it is VERIFIED . 

> •

Outputs of host b’s GPU are candidate shares; no module ever authen-ticates them. Fig. 9. Authenticated input, linear, multiplication and output protocols of VIGOR-DFA, written for party Pb. Steps labeled T0, T 1 or Tb run inside the protected modules; steps labeled host b run on the untrusted GPU and yield only candidate shares until the next CheckOpen . With honest messages the protocols realize their operations exactly; otherwise they select an allowed input, preserve the computation or abort, except with the probability bounded in Theorem 6 (Theorem 7). 

Shifted intervals. For a proper nonempty public interval 

[a, b ) ⊊ [0 , p ) (here a, b are endpoints, not party indices), Algorithm 6 opens c = x + r mod p for a fresh field mask r,and Tb derives from the same rL = ( a + r) mod p, H = ( b + r) mod p, ω = 1[L > H ].

With conservative DCFs for thresholds L and H (Algorithm 8; orientation 1[c < θ ], payload 1, independent coins) and a sharing of the wrap bit ω, the raw share ˜sb = ωb +DCF H (c)b −

DCF L(c)b goes to the epilogue. Translation by r maps [a, b )

onto the circular half-open interval from L to H (L̸ = H

since 0 < b − a < p ): the indicator is 1[c < H ] − 1[c < L ]

if L < H and 1 + 1[c < H ] − 1[c < L ] if the interval wraps, so Theorem 4 gives ˜s0 + ˜ s1 = 1[a ≤ x < b ] (Theorem 11). Empty and full intervals are public constants. A partition may share one mask and one opening if every endpoint, wrap bit and auxiliary share is derived jointly from that mask, while distinct DCFs keep independent coins. The conservative DCF evaluates n(n + 1) /2 path levels per threshold; its cost, and that of a projected O(n) DCF [9], [15] that Theorem 4 does not cover, are in Table VII. 

Piecewise polynomials. Activation and normalization func-tions are approximated by low-degree polynomials on a public partition [10], [74], [75]. The parties select coefficients [¯ aj ] = 

P 

> i

ai,j [si] locally ( ΠLin , Fig. 9) and apply Horner’s rule with 

d authenticated products, independently of the number m of pieces. Exactly one indicator of a disjoint exhaustive partition is one, so Theorem 7 yields the active polynomial; rescaling is inserted where the manifest specifies it (Algorithm 10). Approximation error of the approved coefficients is distinct from the cryptographic correctness of Theorems 10 and 11, which concerns the declared finite arithmetic. 

Exact comparison, bit decomposition and truncation. 

The LUT input, exact threshold comparisons and toward-zero signed truncation need canonical bits, which Algorithms 9 and 10 (Appendix D) derive with authenticated arithmetic alone (Theorems 8 and 9) [76]–[78]. 

Exact circuits as fallback. Their carry chains cannot be shortened by batching: Algorithm 9 performs 2n = 122 

dependent multiplications for n = 61 , hence 2n + 1 = 123 se-quential verified openings, and exact signed truncation chains two decompositions and a comparator into 307 authenticated multiplications and 309 sequential rounds, 30.9 ms on LAN (Table VII). At two round trips per verified opening (Sec-tion V-G), the 123-round chain alone would take about 17.2 s on WAN. inside its boundary, evaluate an approved deterministic gate
