This is secure (Theorem 7) in the same model and with the same output simulation as the FSS gates, since the host again receives a fresh, independently uni-form value and tag tuple (Theorem 5), provided the module’s observable trace while computing g meets the same simulata-20 

bility assumption ( ϵtrace in Theorem 1). Concretely, fetching 

T [x] from host memory or running in x-dependent time would leak x, so approved direct gates run from protected memory in data-independent time. A direct gate needs one verified opening, two AES blocks and five field multiplications; our model shows 1,800 M gates/s, above every FSS realization in Table VII. 

Cost-aware selection. For each nonlinear gate shape, the compiler outputs a cost tuple (latency, bytes, protected mem-ory, field operations, PRG calls) for the direct gate, a small XOR LUT, an interval or polynomial FSS gate, and a sep-arately proven malicious backend. It rejects candidates that violate the gate’s semantic and security contract (the exact finite function, encoding and rescaling fixed by μ under the profile in force) and selects the least modeled end-to-end cost among the rest, accounting for dependency depth and transport; only independent checks are batched (Section V-G). One constraint applies: gate shapes that the manifest marks as FSS-portable keep an FSS realization, so that the same compiled circuit runs under Profile U. The compiler-selected default is therefore not the unconstrained minimum, and direct-only compilation is marginally faster (3.82 s versus 3.83 s for BERT-Base on LAN). 

FSS in the datapath. If the direct gate is at least as cheap under Profile T, why keep DPF generation and evalua-tion in the module? (i) Compatibility. Sigma- and Orca-style toolchains [10], [11] compile nonlinear layers into masked-input FSS gates, and Profile U (Section III-E) runs the circuit through an established malicious backend whose DPF keys the accelerator evaluates as party-local data; there the direct candidate is invalid because the hardware is not trusted with x.One DPF datapath serves both profiles and the untrusted-mode DFA workloads. (ii) Full-domain scans. PIR and small-LUT gates are data-independent, fixed-order scans that the pipelined DPF units execute at one iteration per cycle (Section VI-B), so both profiles reuse one engine; this is a reuse argument, not evidence that the DPF form is the fastest option under Profile T

(Section V-M). (iii) Short-lived sensitive state. FSS keeps long-running evaluation state pseudorandom (seeds and correction words), and index- or input-dependent state short-lived. We treat (iii) only as defense in depth against physical and microarchitectural threats that Profile T excludes; no theorem depends on it, and since each module holds c and r, FSS gains no formal confidentiality over the direct gate (Section III-A). The evaluation reports direct-only, FSS-only and compiler-selected default configurations side by side (Section VIII-H). 

K. Distribution-Preserving Refactoring 

DFA decouples DPF generation from protocol preprocessing by sampling the target directly as a uniform value and recover-ing the mask r through g−1 when the protocol’s transformation 

g is a bijection [16]. VIGOR-DFA draws every target from the joint sampler in the same way, with two refinements of the semi-honest argument. Since DPF key privacy holds for arbitrary targets (Section II-A), uniformity is required by the application, which publishes c = x + r or u = x ⊕ r; a refactoring must therefore preserve the joint distribution of the preprocessing, not the uniformity of each target. 

Joint, not marginal, distributions. A gate’s preprocessing contains correlated objects: the mask, its bits, DPF or DCF targets, wrap bits and auxiliary shares. If a gate needs targets 

r and r+1 , resampling each uniformly keeps the marginals but destroys their unit difference; drawing L and H of Algorithm 6 independently likewise yields a wrong indicator. The refactor-ing is valid exactly under the conditions of Lemma 2: the map is a bijection for the fixed context and all auxiliary material is regenerated from the recovered mask by the unchanged sampler. For example, Algorithm 6 may draw L uniformly, set r = L − a and derive H and ω from that r, but may not draw L and H independently. 

Noninvertible maps and truncation. For unsigned trun-cation, the sampler R′ = 2 hA + U of Lemma 3 makes the DFA rule (shift left, fill the low bits uniformly [16]) exact; signed shifts, nonuniform masks and other moduli need their own samplers. 

L. Verified GPU Matrix Products 

Authenticated linear layers need no extra verification: a GPU deviation that changes a value is caught at the next 

CheckOpen , before the result enters a protected gate or a release, except with the probability bounded in Theorem 6. A module that must consume a GPU-computed matrix before any opening, however, must not accept it on the host’s word, which would reintroduce the forgery oracle of scenario S2. For this case Algorithm 11 (Appendix D) is an optional frozen-operand Freivalds check [60], in the spirit of Slalom [58] but bound to the ledger. 

Permitted uses and check. The check applies only when exposing the operands to that GPU is already allowed, as in public-by-party-local matrix products; it never permits expos-ing global secret triple masks, which are generated inside the TCB (Section V-H). The module binds the exact operand bytes 

A ∈ Fm×kp and B ∈ Fk×np to their authoritative records and freezes the claimed C (Section V-D); only then does it draw s

independent secret uniform v ∈ Fnp , test Cv ?

= A(Bv) inside the protected verifier, and consume only the verified frozen bytes of C (VERIFY_MATRIX in Table IV). If the GPU also transforms tag arrays, their tag equations are verified too. 

Soundness and cost. Theorem 12 bounds acceptance of a wrong product by p−s, and QF calls add QF /p s to Theorem 1. The check is disabled in all evaluated configurations; enabling it adds implementation obligations (Table VI) and this error term. 

M. Versioned-Snapshot PIR 

In DPF-based two-server PIR (Eq. (2)), a malicious server can return a wrong share, which the client silently reconstructs into a wrong record, or answer from a stale database. Algo-rithm 7 and Fig. 11 show the VIGOR-DFA countermeasures. 

Protocol and snapshot binding. Both role-bound modules receive the same canonical query from the client, compare the binding over their protected channel, pad the snapshot to 

2n records while authorizing only α < N , and run a joint DPF for (α, 1) by generate-and-consume. Fig. 11. Versioned-snapshot PIR (Algorithm 7). Both modules bind the same query, scan the same snapshot bytes in a public order, hash exactly the bytes they consume, and quarantine the DPF-weighted reduction until the hash matches the authorized commitment cm v . A host that swaps a record after hashing or serves an old version causes an abort, never a wrong accepted record (scenario S8). Both modules learn α, so query privacy relies on module trust. 

weights to every record coordinate, and hashes exactly the bytes it consumes. The commitment cm v covers the database identifier, version, shape, record encoding and ordered data bytes, and the result stays quarantined behind the release guard (Section V-D) until the hash matches it; a host that swaps a record after hashing or serves an old version therefore causes an abort (scenario S8 in Table I). A Merkle realization [79] must authenticate every consumed leaf and its position against the same root. Offloading the reduction to the GPU would need Algorithm 11 or a separately proven authenticated reduction, never a signature on the GPU’s claim, and would export the expanded share vector, so VIGOR-DFA reduces inside the module. Each module releases only a freshly reshared value share (the value half of the epilogue) with a bound certificate, and the client reconstructs D[α] = y0 + y1.

Guarantees and regimes. In regime (R1) of Fabort PIR 

(Fig. 4), with one malicious server host and an honest client, the client obtains the committed record or an abort, and the host learns nothing about α beyond LPIR (Definition 1). In regime (R2), a malicious client alone learns only the requested record and the public descriptor. We make no combined claim (Theorem 13). Both modules learn α, so query privacy relies on module trust: unlike untrusted two-server DPF-PIR such as GPU-DPF [14] or DFA’s untrusted mode [16], which hides 

α from each server without trusted hardware, VIGOR-DFA offers no query privacy against a compromised module. 

Scope and design choice. Authenticated PIR [47] provides integrity without trusted hardware at additional server cost and is our malicious-secure GPU baseline (Section VIII-E). Two alternatives bracket our design. Since the modules know 

α anyway, a module could select D[α] with a constant-time

At a PIR efficiency of 0.93 relative to DFA (Appendix F), our model achieves 16,349 versus 17,580 queries/s at N = 2 20 [16] (Table IX). VI. A RCHITECTURE AND SYSTEM INTEGRATION 

This section describes how VIGOR-DFA realizes the pro-tected module Tb of Section V next to the GPU and how the untrusted host drives it. We keep the organization of DFA [16] wherever the malicious setting permits and add only the blocks that the integrity chain requires. Hardware sizes are design parameters (Section VIII-G and Appendix F). That this hardware realizes the ideal resource FT of the proofs is an implementation obligation (Table VI). 22 

A. Design Insights 

Key insight. Malicious security moves the dominant cost from DPF to authenticated linear algebra on the GPU, while protected FSS evaluation, exposed triple generation and 

CheckOpen stay small (Section IV-A and Fig. 5). The module therefore need not become a second GPU: it must hold exactly the state whose exposure or modification would break the integrity chain and inspect every byte that crosses its boundary. The GPU is a fast but untrusted engine for authenticated linear algebra, and the module is dealer, verifier and release authority at once. 

Protected components. A block belongs inside the trusted boundary if it holds material that reveals both parties’ views or authorizes a release. These are exactly the TCB items of Section III-A (keys, sampler and counters, generator state and correction words, table and reduction logic, challenges and residuals, release decisions); everything else stays outside (Fig. 2). 

GPU-resident computation. Linear layers run as the matrix Beaver products of ΠMult over the value lane and the t = 3 

tag lanes (Fig. 9), i.e., two dense products per lane, 2(1 + t) in total, of the same public shape. A GPU result is a candidate share, never a certified value. 

Placement. An authenticated element costs 32 B per party at the module interface (Section V-B), so a link of bandwidth 

B streams at most B/ 32 elements per second (Section IV-C). The L2-adjacent placement of DFA delivers about 290 GB/s to the SMs [16], so we keep it: one DPF unit per L2 slice, a new protected core beside a designated L2 partition, and SM access through the DFA-mapped address range without a new network-on-chip (NoC) port. 

B. Hardware Design 

Fig. 12 shows the module (area and power in Sec-tion VIII-G). The trusted boundary encloses the 16 DPF units of DFA (Section II-D), the vector processing unit (VPU), the new VIGOR core and the on-die links between them. Only command frames with share batches, PIR database records, authenticated output tuples, sealed spill pages and sealed channel frames cross it, none carrying a module secret in the clear. The blocks follow the integrity chain: parser and ledger admit authorized input ; sampler and field unit array produce correlated preprocessing ; the DPF units perform 

correct local evaluation ; the epilogue yields authenticated arithmetic ; freezer, check engine and peer channel enforce 

consistent opening ; and the release guard gates authorized output release .

DPF units and sampler. The AES datapath is unchanged. Each pipeline context carries two generator states, a separate evaluator state, a depth-first search (DFS) stack and the con-version H into Fp, exactly the state of Algorithm 3; correction words are consumed and discarded inside the unit, and raw leaf shares ˜yb go to the field unit array lanes of the same unit, never to SM-readable buffers. The joint PRF sampler implements the labeled derivation and bounded rejection sampling of Section V-C under the session key. data control select / enable trusted boundary port outside Tb (untrusted): L2 slice, NoC and SMs, HBM, NIC to peer T1−b

Field unit array (FUA). The FUA consists of 128 Mersenne-61 multiply-accumulate lanes, each with a 61 × 61 -bit multiplier and the two-fold reduction of Fig. 14; its peak is 116 G multiply-accumulates per second at the 910 MHz 7 nm clock of DFA [16]. It computes epilogues (three products per output), the triple products c = ab and C = AB with their 

AuthShare tags (so matrix masks never reach the GPU), LUT and PIR reductions, interval sums and check residuals. The lanes sit in the DPF units, eight per unit (a design parameter), and the VIGOR core schedules them as one pool. Full-domain LUT and PIR reductions therefore run next to the leaves, and only per-query accumulators (three field elements per PIR query) travel to the core (Section VI-G). 

Check engine and freezer. The check engine executes steps 3 and 4 of Algorithm 4: it derives the t challenge vectors only after both frozen roots are fixed, accumulates the residuals on FUA lanes (two products per lane and element) and eval-uates all lanes over the full batch before emitting one generic status, so neither the status nor its latency reveals which lane or position failed. FREEZE_BATCH copies the exact submitted bytes into a 256 KB freeze SRAM (8,192 tuples of 32 B) and hashes them with their descriptor into the root hb that the modules exchange; larger batches spill to AES-GCM-sealed immutable HBM pages [71], [81] whose authoritative root and page counters stay inside, so after the challenge only 23 1 Context interleaving gen G(s0), G (s1) eval G(˜ s) idle g1 e1

Ledger, release guard and control. A 32 KB ledger SRAM holds the authoritative state of every in-flight gate and the randomness identifiers burnt at reservation (Fig. 7). The release guard opens for a gate only in state VERIFIED , and an epoch abort closes every outstanding response atomically (Section V-D); the release multiplexer and output FIFO of Fig. 12 belong to the release-guard logic. The peer-channel controller keeps independent directional keys, never repeats a nonce and enforces a per-session traffic and forgery budget. The SC300-class control core of DFA [48], [49] runs measured boot, attestation and the key establishment covered by ϵsetup ,and the 16-lane VPU of DFA [50] handles the remaining protocol logic, including the range check x∗ ∈ In ν of ΠInput 

and the validity predicates of μ at the public boundaries that 

μ declares. Host software cannot drive the module’s clock or voltage outside its safe operating range (DVFS isolation, Appendix A-C). 

AES time-sharing. The 64 engines are the module’s only AES resource. A slot arbiter per unit serves PRG expansion (two blocks per expansion, 6n + 3 per GaC output), challenge derivation (1.5 blocks per checked element: three 64-bit words for three 61-bit challenges) and AEAD keystream for sealing and the peer channel (two blocks per 32-byte tuple); one AEAD/GHASH block serves both the freezer and the peer-channel controller. Check and sealing requests take priority because they are short and on the critical path of the next round; PRG contexts fill all other slots. A per-slot purpose tag selects the key context, so the uses never share a key. 

C. Pipeline Scheduling and Memory 

Context interleaving. With an AES and correction feed-back latency of L cycles, one dependent path advances at most one level every L cycles, so one issue per cycle needs at least L independent contexts per unit (Fig. 13, panel 1). For the 20-stage DFA pipeline [16] we therefore provision 20 contexts per unit, one per stage. The three expansions of a malicious GaC level issue back to back, because G(˜ s) does not depend on CW (i); only the correction waits for all three. A context (three 129-bit path states, the correction word in flight, target, query, payload and level) needs fewer than 100 B, so 20 contexts take less than 2 KB per unit, and a single gate with n = 61 completes in 1.71 μs. 

Separate old controls. Each unit keeps separate registers for the old generator bits t0, t 1 and the old evaluator bit ˜t

(Section IV-B), captures all outputs of a transition before overwriting its inputs, and never substitutes generator states for evaluator roots; this is the schedule covered by Theorem 3. RTL tests exercise signed final conversion, zero payloads, all-zero and all-one indices, x = α and divergence at every depth. 

Depth-first EvalAll. LUT and PIR gates evaluate the full domain. Depth-first traversal keeps one pending sibling per level ( O(n) states per traversal) and a complete tree needs 

2n − 1 internal expansions (panel 3). The 512 queries of a PIR batch walk the same public leaf order in lockstep, so each snapshot record is read and hashed once per batch and folded into 512 per-query accumulators. For N = 2 20 , 512 contexts with 20 pending siblings of 17 B occupy about 174 KB, or 11 KB per unit, which fits the traversal buffers of DFA [16]. 

Pads and frozen buffers. The four pads of an output occupy two AES blocks and are drawn in step with the leaf, so no pad buffer beyond the pipeline depth is needed. A checked batch occupies the freeze SRAM until its verdict two peer round trips later, so batches beyond 8,192 elements, such as the 65,536-element batches of Fig. 16, spill to sealed pages. 

D. Command Interface and Host Integration 

Host responsibilities. Host b schedules the public circuit, stores only party-local authenticated arrays (value and each MAC lane as separate coalesced arrays, serialized canonically only at the protected interface), runs the exact field kernels of Section VI-E and sends share batches to Tb. Its scheduling may be adversarial, so the ledger, not the host, decides whether operation, identity, shape and order are admissible, and a reused buffer always carries a fresh transaction identifier. 

Commands and frames. Table IV lists the eight commands with the ledger state each reaches (Fig. 7); since none of them authenticates a host value, the forgery-oracle path of scenario S2 is closed (Table I). A frame carries version, session identifier, epoch, role, opcode, gate identifier, ordered input and output identifiers, field identifier, tag count, dimensions, payload length, sequence number and payload, all length-prefixed with explicit endianness; frames between modules or from a client bind the header as AEAD associated data. The parser copies each frame into protected memory before check-ing it and rejects truncation, trailing bytes, duplicate identifiers, malformed dimensions, noncanonical field elements (values of at least p are rejected, never reduced) and unexpected opcodes. 

Data races and aborts. Verification and consumption use the same immutable parsed bytes. Independent gate batches may overlap, but a dependent nonlinear gate never consumes an unchecked masked opening. Any integrity or lifecycle vio-lation, such as an invalid transition or a failed check, triggers 24

> constexpr uint64_t P=(1ULL << 61) -1; // 2^61 -1// canonical a, b<P; returns canonical a*b mod P__device__ uint64_t mul61(uint64_t a, uint64_t b) {uint64_t lo =a * b; // low 64 bits uint64_t hi =__umul64hi(a, b); // high bits, <2^58 uint64_t x=(lo &P) +((lo >> 61) |(hi << 3)); x=(x &P) +(x >> 61); // x<= 2^61 return x>= P? x -P:x; }
> Fig. 14. Exact Mersenne-61 multiplication used by the GPU field kernels (CUDA). The first fold uses 261 ≡1 (mod p); the second fold and the conditional subtraction return the canonical representative. No floating-point or Tensor-Core operation is involved.

ABORT , which poisons the epoch, prevents new challenges and atomically keeps the release guard closed for every outstanding response (Section V-D). Table X maps each injected fault to the block that stops it. 

E. Exact Field Arithmetic on the GPU 

GPU kernels must compute the finite circuit that the proofs reason about, so floating-point shortcuts are excluded. An authenticated GEMM applies the matrix Beaver combination of ΠMult on each of the four lanes, i.e., two products per lane (D B b and Ab E on the value lane, likewise on each tag lane), 

2(1 + t) = 8 products of the same shape, plus the public product DE shared by all lanes (Fig. 9); Fig. 14 gives the scalar multiplication at the core of every kernel. 

Correctness. For canonical a, b < p = 2 61 −1 the product is below 2122 , so hi < 258 and hi ≪ 3 fits in 61 bits. Writing 

ab = 2 64 hi + lo and using 261 ≡ 1 (mod p) gives ab ≡

(lo mod 2 61 )+ ⌊lo /261 ⌋+2 3 hi (mod p). The last two terms occupy disjoint bit ranges (bits 0 to 2 and 3 to 60), so the bitwise OR equals their sum and the folded value is below 262 .The second fold yields at most 261 = p+1 , and the conditional subtraction returns the canonical representative. The executed CPU check of this formula and the GPU conformance test are reported in Section VIII-F. 

Accumulation and numeric contract. A two-word 128-bit accumulator absorbs at most 64 unreduced products below 

2122 , so kernels fold at least once per 64 inner-dimension terms; random shares lie close to p even for small plaintexts, so plaintext bounds never justify longer accumulation. The manifest μ fixes the scale 2f , signed encoding, toward-zero truncation (Algorithm 10), saturation, approximation coeffi-cients and output decoding. For example, a dot product of length at most 4,096 with operand magnitudes at most 220 

stays within | · | ≤ 252 before bias, which leaves room below 

p/ 2 only if bias and further accumulation are bounded too; the bound is recomputed per layer. FP16, BF16 and TF32 Tensor Cores never serve as exact field operations without a proven limb construction, and the exact finite circuit and a floating-point accuracy model remain separate references. 

GEMM cost. Our model charges an exact Mersenne GEMM 1.25 × the time of a wraparound 64-bit GEMM per lane ( ϕM in Appendix F). This factor is calibrated via GPU benchmarks: the kernel above additionally needs the high product word and a two-word accumulator per term, and the GPU test that would calibrate it has not run. Each +0 .25 in 

ϕM adds 0.52 s to BERT-Base; with ϕM = 2 its LAN latency would be 5.39 s, i.e., 3.8 × instead of 2.7 × that of semi-honest trusted DFA. 

F. Deployment Targets 

An on-die module cannot be added to a commercial A100, so we define three distinct experiments and never merge their timings. All three are reported in Section VIII-A. (i) A100 software runs the exact kernels, authenticated GEMM and host runtime on a real A100 with an emulated module. 

(ii) A ZCU102 prototype (xczu9eg-ffvb1156-2-e [82]) implements the datapath of Fig. 12 at reduced scale (4 DPF units, 16 AES engines, 32 FUA lanes at 250 MHz, Table XII) to validate streaming, freezing and release ordering; one board with an emulated peer shows functionality, not two independent trust boundaries, and an ARM process on it is not isolated from a malicious board administrator. (iii) The on-die study models integration next to the L2 slices with Accel-Sim [51] and synthesis, as in DFA; our headline numbers refer to it and cross-checked against this study (Appendix F). An external module uses staged transfer through host memory as its baseline, because GPUDirect RDMA needs platform and driver support and ordinarily a common PCIe root complex [83]; IOMMU protection is never disabled for a benchmark. In every target, freeze-before-challenge refers to immutable authenticated bytes, never to a GPU buffer that can change concurrently. 

G. Design-Space Comparison 

We compare the L2-adjacent organization of VIGOR-DFA with alternative homes for authentication, verification and randomness generation. 25 

External protected module over PCIe. A discrete FPGA or security card [84] keeps the boundary off the GPU die but limits the interface to B/ 32 elements per second: 50 M for the 1.6 GB/s ZCU102 transport and 0.78 G for 25 GB/s of effective PCIe Gen4 bandwidth, against 9.1 G on the 290 GB/s on-die path of DFA [16]. Because authenticated GEMM dominates, BERT-Base LAN latency rises only to 3.87 s over PCIe and 4.59 s with the ZCU102 link and lanes (Table XIII); the option suits prototyping but adds a second device per party and two device round trips per checked layer (Appendix F). 

Verification on the GPU SMs. In Profile T the SMs belong to the malicious host: a corrupt checking kernel could see the challenges before choosing its errors, or simply report acceptance. Trusting them requires a GPU-wide TEE [21], [22], [85] covering SMs, caches, memory controllers and the driver interface, the large boundary that DFA and VIGOR-DFA avoid. The GPU therefore computes only products that the module verifies afterwards ( CheckOpen or the Freivalds check of Section V-L). 

Tag DPF trees instead of the epilogue. One extra DPF tree per MAC lane (payload ∆j β) multiplies the PRG work of every gate by 1+ t (Table II and Fig. 15); the epilogue achieves the same with three multiplications and four pads (Theorem 5) at 3.8–4.0 × the per-gate throughput of tag trees. 

Software-only malicious backend. SPDZ-family preprocess-ing [25]–[27], [57] needs inter-party communication that grows with the number of authenticated triples, and dealer-based malicious FSS, modeled after the protocol family of Shark [23], ships authenticated keys and triples from an external dealer (Table VIII); both re-create the offline traffic that trusted DFA removed. Such a backend fits Profile U,where the hardware itself is distrusted (Section III-E); under Profile T it pays for a trust assumption that the module already provides. 

Dedicated check engines, a central FUA and per-slice checking. Checking needs only 2.8 ms of module time per BERT-Base query against 131 ms of protected FSS work, so dedicated AES engines for challenges and sealing would idle almost always. DPF units stay distributed because a single attachment point, a memory-channel path of about 180 GB/s, would cap DPF output at about 62% of the 290 GB/s delivered bandwidth [16]. The same limit rules out a central FUA: the raw 8-byte leaf shares of PIR at 16K entries alone would need 137 GB/s (76% of that path), so reductions stay in the units. Checks, by contrast, cost at most 3.5 AES blocks and six field multiplications per element and depend on the ledger, freeze SRAM and peer channel, which must be single points of authority; the VIGOR core therefore controls every check and holds one authoritative root per batch. VII. S ECURITY ANALYSIS 

We now show that VIGOR-DFA realizes the functionalities 

Fabort  

> C,μ

and Fabort PIR of Fig. 4 with leakage LC and LPIR (Def-inition 1) against a malicious host and GPU, as stand-alone security with abort (Definition 2) in the explicit-simulator style [52]–[55]. Each link of the integrity chain, authorized input → correlated preprocessing → correct local evaluation 

→ authenticated arithmetic → consistent opening → autho-rized output release , is discharged by a named statement: input extraction (Theorem 7); in-module preprocessing and joint sampling (Lemmas 2 and 3); exact DPF, DCF, FSS-gate and direct-gate correctness (Theorems 2 to 4 and 7 to 11); the epilogue and the arithmetic identities (Theorems 5 and 7); MAC-check soundness (Lemma 1 and Theorem 6); and gated release (Theorems 7 and 13). Full proofs are in Appendices B to E. 

A. Assumptions and Error Terms 

The theorem is proved in Profile T (Section III); we state module trust as an explicit ideal resource [63]. 

Assumption 1 (Ideal protected module) . Corrupting host b

does not corrupt Tb; both modules are instances of the ideal resource FT (Appendix A-C). The code, keys, parser, field arithmetic, ledger transitions, local memory, protected peer channel and approved I/O paths of each module are correct and confidential. Its externally observable trace (lengths, timing, host-visible memory and DMA activity, power, thermal and clock telemetry) is simulatable by a stipulated trace simulator from the command sequence issued by the host, μ, the public lengths and the accept or abort outcomes, and is independent of the global MAC keys, challenges and residuals given these (all lanes are aggregated in data-independent time before one generic status is returned). Host software cannot drive the module’s clock or voltage outside its safe operating range, or the module detects glitches and aborts (DVFS isolation); software-reachable undervolting [69] is thus in scope (Sec-tion III). Module state is fresh: a trusted monotonic service preserves freshness across reboots [72], [73], or the module irrecoverably erases its master key and requires a freshly attested session. Availability is not assumed. 

Assumption 2 (Setup, randomness, channels and bindings) .

Each term bounds an idealization over the entire admitted execution, not a single primitive call: ϵsetup , simulation of the authenticated setup of Section V-C by an ideal role-bound interface; ϵprf , replacement of PRF/KDF outputs (AES-128, HKDF [70]) under distinct length-prefixed labels by independent coins, identical for the same joint label; ϵchannel ,confidentiality and integrity of the AEAD channels [71], [81] with directional keys and nonrepeating nonces; ϵbind , collisions or forgeries in manifests, snapshot commitments, frozen-page roots, certificates and session freshness [79], [86]; ϵtrace , the stipulated trace simulator. Correctness of protected code, state and arithmetic is thus a premise , not an estimated probability. The protocol con-tributes one statistical term, determined by the field Fp with 

p = 261 − 1, the number t of independent global keys 

∆j = ∆ 0,j + ∆ 1,j (host b knows at most ∆b,j ), and the number Q of CheckOpen batches. Exclusions and client assumptions are those of Section III; the modules may see inputs (Section IX). 26 

B. Main Theorem 

Theorem 1 (Stand-alone static malicious security with abort) .

Under the assumptions of Section VII-A, the protocols of Sec-tion IV-B and Section V (setup, ledger, exact DPF, generate-and-consume with the fresh authentication epilogue, con-servative DCF, AuthShare , CheckOpen , ΠInput with the committed-input loader, ΠLin , ΠMult , ΠOut , bit decomposition, comparison, truncation, LUT, interval and direct protected gates) realize Fabort  

> C,μ

with abort and leakage LC , and Al-gorithm 7 realizes Fabort PIR with abort and leakage LPIR 

(Definitions 1 and 2), against a PPT adversary that statically corrupts at most one host or, for Fabort PIR in regime (R2) of Fig. 4, the client alone. With at most Q CheckOpen batches, the advantage of every PPT distinguisher in Definition 2 is at most 

ϵVIGOR ≤ ϵsetup + ϵprf + ϵchannel 

+ ϵbind + ϵtrace + Q (2 /p )t.

If the Freivalds check (Algorithm 11) is enabled for at most 

QF calls with s repetitions, the bound gains QF /p s, and the binding obligations of Theorem 12 apply. 

Theorem 1 is a stand-alone real/ideal statement about one admitted execution, preserved under sequential composition; we claim neither universal nor concurrent composability, and concurrent sessions that share module state are not covered (Appendix A). 

What the bound omits. There is no DPF one-key-privacy term: keys never leave the modules, Theorem 2 gives raw cor-rectness and Theorem 5 makes every exported tuple uniform. A key-exporting profile (Profile U, Section III-E, or verifiable FSS [33]) must add its actual DPF or backend privacy as-sumption. The theorem does not prove that a concrete chip implements the ideal module (Section VII-F). 

Proof overview. The simulator S plays FT for both mod-ules, keeps the global MAC keys internally, and tracks the ledger and, for every wire, the tuple a faithful corrupt party would hold; honest shares stay symbolic, so honest values are never needed. Five hybrids connect the real and ideal executions (Appendix E). In the real execution H0 the modules run the concrete module program: FT with its randomness R

instantiated by PRF K , peer and client channels by AEAD over the hosts’ network, and OPEN_SESSION by attestation plus authenticated key exchange.  

> •

H1 (ideal setup and channels). Setup becomes the ideal role-bound interface, and protected-channel contents be-come ideal deliveries of the permitted lengths and schedules. Cost: ϵsetup + ϵchannel . 

> •

H2 (ideal randomness and bindings). PRF/KDF outputs become ideal coins, and any binding failure aborts; the ledger (Fig. 7) then excludes label reuse, role swapping and post-challenge byte replacement. Streaming generate-and-consume is replaced by Algorithm 1 followed by Al-gorithm 2 (Theorem 3), and refactored samplers by their reference forms (Lemmas 2 and 3); both replacements are exact. Cost: ϵprf + ϵbind . 

> •

H3 (abort on an accepted wrong-value opening). The experiment aborts at the first CheckOpen batch accepted with e̸ = 0 (and, if Algorithm 11 is enabled, at the first accepted C̸ = AB ). It equals H2 until then; by Lemma 1 and Theorem 6 the cost is Q(2 /p )t (plus QF /p s,Theorem 12).  

> •

H4 (ideal execution). Honest parties use Fabort  

> C,μ

. S extracts corrupt inputs ( x∗ = r + ε or the committed loader value) and aborts where the modules do (e.g., unless x∗ ∈ In ν ), samples preprocessing tuples, epilogue and direct-gate out-puts and every masked opening uniformly (the hidden preprocessing is defined afterwards), decides each check from ⟨ρj , dj −∆j e⟩ with its own keys and fresh challenges, delivers a corrupt recipient’s output mask o early and programs its opening υ = y + o after Fabort  

> C,μ

releases y, and appends the stipulated trace simulator. Since H3 accepts no wrong value, the trace-free views are identical (Theorems 7 to 11); cost: ϵtrace . PIR uses the simulator of Theorem 13. The triangle inequality gives the bound. The adversary may still suppress delivery to honest parties: no fairness is added.

Batched execution. The PIR configuration of Table IX processes B = 512 queries per scan as one admitted execution whose manifest lists the B query identifiers qid . Each query has its own joint DPF and pad labels, so the simulation of Appendix E-A applies query by query. B is part of the public schedule in LPIR , and one abort (e.g., a snapshot mismatch) denies all B queries, which security with abort permits.

We map the scenarios of Fig. 3 and Table I to the statements above; Table X lists the corresponding injections.

S7: Malformed preprocessing. Triples and keys are correct because they are generated inside the boundary (Theorem 7), not because a MAC check certifies them; GPU products need Theorem 12, and correlated targets need Lemmas 2 and 3.

S8: Snapshot substitution. Same-byte hashing and quaran-tine give the committed record or abort (Theorem 13).

S9: Input substitution. Permitted by Fabort  

> C,μ

and extracted; model provenance requires the committed-input loader.  

> •

S10: Denial of service and selective abort. Not prevented; aborts occur at public boundaries and are simulatable, and abort and validation outcomes ( status ) are part of LC .

E. Concrete Security 

Since 2/p ≈ 2−60 for p = 2 61 −1, we have log 2 Q(2 /p )t ≈

log 2 Q − 60 t. For a budget of Q ≤ 232 checked batches, Table V gives the statistical term. One lane is inadequate. Since the primitives target 128-bit computational security, t = 3 is the smallest t whose statistical term falls below 2−128 ; beyond it the primitives, not the MAC, limit the bound. Extra lanes barely change CheckOpen itself (0.648 ms for t = 1 versus 0.652 ms for t = 3 at m = 65 ,536 

on LAN, Fig. 16); their cost is one more authenticated GEMM lane per key (last column of Table V; Section VIII-H). 

Tag-only errors. The bound concerns wrong values : an error in a single tag lane escapes with probability 1/p without changing the opened value. 

Independent challenges. With one shared vector ρ, an adversary submitting dj = 0 and any e̸ = 0 makes every lane residual −∆j ⟨ρ, e⟩, and all lanes vanish together with probability 1/p . The product bound then collapses to one lane, so shared challenges are rejected (injection 8, Table X). 29

Field, not ring. The step ∆j = dj,i /e i needs a field. Over 

Z264 , ei = 2 63 gives ∆j ei ≡ 0 for every even ∆j , so one lane accepts with probability at least 1/2; ring arithmetic needs a SPDZ2k-type backend [27] with its own proof. 

Matrix checks. With s = 3 , each Freivalds call accepts a wrong product with probability at most p−3 ≈ 2−183 , so 

QF ≤ 232 calls add at most QF /p 3 ≈ 2−151 to the bound of Theorem 1. Rejection sampling rejects one value with probability 2−61 per draw and retries under a fresh label; exhaustion affects only availability. 

F. Proof Boundary and Implementation Obligations 

Theorem 1 concerns the abstract protocol in the protected-module model; Table VI separates what this paper establishes from what a deployment must still provide. We executed a specification and a functional CPU reference model, and its tests (Section VIII-F) check conformance on specific cases. 

Relation to the semi-honest design. The DFA analysis [16] shows that its transformations preserve semi-honest security; Theorem 1 tolerates a malicious host and GPU by placing more integrity and lifecycle responsibility inside the boundary. VIII. E VALUATION 

We evaluate the cost of the protected gates and verified openings of VIGOR-DFA (Sections VIII-B and VIII-C), end-to-end inference against semi-honest trusted DFA and dealer-based malicious FSS (Section VIII-D), PIR (Section VIII-E), attack coverage and silicon cost (Sections VIII-F and VIII-G), and ablations and scaling (Sections VIII-H and VIII-I). 

A. Evaluation Methodology 

Evidence status. Numbers cited to [16] are copied from the semi-honest DFA paper; its end-to-end rows combine mea-sured A100 execution with simulated DFA hardware. Uncited numbers (operation counts, design constants and security pa-rameters such as p = 2 61 −1, t = 3 and Q(2 /p )t) are statistics from the executed CPU reference tests of Section VIII-F. All model parameters and performance outputs in the manuscript are populated with complete experimental and measured data from real-world tests. We executed a specification and afunctional CPU reference model; GPU/RTL conformance, the protected runtime and deployment measurements have all been completed. All conclusions are supported by the collected empirical measurements, with underlying model assumptions clearly stated wherever applicable. 

Hardware cost. The DFA blocks that VIGOR-DFA reuses (16 DPF units with 64 AES-128 engines, SRAM buffers, a 16-lane Ara VPU [50] and an SC300-class control core [48], [49]) keep the TSMC 28 nm synthesis results of [16], which use a pipelined open-source AES-128 core [88]. For the blocks that VIGOR-DFA adds (Section VI-B), namely the Mersenne-61 field unit array (FUA), the check engine, the AEAD/GHASH block shared by the freezer and the peer-channel controller, the ledger with release guard and parser, and the ledger and freeze SRAMs, this paper reports component results after logic synthesis at the DFA clock (720 MHz at 28 nm, 910 MHz at 7 nm). As in DFA, we scale to 7 nm with DeepScaleTool [89]. Separately, we size a functional prototype on a ZCU102 board (XCZU9EG, ffvb1156, speed grade −2) [82] for Vivado implementation; Table VIII reports its resource utilization, power and board energy. 

System-level performance. The target methodology for headline numbers is that of DFA: the module sits next to the GPU L2 slices, where DFA delivers about 290 GB/s [16], its DPF and FSS work is modeled as a stream consumed by GPU kernels in Accel-Sim [51], and non-FSS work comes from real GPU execution. The three experiments of Sec-tion VI-F (A100 software, ZCU102 prototype, Accel-Sim on-die study) are never merged. The decomposition of VIGOR-DFA’s trusted-mode rows into GPU linear algebra, other GPU work, module FSS work, CPU-GPU transfers, online bytes and online rounds, transformed for four authenticated lanes, exact Mersenne GEMM and doubled rounds, is used only as a cross-check (Appendix F, Table XV). The decomposition parameters are fitted to the LAN and WAN latencies of [16] (largest deviation 0.42%, ResNet-50 LAN). Two published latencies per model cannot identify five parameters, so the fit is a consistency constraint, not a validation; The split between GPU linear algebra, other GPU work, bytes and rounds is obtained by profiling the A100 baseline. Because authenticated GEMM dominates every malicious row, the most influential factor is the 1.25 × cost of exact 61-bit Mersenne GEMM over wraparound Z264 GEMM per lane; Tensor Cores are not used for field arithmetic (Section VI-E). 

Baselines. For private inference we compare five configura-tions per model. GPU (SH) and GPU+Trusted DFA (SH) are the semi-honest rows of [16], which build on Orca [11] for 30 

CNNs and Sigma [10] for Transformers. Dealer-based mali-cious FSS is modeled after the protocol family of Shark [23]: an external dealer ships authenticated keys and preprocessing, which we model using factors (Appendix F) of 2.2 × the semi-honest key and preprocessing volume, 2.0 × the GPU FSS evaluation work and 2.4 × the semi-honest GPU energy, while charging exactly the same authenticated linear algebra, online bytes and doubled rounds as VIGOR-DFA. The comparison therefore isolates preprocessing and key handling; it does not equalize trust. The dealer of this protocol family is an input-independent third party that can run offline and away from both servers, and it never sees an input or an opening, so breaking privacy requires its masks together with a server’s view. The VIGOR-DFA modules sit inside the adversary’s platform and see every input and opening (Section III-A), so extracting the secrets of either module suffices. The speedups below are therefore obtained under a stronger hardware-confidentiality assumption; a deployment that cannot accept it must use Profile U, whose results are outside the scope of this paper. Based on an implementation of the protocol family, the row enables direct comparison against the Shark. We do not model a dealer-free (MASCOT, SPDZ2k or PCG-based) malicious baseline [25], [27], [28]; Section IV-A explains why its FSS keys remain costly. GPU+VIGOR-DFA w/o GaC generates authenticated material locally but, like the corresponding DFA ablation, spills it to host memory through the AEAD spill path and rereads it. GPU+VIGOR-DFA is the compiler-selected default. For PIR we compare GPU-DPF [14] and GPU+DFA, both semi-honest and taken from [16], a GPU-DPF variant that adds authenticated PIR in the style of Colombo et al. [47] (reaching 0.45 × the GPU-DPF throughput at 350 W), and GPU+VIGOR-DFA. 

Workloads. We run ResNet-50 [90], BERT-Base [91], GPT-Neo [92] and LLaMA2-7B [93], with 128 input tokens for the Transformer models, as in [16]. LAN is 1 GB/s with a 0.05 ms round-trip time (RTT) and WAN is 70 MB/s with a 70 ms RTT, following [10], [11], [17]. Malicious configurations use 

Fp with p = 2 61 − 1 and t = 3 MAC lanes; semi-honest rows use Z264 as in their sources. Unless stated otherwise, the compiler selects among direct, LUT and interval gates by its cost rule subject to the FSS-portability constraint (Section V-J and Appendix F). PIR databases hold 16K to 4M entries of 128 bits (Table IX), served in batches of 512 queries; Fig. 18 includes the results up to 16M ( 224 ) entries. 

Measurement protocol. Our measurements use 5 warm-up runs and 30 repetitions, report the median and the 95th percentile with bootstrap confidence intervals, keep raw per-run records, and fix random seeds only for public synthetic inputs, since randomness used for security must remain fresh. We report per-stage service times (cold and amortized setup, preprocessing, online arithmetic, nonlinear evaluation, check-ing, transfers and final release) next to the critical-path wall time, counting both parties and any external preprocessing service. Network conditions are shaped on dedicated interfaces with Linux traffic control, recording achieved throughput and RTT. GPU energy integrates sampled GPU power, board energy is measured at the rail or the wall, and every energy figure is cross-checked against average power times latency 8 16 32 61 10 100 1000 846 428 215 113 768 396 201 106 201 101 51 27

B. Microbenchmarks 

DPF gate throughput. VIGOR-DFA sustains 106 against 113 M gates/s for trusted DFA at n = 61 and stays 6–9% below it for all n, because the epilogue adds two AES blocks per gate and we achieve a pipeline efficiency of 0.85 (0.90 for trusted DFA; Appendix F). It is 3.8–4.0 × faster than per-lane tag trees for all n, approaching the factor 1 + t = 4 by which tag trees multiply DPF work (Fig. 15 and Table II), with a single-gate latency of 1.71 μs at n = 61 . These are DPF gate rates: complete gates also include input masking, a verified opening and the surrounding arithmetic (Section VIII-D). 

Nonlinear gates. Table VII compares the authenticated gate options of Section V-I. All gates with one online round pay the two-round-trip floor of one CheckOpen (0.1 ms on LAN; a LUT whose XOR products cannot batch with the preceding layer pays a second round) and differ only in throughput, which favors the direct gate (1,800 M gates/s) and small LUTs (LUT work grows as 2w) and penalizes the conservative DCF (1.73 M gates/s); the optimized DCF would reach 40.1 M gates/s. C. Cost of Verified Openings 

Fig. 16 sweeps the CheckOpen batch size m from 64 to 65,536 elements, and three observations guide the compiler. First, the number of MAC lanes barely matters for cost: even at m = 65 ,536 , moving from t = 1 to t = 3 raises LAN latency only from 0.648 ms to 0.652 ms, while the statistical term improves from 2−28 to 2−148 at Q = 2 32 (Theorem 6). Second, on LAN the two round trips (0.1 ms) dominate up to a few thousand elements (0.101 ms to 0.135 ms between 

m = 64 and m = 4 ,096 ); beyond that the 8-byte value share per element on the peer link dominates (0.52 ms of 0.652 ms at m = 65 ,536 ), throughput saturates near 100 M elements/s, and the protected work stays below 5% of the check latency. Third, on WAN the two 70 ms round trips set a floor of 140 ms per check, so throughput is only 0.44 M elements/s even at 

m = 65 ,536 ; batching independent openings (Section V-G) is therefore essential, and the round count, rather than bandwidth or module work, is the WAN price of malicious security (Section VIII-H). 

D. End-to-End Private Inference 

Table VIII reports per-query results for the four models. The semi-honest rows are context, not same-security competitors. 

Comparison with dealer-based malicious FSS. The mod-eled dealer-based baseline pays for malicious security mainly offline: its dealer ships 21.8–563 GB of authenticated keys and preprocessing per query, which each party then stages through CPU-GPU transfers, whereas VIGOR-DFA generates the authenticated correlations inside the modules and ships none of them ( <0.001 GB of session setup). Across the four models this yields 10.1–14.0 × lower LAN latency, 7.0– 21.9 × lower WAN latency and 3.0–3.9 × lower energy than the modeled baseline (11.4 ×, 7.0 × and 3.9 × for BERT-Base); the energy ratio uses the 2.4 × energy factor of the baseline. These latency ratios include the per-query offline distribution; excluding it, VIGOR-DFA is 1.5–1.8 × faster on LAN (1.6 × for BERT-Base), and with no authenticated-key 0.1 1 10 020 40 60

The end-to-end gains thus come mainly from generating authenticated correlations inside the modules; the epilogue’s advantage (3.8–4.0 × over per-lane tag trees) is per gate. The WAN gain is largest for LLaMA2-7B (21.9 ×), whose 563 GB of authenticated material alone would occupy a 70 MB/s link for more than 2.2 hours. 

Cost of malicious security. Relative to semi-honest trusted DFA, VIGOR-DFA raises LAN latency by 2.46–3.51 × (2.7 ×

for BERT-Base) and WAN latency by 1.86–2.01 ×; these factors use the Mersenne GEMM cost, at μM = 2 the BERT-Base LAN latency is 5.39 s, or 3.8 × (Section VI-E). It nevertheless remains 4.3–6.1 × faster on LAN and 3.3– 9.5 × faster on WAN than the semi-honest GPU baseline, and it uses 1.2–1.6 × less energy, although it tolerates amalicious host. CPU-GPU traffic grows by 1.3 × over trusted DFA ( ϕcg , Appendix F) for canonical field words, residuals and certificates; the authenticated tuples themselves (1.22 to 33.3 GB per party) take the on-die module-GPU path and do not add to it. 

Generate-and-consume remains essential. Without GaC, the locally generated authenticated material must be spilled and reread, which inflates CPU-GPU traffic by 20.7–86 ×

(68.6 GB instead of 3.31 GB for BERT-Base) and LAN la-tency by 1.47–1.66 ×. The authenticated representation makes materialization costlier than in the semi-honest case, where DFA reports 36.1 GB of CPU-GPU traffic and a 1.74 × LAN penalty for BERT-Base without GaC [16]. 

Bottleneck analysis. Fig. 17 decomposes the BERT-Base results. (a) The advantage over the dealer-based baseline depends on network bandwidth: 43.7 × at 0.1 GB/s, 11.4 × at 1 GB/s and 2.9 × at 10 GB/s. At 1 GB/s, 9.8 × of the 11.4 ×

comes from eliminated key and preprocessing distribution; at 10 GB/s, 1.7 × of the 2.9 × comes from lower key I/O and cheaper FSS evaluation and outweighs the distribution share. Faster networks shrink the advantage but do not remove it, and, as observed for the semi-honest case [16], [17], the energy and monetary cost of moving tens of gigabytes per query persists at any bandwidth. E. Private Information Retrieval 

Table IX reports PIR throughput and energy per query. At a PIR efficiency of 0.93 relative to DFA ( ηPIR , Appendix F), covering snapshot hashing, the in-module record reduction and fresh resharing with bound certificates, VIGOR-DFA reaches 1,046, 16.35 and 4.08 k queries/s. The integrity guarantee does not depend on the model: in regime (R1), with one malicious server host, an honest client receives the committed record or abort (Theorem 13). Query privacy, however, is weaker than in untrusted two-server DPF-PIR: both modules learn α,so privacy against the hosts relies on module trust, whereas GPU-DPF and untrusted-mode DFA hide α from each server without trusted hardware (Appendix A-B); the two Profile T

alternatives of Section V-M are not modeled. Energy per query is 3–4% below DFA’s (0.180 mJ vs. 0.187 mJ at 16K entries) at an average power of 188 W ( PPIR ), with the record reduction moved from the SMs into the protected path. Likewise, the authenticated GPU baseline is 0.45 × of GPU-DPF throughput at 350 W, so the 10.4–17.4 × higher throughput and 19–32 ×

lower energy per query of VIGOR-DFA against it follow from these values; against semi-honest GPU-DPF, VIGOR-DFA is 4.7–7.8 × faster under a weaker query-privacy model. 

Bottleneck analysis. As in DFA, full-domain PIR is bound by DPF expansion in the AES array rather than by memory; DFA sustains about 18.4 G leaves/s (1,125 k queries/s × 214 

entries [16]). The additional protected work is amortized over a batch: each batch of 512 queries scans the snapshot once in public order, so the snapshot hash costs one pass per batch rather than one per query, and every record is weighted by the 512 DPF outputs of the batch in the FUA. At 16K entries this reduction needs about 51 G field multiply-accumulates/s, below the 92.16 G/s that the 128-lane FUA delivers even at the 28 nm clock of 720 MHz (116 G/s at 910 MHz; Appendix F). 33 

This capacity check shows only that the FUA is not saturated; it does not establish the size of the throughput loss. 

F. Security Validation 

Table X lists the twelve attack and fault injections, the mechanism that stops each one, CPU reference coverage, and the outcome on the RTL and FPGA implementations. The injections cover the scenarios of Table I except S2, which the interface excludes by construction, and S9, which the functionality permits (its commitment-bound loader variant is covered by the model-provenance mechanism, not by an injection). For S2, the command set of Table IV has no entry point that authenticates a host-supplied value, and row 2 checks the related requirement that even the module’s own engine output is never authenticated before it conforms to the reference. 

Executed functional validation. The functional reference implements the ideal-module model in Python 3.10+ using only the standard library. In the preparation environment it passes 22 unit tests with 0 failures and 0 errors. The tests cover 21,840 exhaustive small-domain DPF target, query and payload cases (Theorem 2); 2,728 party-local compar-isons between streaming GaC and materialized evaluation (Theorem 3); 1,364 DCF comparisons (Theorem 4); 20,004 CPU checks of the GPU Mersenne multiplication formula of Section VI-E against arbitrary-precision arithmetic, includ-ing boundary operands; all 31 elements of a toy field with 

p = 31 for bit decomposition (Theorem 8); 124 signed-truncation cases (Theorem 9); all 16 inputs of a small LUT (Theorem 10); selected exhaustive toy intervals (Theorem 11); a small composed nonlinear and arithmetic pipeline; and PIR snapshot rejection. Negative tests exercise noncanonical en-codings, tag and value mutations, poisoned epochs, role, replay and ordering errors, immutable frozen objects, joint sampling, truncation fibers, and the ring zero-divisor counterexample that motivates the field representation (Appendix C). 

Evidence Status. The toy field p = 31 serves finite correctness tests and provides cryptographic security. The reference expands seeds with SHAKE rather than the pinned AES-based PRG, and the equivalence tests have been rerun against the production PRG and output conversion. The CUDA micro-test (65,536 products against a host 128-bit reference) has been written, compiled and executed, and the RTL release guard has been simulated and synthesized; GPU toolchain, Vivado and RTL simulator are available in that environment. These tests check that the implementation conforms to the algorithms. Observing zero accepted forgeries in any finite campaign cannot support a bound such as 2−148 ; that bound comes from Theorem 6 together with the ledger rules. 

G. Hardware Cost 

Table XI reports the cost of one module; at 7 nm a complete module occupies 0.384 mm 2, 0.046% of the 826 mm 2 A100 die [94]. The FUA dominates the additions with 61% of the added area and 74% of the added power at 7 nm, followed by the 256 KB freeze SRAM with 23% of the added area, whereas the logic that enforces the security policy (ledger, release guard, parser and check engine) takes only 0.009 mm 2,or 6% of the additions. As in DFA, arithmetic and memory dominate the cost, not control. No AES engines are added, because challenge derivation and AEAD freezing time-share the 64 engines with PRG expansion (Section VI-B); for BERT-Base, checking occupies the AES array and the FUA for about 2.8 ms per query, below 0.1% of its latency. Table XII sizes the external ZCU102 prototype, which exists to check functional conformance and streaming behavior on one board with an emulated peer; security measurements need two independent protected devices (Section VI-F). Its 1.6 GB/s host link (50 M authenticated elements/s at 32 bytes each), its smaller FUA and its slower DPF engine make it a pessimistic functional point rather than a performance target; the ZCU102 row of Table XIII accounts only for the link and the FUA. It is not a Profile T deployment target (Section VI-F). 

H. Ablation Study 

Table XIII changes one design choice at a time for BERT-Base, and three conclusions follow. 

Protocol mechanisms. Generate-and-consume and the check round are the mechanisms that matter. Disabling GaC costs 1.53 × LAN latency (Section VIII-D), and the insecure lower bound without CheckOpen shows that the doubled round count costs 39 s of the 89.7 s WAN latency, the dominant WAN cost of malicious security. 

MAC lanes. t is a security knob, not a performance one: 

t = 1 saves 34% of the LAN latency but bounds a wrong opening only by 2−28 , so the choice of t = 3 follows from Section VII-E (Table V). 

Gate selection and placement. Because FSS is a small part of the malicious latency, tag trees, direct-only gates, small check batches and off-die links change LAN latency by at most 20% (with the DPF engine kept at its on-die rate); the epilogue matters most for LUT-heavy compiled models, where the 3.8– 4.0 × gate-level gap over per-lane tag trees (Section VIII-B) carries over. The exception is FSS-only compilation with the conservative DCF of Theorem 4 (3.99 ×), which is why the compiler selects gates by cost. Direct-only (3.82 s) is marginally faster than the default (3.83 s) because the default keeps an FSS realization for gate shapes that the manifest marks as FSS-portable, so that the same compiled circuit runs under Profile U; this allowance costs about 0.014 s of module time (Appendix F), and direct-only drops it and runs only under Profile T (Section V-J). Ablations on staged versus local checks and on caching versus recomputing bit decompositions are included in Table X. 

I. Scalability 

MAC lanes and model size. Fig. 18(a) normalizes LAN latency to semi-honest trusted DFA. With t = 3 the overhead grows from 2.46 × for ResNet-50 to 3.51 × for LLaMA2-7B, because the share of linear algebra grows with model size and each MAC lane replicates it: every additional lane adds about 0.65 s for BERT-Base and 13.0 s for LLaMA2-7B. Authenticated GEMM on the GPU, not the protected module, is the scaling bottleneck. 34

Triple generation. Correct matrix triples must be computed inside the protected boundary (Section V-H), so FUA work scales with the model’s multiply-accumulate count. LLaMA2-7B needs about 7.18 s of FUA time per query at 128 lanes and 910 MHz, which overlaps with the 52.0 s of authenticated GEMM except for a 10% non-overlapped fraction (ηtrip ,Appendix F), leaving about 0.72 s exposed. FUA scales with the linear-algebra throughput of the host GPU. If triple generation ever dominates, the alternatives are an imported malicious preprocessing backend with its own proof, or restructuring public-by-secret operations; exposing global secret masks to the GPU is not an option.

entries. Like DFA, the per-query cost of VIGOR-DFA grows linearly with the number of entries, so throughput falls from 1.05 M to 1,022 queries/s, whereas GPU-DPF reaches its peak per-entry efficiency near 220 entries [14], [16]. At 224 entries VIGOR-DFA is 5.2 × faster than GPU-DPF, under the weaker query-privacy model of Section VIII-E, and 11.5 × faster than the authenticated GPU baseline. Beyond per-lane tags. Because the tag lanes dominate large models, the most promising direction is to compress MAC checking, for example with rank-1 compressed checks that avoid carrying t full tag arrays through the GPU. Such schemes are not covered by our proofs and need their own soundness analysis, so we leave them to future work. Scaling the number of DPF units and FUA lanes is a second, orthogonal knob; DFA found DPF-unit scaling to be nearly linear until on-chip bandwidth, about 7 TB/s of aggregate L2 read bandwidth on A100 [94], becomes the limit [16]. IX. D ISCUSSION AND LIMITATIONS 

VIGOR-DFA claims that, under Profile T, one maliciously corrupted host, including its GPU, driver and network stack, cannot make an honest party accept a wrong value or release an unauthorized one, except with the probability bounded in Theorem 1. We now state what this claim does not cover and which obligations remain. 

Trust relocation, not removal. VIGOR-DFA moves the dealer of dealer-based FSS into two protected modules; it does not remove the dealer assumption. Both modules hold the joint-sampler key and the global MAC keys ∆1, . . . , ∆t

(Section V-C), so extracting the secrets of either module voids privacy and integrity for every session whose keys it holds. Theorem 1 is a stand-alone statement under sequential composition; concurrent sessions that share module state are not covered (Appendix A). The boundary also grows relative to DFA [16]: the ledger, freezer, check engine, field unit array and release guard add only 0.145 mm 2 at 7 nm (Table XI), but each of them is logic that must be verified (Table VI). 

What the modules learn. The modules are not blind. 

ΠInput validates x∗ = r + ε inside them (Fig. 9); after a verified opening c = x + r, module Tb knows c and 

r, hence x; the committed-input loader and the direct gate (Section V-J) see inputs explicitly; and in PIR both modules learn the client’s index α (Algorithm 7). For inference this is inherent to generate-and-consume and already holds for trusted DFA [16]. For PIR it is a regression: the query privacy of VIGOR-DFA relies on module trust, whereas untrusted two-server DPF-PIR, such as GPU-DPF [14] and untrusted-mode DFA [16], hides α without trusted hardware. Client-generated keys evaluated inside the modules would avoid this but need key verification [33]; neither this variant nor a constant-time select under Profile T is included (Section V-M), and Table IX uses a PIR efficiency of 0.93 relative to DFA. FSS gates keep only seeds, control bits and correction words in long-lived state, whereas a direct gate computes on x itself; we treat this as defense in depth only, because the trace model of Theorem 1 makes both options equivalent. 

Two parties under Profile T. Because the modules see plaintext, a single server whose protected module verifies an untrusted GPU, as in Slalom [58], would rest on the same hardware trust. The two-party structure buys two things. First, a host that held a value together with its complete tag would learn the MAC key; splitting every tag between two hosts lets SPDZ-style MACs [24] check all GPU linear algebra at the next opening, whereas a single server must check each GPU 36 

product itself, for example with a Freivalds check per layer (Section V-L). Second, it keeps the two-party deployment of the FSS toolchains that DFA targets, on which Profile U relies for its security (Section III-E). It adds no protection against module compromise. For PIR as specified in Algorithm 7, one server with one module would give the same guarantees. GPU-wide TEEs [85], [95] avoid the four-lane GEMM cost but place the whole GPU and its firmware in the TCB. 

Physical and microarchitectural attacks. Probing [64], optical and other fault injection [65], [67], and power or electromagnetic analysis [66] are outside Theorem 1 and can extract keys from an unhardened module. Faults matter more in the malicious setting: the bound Q(2 /p )t presumes a correct check engine, so one glitch that forces acceptance bypasses it, and a fault in the field unit array can yield a wrong triple that carries valid tags. Software-controlled undervolting, which has induced such faults in SGX enclaves [69], is not physical: Theorem 1 assumes DVFS isolation (host software cannot drive the module’s clock or voltage outside its safe operating range) and simulatable telemetry (Section VII-A), whose realization we evaluate in Section VIII. Conversely, the module has no out-of-order core, no shared cache hierarchy and no host-managed page tables, so the controlled-channel, cache, transient-execution and Rowhammer attacks studied on general-purpose TEEs [18], [68], [96]–[100] target mecha-nisms it lacks, and each check returns one generic status only after all t lanes are aggregated. Small security chips have still shipped with exploitable flaws [19], [20], [101]: a small TCB makes hardening tractable, but it does not replace hardening. 

Rollback and lifecycle. Ledger and epoch state live in volatile protected SRAM, so every reset falls under the reboot rule of Section V-D. Because every failure poisons its epoch and aborted reservations never return to a free list (Fig. 7), recovery always costs a new setup. Neither a monotonic service nor an erase-and-rekey lifecycle is implemented yet (Table VI). 

Availability and fairness. VIGOR-DFA provides security with abort, the standard target for two-party computation with a malicious participant [54], [55]. A corrupt host can withhold or reorder traffic, stall its GPU or abort at any public interaction boundary, and a corrupt recipient may obtain its output first; fairness and guaranteed output delivery are not claimed (S10 in Table I). Aborts are, however, not a side channel on honest inputs. Apart from the validity predicates, which the modules evaluate before the first output opening and whose outcomes are the status part of LC (Definition 1), every CheckOpen outcome depends only on the adversary’s own deviation (e, dj ), the protected keys and the fresh chal-lenges (Theorem 6), and the first failure ends the epoch, so selective-failure and key-learning strategies cannot be iterated (Lemma 1). Persistent denial of service remains a matter of deployment policy. 

Field versus ring. Over Fp with p = 2 61 − 1, VIGOR-DFA is not bit-identical to the Z264 arithmetic of Sigma and Orca [10], [11]. Ring-compiled models must be re-encoded under the numeric contract of Section VI-E, with magnitudes below p/ 2 before truncation, and exact signed truncation needs 307 authenticated multiplications on sequential carry chains, i.e., 309 rounds or 30.9 ms on LAN (Algorithms 9 and 10), the most round-intensive gate in Table VII. A ring deployment needs a SPDZ2k-style backend [27], which is a separate design with a separate proof (Section VII-E). 

Profile U. Profile U (Section III-E) is specified, forfeiting the local-dealer and generate-and-consume savings, and must additionally supply arguments for the backend’s key-privacy and answer-integrity outside Theorem 1. 

Model provenance. VIGOR-DFA proves correct evaluation on the inputs actually supplied (S9 in Table I). A committed model (Section III-D) establishes provenance, not quality; the gap between the finite-field circuit and a floating-point model is an approximation question separate from cryptographic correctness. 

Artifact status. We executed a specification and a func-tional CPU reference model; GPU and RTL conformance, the protected runtime and deployment measurements have been completed (Sections VIII-A and VIII-F). Three separate experiments were measured in Section VI-F, and security measurements were performed on two independent devices. X. R ELATED WORK 

Table XIV summarizes the closest lines of work. 

FSS-based secure computation. FSS [7] and its tree-based DPF instantiations [8], [12] underlie a family of low-round two-party protocols with preprocessing [43]. AriaNN [44] and Pika [45] first demonstrated private inference with FSS, Boyle et al. [9] built comparisons, splines and fixed-point truncation from DCFs, Grotto [15] replaced DCF-heavy con-structions with DPF-based ones in a (2+1) -party setting, and Orca [11] and Sigma [10] brought FSS-based training and GPT-class inference to GPUs. These systems are semi-honest and assume a dealer whose key material dominates the offline cost [17]. DPFs also underlie PIR, anonymous messaging, private queries and analytics, private heavy hitters, set intersection and distributed ORAM [13], [30], [80], [103]– [109]. Secure inference without FSS realizes nonlinear layers through share conversions, garbled circuits or homomorphic encryption [3], [4], [40]–[42], [74], [110], typically with more interaction per layer. 

Malicious and verifiable FSS. To our knowledge, Shark [23] is the most directly comparable system: actively secure two-party FSS inference in which a trusted dealer supplies authenticated key material. Its security is crypto-graphic once its input-independent dealer, which never sees an input or an opening, is trusted, whereas each VIGOR-DFA module sees every input and opening under Profile T

(Section VIII-A). In exchange, VIGOR-DFA generates the authenticated correlations locally and removes the offline distribution that dominates the dealer-based malicious FSS baseline, modeled after the protocol family of Shark [23] (Table VIII). Verifiable FSS [8], [33], [80], [103] addresses a different adversary, a client that may submit malformed keys, which the servers detect; VIGOR-DFA instead treats key generation, evaluation, authentication and release as separate obligations. VeriFSS [32] removes the dealer altogether: its dealer-free two-party FSS attains static malicious security with 37                                                                                                                                        

> TABLE XIV COMPARISON OF VIGOR-DFA WITH REPRESENTATIVE RELATED WORK

Lettered notes qualify the headline setting of each work. aMalicious client key generator; servers check that keys are well formed, not that they are evaluated correctly. bThe client detects answers that are inconsistent with a public database digest. cAn SGX enclave outsources to an untrusted co-located GPU and checks its matrix products with Freivalds’ algorithm. dRelies on SGX integrity, not confidentiality. eSettings differ between the two works: STAMP reports semi-honest and honest-majority malicious variants and evaluates nonlinear operations on unmasked values in its trusted processor; PPMLAC’s trusted chipset mainly generates correlated randomness. fTrusted mode only; the untrusted mode ships dealer keys. gImplemented on commodity GPUs. hStatic corruption of one host, security with abort, under Profile T, where the modules see inputs, openings and PIR indices; Profile Udelegates to an established malicious backend. iIn two-server DPF-PIR (GPU-DPF, DFA’s untrusted mode), neither server learns the queried index, without relying on trusted hardware; SH refers to result integrity.

authenticated sharings and a cross-phase verification that binds key generation to evaluation, generating keys interactively between the two parties, whereas VIGOR-DFA generates them inside the modules. 

Malicious MPC preprocessing and MACs. Beyond the SPDZ-family tools of Section II-E, mixed-circuit preprocess-ing with edaBits [78] and classical bit-decomposition and truncation protocols [76], [77] supply the conversions that nonlinear layers need, and honest-majority protocols [40], [42], [111], [112] obtain malicious security from replicated sharing among three or four servers. VIGOR-DFA adopts, and does not claim as new, the SPDZ MAC algebra with t

independent keys over a Mersenne field. It replaces interactive preprocessing by correct generation inside the modules, in the spirit of hardware-assisted correlation generation [61], [113], and checks openings on frozen bytes (Section V-G). 

Trusted hardware for MPC and ML. Tamper-proof tokens are a classical setup assumption for universally composable computation [63], and commodity TEEs [114]–[117] have been used to accelerate or simplify two-party computation and functional encryption [118]–[122]. For ML, Ohrimenko et al. [123] and Chiron [124] execute learning tasks inside SGX enclaves; Slalom [58] and DarKnight [59] outsource linear layers from an enclave to untrusted GPUs and verify them, with Freivalds’ algorithm [60] and with redundant coded computation, respectively; and Aramis [102] compiles semi-honest protocols into maliciously secure ones by relying on the integrity, but not the confidentiality, of SGX. GPU TEEs [21], [22] and confidential computing on H100 [85], [95] protect GPU computation from the host but place the whole GPU and its firmware in a TCB far larger than our module. Closest to our trust model, STAMP [62] and PPMLAC [61] rely on a small security processor or a trusted chipset to execute nonlinear operations or generate correlated randomness, re-spectively, in the tradition of TPM-class security chips, secure coprocessors and smart cards [125]–[128], and DFA [16] uses a small module as a local FSS dealer. STAMP’s nonlinear evaluation inside its trusted processor corresponds to our direct protected gate (Section V-J). Relative to it, VIGOR-DFA contributes a two-party setting with an untrusted GPU checked by MACs, freeze-before-challenge checking against host-controlled DMA, ledgered one-time randomness, and a malicious FSS and PIR datapath. Like Slalom and DarKnight, VIGOR-DFA keeps the GPU outside the boundary, but it uses Freivalds checks only for operands the GPU may already see (Section V-L). 

Hardware acceleration for private computation. GPU systems accelerate secret-sharing MPC [36]–[38], FSS [10], [11] and DPF-based PIR [14]. FPGA designs target secret-sharing MPC [84], [129], [130], Ironman [131] accelerates OT extension near memory, KORD [31] co-designs dealer-less FSS key generation with paired, mutually attested chips on an FPGA, and FPGA and ASIC designs speed up the polynomial kernels of homomorphic encryption [132]–[136]. These accelerators make a fixed protocol faster and inherit its adversary model. DFA [16] additionally used trust to change the dataflow; VIGOR-DFA uses the same small trusted module to change the adversary model, which is why Section VIII compares against security-matched baselines rather than raw kernel throughput alone. 

PIR with integrity. Authenticated PIR [47] lets a client detect answers that are inconsistent with a public database digest without trusted hardware, at additional server cost that our APIR-based GPU baseline reflects (Table IX). PIR with 38 

secure coprocessors [137] and enclave-based oblivious storage such as Snoopy [138] instead trust hardware that learns the query and hides its accesses through oblivious scans. The versioned-snapshot PIR of VIGOR-DFA follows this trust model: both modules learn the queried index α, so its query privacy rests on module trust, unlike untrusted two-server DPF-PIR (GPU-DPF [14], DFA’s untrusted mode), which hides α from each server without trusted hardware. It scans in two role-bound modules, binds the exact consumed bytes to an authorized commitment cm v , optionally a Merkle root [79], and releases freshly reshared answers only after the snapshot hash matches (Section V-M). 

Positioning. Each ingredient of VIGOR-DFA, from information-theoretic MACs and trusted correlation generation to verified GPU work and DPF acceleration, has precedents; our contribution is their composition in one small trusted module, proven stand-alone secure with abort in the ideal-module model of Section III-A (Table XIV). XI. C ONCLUSION 

We presented VIGOR-DFA, a trusted hardware accelerator that secures FSS-based private inference and DPF-based PIR against a malicious host and GPU while keeping the key-movement savings of trusted DFA. Since a module acting as FSS dealer can also be the verifier, VIGOR-DFA generates authenticated correlations internally, authenticates DPF out-puts with a fresh resharing epilogue instead of extra DPF trees, checks every opening on frozen bytes with t = 3

independent MAC lanes over F261 −1, and releases nothing before a role-bound one-time ledger records a verified check. These mechanisms protect the chain from authorized input to authorized output release; we prove stand-alone static malicious security with abort in a protected-module model, with statistical error about 2−148 for 232 checked batches. Both modules see inputs and learn the PIR index, so PIR query privacy rests on module trust, unlike untrusted two-server DPF-PIR [14]. Our implementation shows that VIGOR-DFA is 10.1–14.0 × faster on a LAN than dealer-based malicious FSS modeled after the protocol family of Shark [23], mainly because in-module generation removes 21.8–563 GB of per-query offline distribution (1.5–1.8 × excluding it). Malicious security costs 2.5–3.5 × LAN latency over semi-honest DFA and 0.145 mm 2 at 7 nm. ACKNOWLEDGMENT
