APPENDIX AIDEAL FUNCTIONALITIES AND THE PROTECTED -M ODULE 

RESOURCE 

This appendix states in full what Fig. 4 and Defini-tions 1 and 2 summarize: the circuit functionality Fabort  

> C,μ

(Ap-pendix A-A), the PIR functionality Fabort PIR (Appendix A-B) and the ideal resource FT of one protected module under Profile T (Appendix A-C). The proofs in Appendices B to E use no other trust assumption. 

Real and ideal executions. Security is Definition 2with the bound ϵVIGOR of Theorem 1; the view of A in 

Real Π,A(z),I (μ,⃗ x) includes the observable trace of its mod-ule. In the real execution, I contains either at most one statically chosen computing host or, for Fabort PIR in regime (R2) (Appendix A-B), the client alone. A corrupt computing host comprises the CPU, operating system, driver, runtime, GPU, host memory and network interface of one party Pb⋆ ,

b⋆ ∈ { 0, 1}. The corrupt host may issue any command to its local module, modify, replay, reorder, delay or drop any message or buffer under its control, and stop at any time. The modules T0, T 1 are never corrupted; each runs the concrete module program, which instantiates the randomness, channels and setup of FT with real primitives (Appendix A-C). A

may prevent honest outputs; neither fairness nor availability 42 

is provided. The statement covers one admitted execution (one session, one manifest); independent sessions use fresh identities, epochs and keys (Section V-C). This is stand-alone real/ideal security with abort under sequential composi-tion [52]; we claim neither universal composability [139] nor security for concurrent sessions that share module state. 

A. Circuit Functionality 

Manifest. The public manifest μ contains the protocol version; the session identities and roles; the field Fp with 

p = 2 61 − 1 and the tag count t; the numeric encoding (fixed-point scale, signed encoding, truncation rule, saturation and range behavior, output decoding); the finite circuit C with its gate identifiers, wire identifiers and tensor shapes; the approved public tables and interval coefficients; the authorized recipient of every output; the input interface and validation rules of every input wire; and the public batch schedule. For an input wire ν we write In ν for its declared interface (canonical encoding, shape and admissible range) and Oν

for its designated owner. We write V for the finite set of validity predicates of μ, evaluated inside the modules at public boundaries declared in μ, and y1, . . . , y K for the outputs of C,where output yk has the authorized recipient Rk. Since Fabort 

> C,μ

is non-reactive and computes status only after it has recorded every input, the public schedule of μ places every input gate before every validity check, and both before the first output opening. Setup binds the manifest hash (Section V-C), so all parties agree on μ or abort.                                                                                                  

> Functionality Fabort
> C,μ
> Parameters: public manifest μ(circuit Cover Fp, interfaces In ν, predicates
> V, recipients Rk, schedule). Parties: input owners, P0, P 1, recipients; S
> controls the statically corrupted host; the external client is honest and does not collude with a computing party. 1. Inputs. For every input wire ν: if Oνis honest, receive xνfrom Oν; if
> Oνis corrupted, receive x∗
> νfrom Sand set xν←x∗
> ν. Send (input , ν )
> and the public metadata of νto S. If xνis missing, malformed or not in In ν, send (abort , ν )to all parties and halt. 2. Validation. Compute status = ( V(⃗x)) V∈V on the recorded inputs ⃗x= ( xν)νand send it to S. If some V(⃗x) = 0 , send (abort , V )to all parties and halt. 3. Evaluation. Compute (y1, . . . , y K)←C(⃗x)exactly in the finite arithmetic of μ, including every declared comparison, truncation, table lookup and piecewise polynomial. 4. Corrupt and public outputs. Send to Severy ykwhose recipient Rk
> is corrupted and every ykthat μdeclares public. 5. Delivery. At each public interaction boundary of the schedule, Smay send delay or abort . On abort , send abort to every honest party and halt. Otherwise, when Ssends (deliver , k ), give ykto its honest recipient Rk.

Leakage. The leakage is LC = ( μ, len , sched , pub , status )

of Definition 1: len and sched are functions of μ alone, pub 

are the outputs of Step 4 that μ declares public, and status 

is the validation vector and the abort outcomes of Steps 1 and 2. An input-dependent predicate thus reveals its accept or abort bit; input ranges and intermediate bounds should be fixed statically where possible (Section VI-E) so that validity bits are input independent. 

Extraction. A corrupted owner’s input is x∗ = r + ε

for the full mask r and the bound masked difference ε of 

ΠInput (Fig. 9). Both modules, which generated r, compute 

x∗ and abort unless x∗ ∈ In ν ; the simulator, which samples 

r, emulates the same abort (Appendix E). Fabort  

> C,μ

accepts any value in the declared interface, not an input that an application hopes the adversary would use. 

Exact finite semantics. Fabort  

> C,μ

evaluates the declared fi-nite circuit over Fp, neither a real-valued network nor Z264 

arithmetic. The gap between C and an intended floating-point model (approximation error, fixed-point rounding) is compiler accuracy, not a security error. A ring deployment needs a dif-ferent functionality and authenticated backend (Section V-B). 

Gate implementation is public. Whether a nonlinear gate is compiled to a direct protected gate, a small XOR-masked LUT or an interval FSS gate (Section V-J) is recorded in μ,and hence in LC . All choices implement the same gate of C

and leave Fabort  

> C,μ

unchanged. 

Model provenance. If μ binds a pre-approved model by a commitment to canonical quantized weight bytes (Sec-tion III-D), the interface In ν of the weight wires contains only the byte strings that open this commitment; otherwise the weights are their owner’s freely chosen input. 

B. PIR Functionality 

Let desc v = ( id , v, N, enc , cm v ) be the public descriptor of snapshot version v, where cm v commits to the database identifier, the version, the shape, the record encoding and the ordered record bytes. Let D = ( D[0] , . . . , D [N − 1]) be the snapshot that cm v binds; a second opening is a binding failure charged to ϵbind (Theorem 1).                                                        

> Functionality Fabort PIR
> Parameters: descriptor desc vand the committed snapshot D.1. Query. Receive (query ,qid , α, desc v)from the client, with a fresh query identifier qid . If the request is malformed or α / ∈ { 0, . . . , N −1},send reject to the client and to Sand halt. Otherwise send LPIR
> (Definition 1) to S.2. Retrieval. Set y←D[α]; for a multi-field record, ycontains every coordinate of D[α].3. Delivery. Smay send abort ,and then the client receives abort ;otherwise the client receives y.
> Corruption regimes (separate statements).
> (R1) Malicious server host: the client is honest and Scorrupts at most one server host; Slearns nothing beyond Step 1. (R2) Malicious client only: both server hosts are honest and only the client is corrupted; in Step 1, Schooses α∗(extraction) and in Step 3 it receives D[α∗]only.

Scope of the two regimes. The regimes are not combined: no claim covers a malicious client colluding with a malicious server host. Regime (R1) is the integrity and query-privacy statement; regime (R2) is the database-privacy statement. The descriptor desc v , including cm v , is part of LPIR in both regimes. 

Padding and boundary cases. For N ≥ 2 the protocol pads the snapshot to 2n records with n = ⌈log 2 N ⌉ and authorizes only α < N ; for N = 1 it uses a constant-record special case with the same snapshot and release checks (Algorithm 7). Padding is invisible in Fabort PIR because only 

α < N is admissible and both the true and the padded length are functions of desc v .

Non-claims. Fabort PIR is realized under trusted modules, and both modules learn α: the query privacy of (R1) holds against the hosts only and rests on module trust, unlike untrusted two-server DPF-PIR [5], [8] (GPU-DPF [14], untrusted-mode 43 

DFA [16]), which hides α from each server without trusted hardware. Its database privacy in (R2) likewise rests on the trusted modules; it does not make untrusted two-server DPF-PIR symmetric. Externally generated verifiable-FSS keys [33] would have to prove client key validity and server-answer integrity separately, and authenticated PIR [47] needs its own statement (Section V-M). 

C. Ideal Protected-Module Resource 

The resource FT captures the premise of Profile T: the code, keys, input parser, arithmetic, state transitions, local memory, protected peer channel and approved I/O paths of a module are correct and confidential. The instance F(b) 

> T

of party b

accepts commands from host b and from external endpoints over authenticated confidential channels, and it talks to its peer instance over an ideal protected channel. 

Resource F(b) 

> T

: state, channel and global rule State. Role b; session identifier sid ; epoch ep ; manifest μ; epoch state 

∈ { active , poisoned }; a joint random function R, which returns a fresh uniform string for every new label and the same string to both instances for a joint label; global MAC keys ∆1, . . . , ∆t and their additive shares 

∆0,j , ∆1,j (joint draws); a ledger that maps every gate to one of NEW ,

RESERVED , PREPARED , FROZEN , VERIFIED , CONSUMED , RE-LEASED , ABORTED ; a frozen store; the set of burned randomness identifiers. 

Peer channel. Messages between the two instances are delivered privately and unmodified. The adversary learns their lengths and times and may delay or drop them; a missing message leads to abort. 

Global rule. Every command first checks that the epoch state is active ,that the frame is canonical (length prefixed, field elements below p, no truncation, extra bytes or duplicate identifiers) and that μ allows the requested ledger transition. Gates move only along the chain of Fig. 7. At each validation boundary of μ, the instance evaluates every predicate of V on the bound inputs. Any failure executes ABORT . No command authenticates a host-supplied value. 

Resource F(b) 

> T

: commands (from host b unless noted) 

1. OPEN_SESSION : with the peer instance, agree on the ordered identities, roles 0 and 1, sid , a fresh ep , the manifest hash, the field and tag parameters and the protocol version, or abort. Return a session capability and ∆b, 1, . . . , ∆b,t to host b.2. RESERVE_PREP (g, ids ): if g is NEW and ids are unused, burn ids and set RESERVED ; draw the preprocessing of g from R (masks, triples with c = ab , authenticated bits, internally held FSS material) and share it with AuthShare ; set PREPARED and return the tuples of party b

once. For an input gate ν, also send the full mask r to the owner over its confidential channel, bind the owner’s ε after comparing it with the peer, and execute ABORT unless r + ε ∈ In ν ; for a private output, send the full output mask o to the recipient. 3. FREEZE_BATCH (g, bytes , dims , wires ): parse canonically; copy the bytes into the frozen store or into immutable AEAD pages bound to sequence numbers, lengths, gate identifiers and a protected root; hash the descriptor and the bytes; set FROZEN ; return the handle and the root. 4. CHECK_OPEN (h): with the peer, run Algorithm 4 on the frozen bytes of handle h; on acceptance set VERIFIED and return the authorized opened values; otherwise return one generic failure and execute ABORT .5. EVAL_FSS (g, h ): only for a VERIFIED masked opening bound to g

and role b; evaluate the bound FSS or direct gate internally, apply the epilogue, set CONSUMED and return the fresh tuple [y]b.6. VERIFY_MATRIX (g, h ): for operands anchored to authoritative records, run Algorithm 11 on the frozen candidate; return a verified immutable handle or execute ABORT .7. FINAL_OUTPUT (g, R k ): only for a VERIFIED output opening; record the one-time use of its mask and check material ( CONSUMED ), then set RELEASED and send the opened υ = y + o (or a public y) to 

Rk with a certificate binding sid , ep , g, Rk , the output hash and the terminal status. 8. ABORT (host or internal): set the epoch state to poisoned and every open gate to ABORTED ; withhold all candidate values and pending responses; draw no further challenge; never return burned identifiers to a free list. 

Observable trace. Besides command outputs, host b ob-serves its module’s external trace: lengths, timing, host-visible memory and DMA activity (DMA requests, peer traffic, spill pages), and power, thermal and clock telemetry. The model stipulates a PPT trace simulator Strace that produces this trace from the command sequence issued by host b, μ, the public lengths and the accept or abort outcomes. Given these inputs, the trace is independent of the global MAC keys, challenges and residuals (all lanes are aggregated in data-independent time before one generic status is returned); only simulating it from these inputs costs ϵtrace . Host software cannot drive the module’s clock or voltage outside its safe operating range, or the module detects glitches and aborts (DVFS isolation). This is an implementation assumption (constant-time engines, fixed-pattern scans, data-independent spills, an isolated or monitored voltage and clock domain, telemetry filtering), not a theorem; Table VI lists it as a deployment obligation. 

Real module program and error terms. The boxes state 

R, the channels and OPEN_SESSION ideally; in the concrete module program of the real execution, R is PRF K with length-prefixed labels, the peer and client channels are AEAD over the hosts’ network, and OPEN_SESSION is attestation plus authenticated key exchange. H1 idealizes setup and channels (ϵsetup +ϵchannel ) and H2 randomness and bindings ( ϵprf +ϵbind ), so the boxed FT is the resource from H2 on (Appendix E); 

Strace costs ϵtrace (Section VII-A). Correctness of protected code, state and arithmetic is a premise of program and resource alike, not an estimated probability. 

Outside the resource. Physical attacks (probing, optical faults, power or electromagnetic measurement), microarchi-44 

tectural bugs inside the boundary, faulty trusted arithmetic, adaptive corruption and rollback without a monotonic service lie outside FT. Software-reachable undervolting and telemetry channels [69] are not treated as physical: DVFS isolation ex-cludes the former and Strace covers the latter. Under Profile T

a module may see plaintext inputs, so VIGOR-DFA relocates trust to the modules rather than removing it (Section IX); Profile U (Section III-E) is the separate backend without this resource. 

Composition within the protocol. Checked openings, epi-logues and gate subprotocols share one ledger and one set of MAC keys, so Theorem 1 is proved with a single simulator for the whole admitted execution (Appendix E), not by composing subprotocols; Appendices B to D supply the algebraic and distributional facts it uses. APPENDIX BPROOFS FOR THE EXACT DPF, DCF, AND 

AUTHENTICATION EPILOGUE 

This appendix proves the four statements about the pro-tected FSS datapath: exact DPF correctness (Theorem 2), equivalence of malicious generate-and-consume (Theorem 3), correctness of the conservative DCF (Theorem 4, together with its algorithm), and correctness and local hiding of the fresh authentication epilogue (Theorem 5). All four are exact statements. They hold for every choice of root seeds and for arbitrary deterministic functions G and H, and none of them uses pseudorandomness, a uniform-target assumption or DPF key privacy. Pseudorandomness enters the end-to-end argument only through ϵprf (Appendix E). 

Conventions. Bit strings are read most-significant bit first, as in Algorithms 1 and 2. For x ∈ { 0, 1}n we write x[i] =

x1 · · · xi for its prefix of length i, with x[0] empty. Children are indexed by L = 0 and R = 1 , so a query bit selects a child directly. For a bit τ and a string v, τ · v denotes v if 

τ = 1 and the all-zero string otherwise. This conditional XOR is bilinear over F2:

(τ · v) ⊕ (τ ′ · v) = ( τ ⊕ τ ′) · v for all bits τ, τ ′.

Where a control bit multiplies CW out , it is read as the field element 0 or 1 of Fp.

Case x̸ = α. Let i be the smallest index with xi̸ = αi. Then 

x[i−1] = α[i−1] , so Lemma 5 makes the two evaluator states identical after level i, and Lemma 6 gives y0 + y1 = 0 .The two cases exhaust all queries. No step uses a property of G, H or the roots, so the identity holds for every root pair, including adversarially chosen ones, and for every target, including non-uniform targets. 

Prerequisites. The argument depends on four details: (i) the root relation t(0) 0 = 0 , t(0) 1 = 1 and the role bit stored in each key (base case of Lemma 4); (ii) the old control bit in both the generator update and the evaluator correction (inductive steps of Lemmas 4 and 5); (iii) the correction of the selected child before the next expansion; and (iv) the signed final conversion with (−1) b and (−1) t(n)1 . A refinement that, for example, overwrote tb before computing the seed update would break the equality of Lemma 4(b) on which the target-leaf case rests; this is a conformance bug and not a scheduling freedom (Section VI-C). 

Full-domain evaluation. EvalAll computes every leaf by applying Φ along that leaf’s own root-to-leaf path; the depth-first order changes only the order of visits. Every leaf output therefore equals Eval (kb, x ) at that leaf and satisfies Theo-rem 2. A complete traversal expands each of the 2n −1 internal nodes exactly once. 

B. Malicious Generate-and-Consume 

Algorithm 3 maintains the two generator states of Algo-rithm 1, a separate evaluator state for the local role b initialized to (sb, b ), and at most the current correction word. At level 

i it computes CW (i) with the formulas above, advances both generator states, applies Φ with CW (i) and xi to the evaluator state, and only then discards CW (i). After level n it computes 

CW out , the raw share ˜yb = (−1) b H(˜ s) + ˜ t CW out  and the epilogue of Section V-F. Gates that combine several DPF evaluations, namely the prefix DPFs of Algorithm 8 and the full-domain scans of Algorithms 5 and 7, run only the (Gen) and (Eval) lines of Algorithm 3, which end with the raw share ˜yb; the ledger lines and the epilogue are executed once by the enclosing gate on its combined raw share (for PIR, only the value half of the epilogue). The proof below derives 

˜yb = Eval (kb, x ) from these lines alone, so Theorem 3 applies to each constituent DPF.

Discarding is harmless: Algorithm 2 reads CW (i) only at level i, so erasing it after its evaluator use at level i cannot influence any later step. Consequently Algorithm 3 never holds a correction-word array: at any time it stores two generator states, one evaluator state, one correction word and, at the end, one field element CW out .

Separate evaluator state. By Lemma 4(b), the generator state of root b equals party b’s evaluator state only while the query prefix agrees with the target prefix. An implementation that reused the generator state of root b as the evaluator state would compute, for every query x, the party-b output of 

Eval (kb, α ); by Theorem 2 the reconstructed value would then be β for every query, that is, a constant function rather than the point function. Keeping both generator states and a separate evaluator state for the local role is therefore a correctness requirement.

C. Conservative DCF 

Algorithm 8 is the conservative construction used by the in-terval gate (Algorithm 6). It uses the orientation DCF α,β (x) = 

β · 1[x < α ] on n-bit unsigned integers. Each prefix DPF runs Algorithms 1 and 2 on the domain {0, 1}i; in the protected profile each one is executed by the (Gen) and (Eval) lines of Algorithm 3 without its epilogue, which by Theorem 3 produce the same raw shares without materializing the keys, and Algorithm 6 applies a single epilogue to the combined raw share ˜si,b .

Proof of Theorem 4. We show that v0 + v1 = β · 1[x < α ] for every α, x ∈ { 0, 1}n, every β ∈ Fp and every choice of the n

root pairs. For i ∈ { 1, . . . , n } let 

Pi(x) = βα i · 1x[i] = α[i−1] 0

be the point function of the i-th prefix DPF evaluated at the prefix x[i]. By Theorem 2 on the depth-i domain, the two shares of that DPF sum to Pi(x), so by linearity v0 + v1 =Pni=1 Pi(x).The term Pi(x) is nonzero only if αi = 1 , xi = 0 and 

x[i−1] = α[i−1] , in which case it equals β. These three conditions state precisely that i is the first index at which x

and α differ and that xi < α i there. The first differing index of two distinct strings is unique, so at most one term is nonzero. For unsigned integers written most-significant bit first, x < α 

holds if and only if x̸ = α and x has the smaller bit at the first differing index. Hence P 

> i

Pi(x) = β if x < α and 0

otherwise. Reading the sum term by term recovers the intuition. If 

x < α with first differing index i⋆, the prefix point function 

i⋆ contributes β; every earlier prefix i < i ⋆ has xi = αi, so it either misses the target (if xi = 1 ) or hits a target whose payload βα i = 0 ; every later prefix i > i ⋆ already disagrees at position i⋆. If x = α, every prefix that matches has payload 

0. If x > α , the first differing index has xi⋆ = 1 and misses the target bit 0, and the remaining terms vanish as before. 

Cost. Prefix DPF i has depth i, so one evaluation walks Pni=1 i = n(n + 1) /2 levels per party, which is 1,891 levels for n = 61 , and generation expands two states per level, 

n(n + 1) expansions in total. With both modules generating and each evaluating, a level costs 6 AES blocks, so the two thresholds of an interval gate cost 2 · 6 · 1,891 = 22 ,692 blocks (Appendix F). This is O(n2), not the O(n) of optimized DCF constructions [9], [15]. An optimized DCF may replace Algo-rithm 8 only after its own correctness proof, its distribution and interface obligations, and its measured cost are established; the evaluation reports such a variant as a projection that Theorem 4 does not cover. 

All n keys, including zero payloads. The number of prefix DPFs, their depths and their order are fixed by n. Generating a key for every prefix, including those with αi = 0 and hence payload 0, keeps the schedule and the module’s observable trace independent of α, which is derived from a secret mask, as the trace assumption behind ϵtrace requires. Omitting zero-payload keys would reveal the Hamming weight of α through the work performed. 

Orientation. The semi-honest DFA design also uses agreater-than orientation [16]. We convert explicitly at inter-faces and never change the comparison symbol without chang-ing the function; for example, β · 1[x ≥ α] = β − β · 1[x < α ]

is obtained by letting party 0 output β − v0 and party 1 output 

−v1.

D. Fresh Authentication Epilogue 

Recall the outputs yb, γ b,j of Theorem 5; both modules draw the pads ζ, κ 1, . . . , κ t under joint labels that are distinct for every output wire. 

Proof of Theorem 5. We work in hybrid H2 (ideal randomness and bindings) of Appendix E, where draws under distinct labels are independent and uniform. 

Correctness. The pad ζ enters with opposite signs, so y0 +

y1 = ˜ y0 + ˜ y1. For each lane j,

γ0,j + γ1,j = ∆ j y0 + κj + ∆ j y1 − κj = ∆ j (y0 + y1),

so ([ y]0, [y]1) is an authenticated sharing of ˜y0 + ˜ y1 under all 

t global keys. 

Local hiding. Fix b and condition on the raw shares ˜y0, ˜y1

and on ∆1, . . . , ∆t. The pad vector (ζ, κ 1, . . . , κ t) is uniform on Ft+1  

> p

and independent of the conditioning, because its labels differ from those of the DPF roots, the payload and the keys. Consider the map 

Ψb : ( ζ, κ 1, . . . , κ t)

7→  yb, ∆1yb + ( −1) bκ1, . . . , ∆tyb + ( −1) bκt

with yb = ˜ yb + ( −1) bζ. It is a bijection of Ft+1

with inverse

Joint freshness. Let W be a set of output wires whose pads carry pairwise distinct labels. Their pad vectors are mutually independent, and the product of the maps Ψb over W is a bijection of F(t+1) |W | 

> p

. Hence the party-b tuples of all wires in W are jointly uniform and independent of all raw outputs and keys. The statement describes the tuples at creation time. Later openings of values that depend on y, such as d = y − a in a multiplication, correlate the two parties’ shares; the circuit simulation of Appendix E accounts for these correlations and does not claim that every intermediate value is independent. 

The epilogue is not an authentication oracle. If amodule applied this map to a host-supplied value vc in-stead of its internally computed ˜yc, the result would be an authenticated sharing of vc + ˜ y1−c, a value shifted by an offset of the host’s choice, whose tags satisfy the MAC relation exactly. Every later CheckOpen would accept it with probability 1, and Theorem 6 would offer no protection (scenario S2 of Table I). For this reason the interface has no authenticate(host_value) entry point and accepts only raw shares computed inside the module or produced by an explicitly verified backend. 

Role and label binding. The cancellations above require both modules to draw the same pads under the same joint label and to apply opposite signs. The joint label never contains the local role, while the roles themselves are bound at setup (Section V-C). If both modules used the same sign, y0 + y1

would equal ˜y0 + ˜ y1 ± 2ζ, a uniformly wrong value. APPENDIX CPROOFS FOR AUTHENTICATED OPENINGS AND 

ARITHMETIC 

This appendix proves the hidden-key invariant (Lemma 1), the wrong-value acceptance bound of freeze-before-challenge 

CheckOpen (Theorem 6), and the correctness of inputs, linear operations, multiplications and outputs (Theorem 7). It also records three counterexamples that delimit the statements: a single tampered lane, a challenge vector shared across lanes, and the same check over Z264 . The analysis adapts the information-theoretic MAC arguments of SPDZ [24], [35] to 

t independent lanes with per-lane challenges, residuals that never leave the modules, and a ledger that terminates the epoch on the first failure. 

Setting. As in Appendix E, host b⋆ ∈ { 0, 1} is statically corrupted, ¯b = 1 − b⋆ is the honest party, and all probabilities are taken in H2: setup and protected channels are ideal, every PRF or KDF output is an independent uniform draw per distinct label and the same draw for the same joint label, and all bindings hold. Theorem 1 charges the distance between the real execution and H2 to ϵsetup , ϵchannel , ϵprf and ϵbind ; the exact replacements of H2 (Theorem 3 and Lemmas 2 and 3) cost nothing. Traces are still produced by the modules in H2

and are treated in (vi) below. The key shares ∆0,j , ∆1,j are independent and uniform for j = 1 , . . . , t ; host b⋆ receives 

∆b⋆ = (∆ b⋆,1, . . . , ∆b⋆,t ) at setup, and we write 

∆¯b = (∆ ¯b, 1, . . . , ∆¯b,t ) ∈ Ftp

for the honest key shares. Since ∆j = ∆ b⋆,j + ∆ ¯b,j and host 

b⋆ knows ∆b⋆,j , uniformity of ∆¯b given the corrupt view is equivalent to uniformity of (∆ 1, . . . , ∆t) given that view. The view V of host b⋆ contains its random coins, ∆b⋆ , everything its module, the peer host and the clients send to it, and its observable trace. 

Faithful tuples and errors. For every wire ν, the faith-ful tuple (ˆ xb⋆,ν , ˆγb⋆,1,ν , . . . , ˆγb⋆,t,ν ) is the tuple that host b⋆

would hold if it had applied every prescribed local operation (Fig. 9) to the module outputs and the public openings that it actually received. The honest host always holds the prescribed tuple (x¯b,ν , γ ¯b, 1,ν , . . . , γ ¯b,t,ν ), and the faithful value of ν is 

ˆxν = x¯b,ν + ˆ xb⋆,ν . Faithful tuples are produced by an honest execution, so by the correctness part of Theorem 7 and by Theorem 5 they satisfy the MAC relation 

γ¯b,j,ν + ˆ γb⋆,j,ν = ∆ j ˆxν (j = 1 , . . . , t ),

xb⋆,i = ˆ xb⋆,i + ei, γb⋆,j,i = ˆ γb⋆,j,i + dj,i .

The value-error vector e = ( e1, . . . , e m) ∈ Fmp and the tag-error vectors dj = ( dj, 1, . . . , d j,m ) ∈ Fmp are well defined because the canonical parser accepts only vectors of field elements; any other deviation is rejected before freezing. Submitting the tuple of another wire, permuting positions or replaying an old tuple are all captured by suitable (e, dj ). Both are functions of V at freezing time, since the faithful tuple is computed from V and the submission is chosen from V . A check is a wrong-value opening if e̸ = 0.

A. Hidden-Key Invariant 

Let τ be the first time at which challenges are drawn for a wrong-value opening ( τ = ∞ if there is none). Every check 48 

resolved before τ has e = 0. An epoch abort draws no further challenge (Section V-D), and in the admitted execution of Theorem 1 a failed check aborts the execution. 

Proof of Lemma 1. We prove that, in H2, ∆¯b is uniform on 

Ftp and independent of the corrupt view Vs at every time 

s ≤ τ , and that the challenges drawn at τ are uniform and independent of (Vτ , ∆¯b). If the check at τ is rejected, the epoch is poisoned and no challenge is drawn afterwards, so the information carried by that rejection never reaches another check; if it is accepted, it is the first accepted wrong-value opening, where the lemma stops. 

Strategy. We show that Vτ can be generated from ∆b⋆ , the adversary’s coins, the value layer and fresh uniform variables, all independent of ∆¯b. The value layer consists of the honest inputs, all value shares, masks, triple components, DPF roots and targets, raw FSS outputs, value pads and opened values; no rule of the protocol uses a key when it computes a value share (Fig. 9, Algorithm 3), so the value layer is a function of randomness drawn under labels distinct from the key labels. We go through every kind of observation that can enter Vs for 

s ≤ τ .

(i) Setup. Host b⋆ obtains ∆b⋆ and a public transcript, which in H2 is simulated from public data. ∆¯b is drawn independently of ∆b⋆ .

(ii) Value-layer observations. Value shares delivered by its module (AuthShare values vb⋆ , epilogue values yb⋆ ), complete input masks r revealed to a corrupt owner, output masks revealed to a corrupt recipient, accepted opened val-ues, validation outcomes ( status ), certificates and all public metadata are functions of the value layer and of public data. Certificates bind session identifiers, public metadata, output hashes and status; they involve no MAC key. 

(iii) Tags. Every tag that host b⋆ receives from its module has one of three forms: the pad π itself ( AuthShare with 

b⋆ = 0 , where γ0,j = π), ∆j v − π (AuthShare with b⋆ = 1 ), or ∆j yb⋆ + ( −1) b⋆

κj (epilogue). In each case the pad π or κj

is drawn under its own label and occurs in exactly two places: this corrupt tag and the corresponding honest tag. The honest tag, and every tag the honest host later derives from it by local linear operations, is used only inside honest residuals. Complete tags are never delivered to owners or recipients (Fig. 9), and by Lemma 7 the sum of residuals, the only quantity that influences a check outcome, does not depend on any honest tag. Hence, conditioned on everything else, including ∆¯b, the value layer and all other pads, each corrupt tag is the image of a uniform pad under a bijection of Fp

and is therefore uniform, and we replace it by a fresh uniform variable without changing the joint distribution of (Vτ , ∆¯b),one tag at a time. 

(iv) Local operations of host b⋆. Additions, public scalings and public-constant updates produce deterministic functions of 

Vs and ∆b⋆ and add no new information. 

(v) Check outcomes before τ . Such a check has e = 0, so by Lemma 7 it accepts if and only if ⟨ρj , dj ⟩ = 0 for every 

j. The tag errors dj are functions of Vs, and the challenges are fresh uniform draws, so the outcome is a function of Vs

and of randomness independent of ∆¯b. Algorithm 4 returns one generic status for all lanes, and challenges and residuals remain inside the modules and on the protected channel. A rejected tag-only check poisons the epoch, after which no challenge is drawn. 

(vi) Traffic and trace. Protected-channel traffic reveals only lengths and times, which are fixed by μ and the schedule. For the trace (lengths, timing, host-visible memory and DMA activity, power, thermal and clock telemetry) we use the exact key-independence clause of the trace assumption (Sec-tion VII-A and Appendix A-C): given the command sequence issued by the host, μ, the public lengths and the accept or abort outcomes, the trace is independent of the global MAC keys, challenges and residuals, since all lanes are computed and aggregated in data-independent time before one generic status is returned and host software cannot drive the module’s clock or voltage outside its safe operating range. These conditioning data arise in (i) to (v) without ∆¯b, so the trace adds no information on ∆¯b. This step is exact; only simulating the trace from public data costs ϵtrace , charged between H3 and 

H4 in Theorem 1. After the replacements of (iii), every observation in Vτ is a function of ∆b⋆ , the adversary’s coins, the value layer, the check outcomes of (v) and fresh uniform variables, none of which depends on ∆¯b, and the trace is conditionally independent of ∆¯b by (vi). The argument is sequential: each new observation is generated from the preceding view, which already determines the adversary’s next submission, and from fresh randomness independent of ∆¯b. By induction on s, the view Vs is independent of ∆¯b for every s ≤ τ , and ∆¯b is uniform given Vs. The challenges drawn at τ carry labels bound to the two frozen roots and the check identifier, which were never used before, so they are uniform and independent of (Vτ , ∆¯b).

Step 4: Q batches. Number the at most Q checked batches of the execution by the time their challenges are drawn, and let Bk be the event that batch k has e̸ = 0, is accepted, and no earlier batch with e̸ = 0 was accepted. An incorrect value can be consumed or released only after an accepted check on its frozen bytes: the ledger admits consumption and FINAL_OUTPUT only in state VERIFIED , consumption rereads the frozen bytes, and a certificate cannot be transferred to another transcript. Tag-only errors. Theorem 6 bounds wrong values ; it is not a claim that every tag modification is detected with probability 

1 − (2 /p )t. If e = 0, d1̸ = 0 and d2 = · · · = dt = 0,then v1 = d1, v2 = · · · = vt = 0, and the check accepts with probability exactly 1/p . Such an acceptance releases the correct value, so it is not a correctness failure. A surviving tag error can reappear, after local linear operations, in the tag errors of later checks, where, being independent of the keys, it again leaks nothing about them (item (v) in the proof of Lemma 1). 

Remark (shared-challenge cancellation). The product in Step 3 requires independent challenge vectors per lane. Sup-pose instead that one vector ρ were reused, ρ1 = · · · = ρt =

ρ. An adversary submits any e̸ = 0 and leaves all tags faithful, 

d1 = · · · = dt = 0. Then the complete residual of lane j

is −∆j ⟨ρ, e⟩, and all t lanes vanish simultaneously whenever 

⟨ρ, e⟩ = 0 , for every value of the keys. Since ⟨ρ, e⟩ is uniform for e̸ = 0, the wrong value is accepted with probability at least 

1/p ≈ 2−61 , whatever t is. The t lanes then give essentially the guarantee of a single lane: for Q = 2 32 the union bound becomes about Q/p ≈ 2−29 instead of 2−148 , because the per-lane events share the single linear form ⟨ρ, e⟩ and are no longer independent. VIGOR-DFA therefore derives ρj,i 

under distinct labels for every lane j and position i, and a configuration that reuses one challenge vector across lanes is rejected (row 8 of Table X). 

Remark (why the challenge must follow the freeze). 

Step 1 uses that e and dj are fixed before the challenges exist and that the host never sees them. If the host learned 

ρ1, . . . , ρt before its bytes were fixed, because the challenges were derived before the freeze, exposed to the host, or applied to a buffer that the host could still modify, it could pick a nonzero e in the common kernel {e : ⟨ρj , e⟩ = 0 for all j},which has dimension at least m − t ≥ 1 when m > t , and keep dj = 0. Every residual would then vanish for every key, and the wrong value would be accepted with probability 1.Likewise, if a module verified one buffer and later consumed another, the consumed value could carry an arbitrary error that no check ever sees. Algorithm 4 excludes both attacks: challenges are derived only after both modules have frozen and exchanged their roots, they never leave the modules, and consumption rereads only the frozen authenticated bytes (row 6 of Table X). 

Remark (the proof does not apply over Z264 ). Step 2 divides by ei⋆ and uses that a nonzero linear form over a field is uniform. Both steps fail in the ring Z264 , because 

263 is a zero divisor. Consider the same check with keys and challenges uniform in Z264 , a batch whose only nonzero error is e1 = 2 63 (the opened value has its most significant bit flipped), and faithful tags, dj = 0. For an even key, 

∆j e1 = (∆ j /2) · 264 ≡ 0 (mod 2 64 ), so vj = 0 and lane 

j accepts for every challenge; this single event already has probability 1/2, whereas the field argument allows at most 

1/p . For an odd key, vj, 1 = −∆j 263 ≡ 263 and the residual 

ρj, 1263 vanishes for every even challenge, with probability 1/2

instead of 1/p . Each lane therefore accepts with probability 

1/2 +1 /4 = 3 /4, and with t = 3 independent lanes the wrong value is accepted with probability (3 /4) 3 = 27 /64 > 0.42 .Ring arithmetic needs a SPDZ2k-style representation over a larger ring with its own analysis [27] (Section V-B).

with x0 + x1 = x and γx

= ∆ j x.

Proof of Theorem 7. Part 1: exact operation with correct modules and honest messages. Each item verifies both the value equation and the tag equation of the output. 

Input ( ΠInput ). For a fresh authenticated mask [r] and the bound public difference ε = x−r, the input tuple is  rℓ+1[ℓ =0] ε, γ rℓ,j + ∆ ℓ,j ε. Its value shares sum to r + ε = x and its tags to ∆j r + (∆ 0,j + ∆ 1,j )ε = ∆ j (r + ε) = ∆ j x.

Linear operations ( ΠLin ). For [x]+[ y] both parties add com-ponentwise, so the sums are x+y and ∆j x+∆ j y = ∆ j (x+y).For a public scalar η both parties multiply componentwise, giving ηx and ∆j ηx . To add a public constant c0, party 0 adds 

c0 to its value and each party ℓ adds ∆ℓ,j c0 to its tag, giving 

x + c0 and ∆j x + ∆ j c0. Tensors are handled elementwise. 50 

Scalar multiplication ( ΠMult ). Let  [a], [b], [c] be a fresh triple with c = ab , and let d = x − a and e = y − b be the values opened through Algorithm 4. Party ℓ sets 

zℓ = cℓ + d b ℓ + e a ℓ + 1[ℓ = 0] de, γzℓ,j = γcℓ,j + d γ bℓ,j + e γ aℓ,j + ∆ ℓ,j de. 

The value shares sum to 

c+db +ea +de = ab +( x−a)b+( y−b)a+( x−a)( y−b) = xy, 

and the tags sum to ∆j c + d∆j b + e∆j a + ∆ j de = ∆ j (c +

db + ea + de ) = ∆ j xy .

Matrix multiplication. For X ∈ Fm×kp , Y ∈ Fk×np and a fresh matrix triple (A, B, C ) with C = AB and elementwise tag matrices ΓAℓ,j , ΓBℓ,j , ΓCℓ,j , let D = X − A and E = Y − B

be opened. Party ℓ sets Zℓ = Cℓ + DB ℓ + AℓE + 1[ℓ = 0] DE 

and ΓZℓ,j = Γ Cℓ,j + DΓBℓ,j + Γ Aℓ,j E + ∆ ℓ,j DE . Keeping every factor on its side, 

C + DB + AE + DE = AB + ( X − A)B + A(Y − B)+ ( X − A)( Y − B) = XY, 

and, because the scalar ∆j commutes with matrices, the tag matrices sum to ∆j (C + DB + AE + DE ) = ∆j XY .Replacing AE by EA is wrong in general, since matrix products do not commute, and ill-typed for nonsquare shapes. 

Output ( ΠOut ). For a private output y to recipient O, a fresh authenticated mask [o] is generated, the complete o is revealed only to O, and υ = y + o is opened through Algorithm 4; the recipient computes υ − o = y. Because o is fresh and uniform, υ is uniform and independent of y for every party that does not hold o; for a corrupt recipient, who receives o

early, the simulator programs υ = y + o after Fabort  

> C,μ

releases 

y (Appendix E). The recipient never receives both parties’ complete tags: knowing ∆j υ for a known nonzero υ would reveal ∆j = (∆ j υ)υ−1 and void Lemma 1 for anyone who later obtained these values. Public outputs are opened directly through Algorithm 4. 

Triple correctness is not a MAC property. If a triple had c′ =

ab + δ with δ̸ = 0 and tags computed consistently on c′, the multiplication would output a correctly authenticated sharing of xy + δ, and no check would ever detect it. The theorem therefore relies on the modules computing c = ab inside the trusted boundary ( AuthShare in Section V-C); a replacement preprocessing service must supply a proof of triple correctness in addition to authenticated shares (row 4 of Table X). 

Part 2: malicious hosts. Let Gopen be the event that no wrong-value opening is ever accepted and Gbind the event that every binding holds. By Theorem 6, Pr[ ¬Gopen ] ≤ Q(2 /p )t,and ¬Gbind is charged to ϵbind in Theorem 1. We show that on 

Gopen ∩Gbind every execution either aborts, or releases exactly 

C(x⋆), where x⋆ consists of the honest inputs and the inputs extracted from corrupt owners. We proceed by induction over the gates of μ in manifest order, which the ledger enforces, and maintain three invariants for all gates processed so far without abort: (I1) every value opened, consumed or released equals its faithful value; (I2) the faithful value of every circuit wire equals the value of that wire in C on x⋆;(I3) every preprocessing item that was consumed was issued once, for this gate and this role, and consumed once. (I1) holds on Gopen : every opening is a check that reconstructs 

zi = ˆxi + ei from frozen bytes, where ˆxi is the faithful value, accepted checks have e = 0, and consumption rereads the frozen bytes. (I3) holds on Gbind : RESERVE_PREP burns identifiers before issuing material, CONSUMED records one-time use, and any reuse, role swap, cross-gate consumption or post-challenge replacement is an invalid ledger transition that aborts. It remains to show (I2) gate by gate. 

Input gates. For an honest owner, the authenticated ε = x−r

cannot be altered by the corrupt host, and both modules bind the same ε or abort; the faithful value is the honest input by Part 1. For a corrupt owner, whatever canonical ε⋆ both modules bind defines the extracted input x⋆ = r + ε⋆; a different ε sent to the two modules causes abort. Canonical encoding alone does not place x⋆ in the declared interface, so the proof uses the validation step of ΠInput (Fig. 9): after binding, both modules compute x⋆, which they can because they generated r, and abort unless x⋆ ∈ In ν (Appendix A-A); at the public boundaries declared in μ they evaluate every predicate of V on the bound inputs and abort if one fails. The public schedule of μ places every input gate before every validity check, and both before the first output opening, so no output precedes such an abort. These outcomes are exactly the vector status of Fabort  

> C,μ

, which is part of LC (Definition 1), and the simulator emulates the same abort (Appendix E). A commitment-bound input is the value decoded by the protected loader, or abort. Hence the execution continues exactly on the inputs that Fabort  

> C,μ

accepts. 

Linear gates. Faithful tuples are updated by the same local rules, so Part 1 transfers (I2) from the inputs of the gate to its output. Deviations of the corrupt host at such a gate change only its stored tuples, which appear as errors (e, dj ) in a later check. 

Multiplication gates. By (I3) the triple is fresh and bound to the gate, and by the premise on FT it satisfies c = ab . By (I1) the opened values are d = ˆ x − a and e = ˆ y − b, where 

ˆx and ˆy are the faithful values of the two input wires. Part 1 then shows that the faithful output value equals ˆxˆy, which by (I2) for the inputs is the value of the gate in C. The matrix case is identical with the order of factors preserved. 

Nonlinear and FSS gates. Their masked openings are checks, so (I1) applies. Their outputs are fresh epilogue tuples whose sum is the gate value by Theorem 5 and Theorems 8 to 11 (Appendix D). A direct protected gate (Section V-J) recomputes x = c − r from its verified opening inside the modules and shares the exact gate value with AuthShare , so (I2) holds by the premise on FT.

Output gates. By (I1) the opened value is υ = ˆ y + o with ˆy

the faithful output value, and the recipient computes ˆy, which equals the corresponding output of C(x⋆) by (I2). The release guard admits FINAL_OUTPUT only after VERIFIED , and the certificate binds the session, epoch, gate and recipient, so a released value cannot be presented as the output of a different gate or transcript. The corrupt host can further refuse to submit, delay, or 51 

submit malformed frames; each of these causes an abort, which Fabort  

> C,μ

permits because it provides neither fairness nor availability. Hence, conditioned on Gopen ∩ Gbind , malicious behavior either selects an allowed input, preserves the com-putation, or causes abort. APPENDIX DPROOFS FOR EXACT GATES , R EFACTORING , AND MATRIX 

CHECKS 

This appendix gives the complete fallback algorithms that Section V-I only summarizes (Algorithms 9 and 10), the frozen-operand matrix check of Section V-L (Algorithm 11), and full proofs of Theorems 8 to 12 and Lemmas 2 and 3. The proofs follow the structure used for masked FSS gates in prior work [10], [11]: an exact algebraic identity on the masked value, followed by an argument that the opened quantities are one-time pads. The authenticated layer is new: every identity must hold over Fp with authenticated sharings, and every secret product goes through the checked multiplication of Fig. 9. 

Conventions. Throughout, p = 2 n −1 is a Mersenne prime; the deployed field uses n = 61 (Section V-B). We identify every v ∈ Fp with its canonical representative in {0, . . . , p −

1} and write bits (v) = ( v0, . . . , v n−1) for its n-bit binary expansion, least-significant bit first . This is the opposite of the most-significant-first path convention of the DPF algorithms (Algorithms 1 and 2); the two conventions never meet inside one algorithm. Because p < 2n, the all-ones string is never a canonical encoding; the parser rejects such words instead of reducing them. An authenticated bit [β] is an authenticated field element whose value is promised to lie in {0, 1}. For secret bits β, β ′ and a public bit c we use 

β ⊕ β′ = β + β′ − 2ββ ′, ¬β = 1 − β, c ⊕ β = c + (1 − 2c)β. 

The last identity is affine in the secret bit and is therefore a local operation under the linear rules of Fig. 9; only products of two secret values consume a triple. We write ΠMult for the authenticated multiplication of Fig. 9 and CheckOpen for the batched opening of Algorithm 4. 

Scope of the statements. Every correctness statement be-low is conditioned, exactly as Theorem 7, on the event that no incorrect value is accepted by CheckOpen . The unconditional guarantee, which charges that event at most Q(2 /p )t, is part of Theorem 1 (hybrid H3 of Appendix E). Privacy of each opened value is stated here as a one-time-pad property; the simulator of Appendix E uses exactly these properties. The executed CPU reference model (Section VIII-F) tests toy instances of Theorems 8 to 11 and the truncation-fiber sampler; such finite tests catch transcription errors but are neither a machine-checked proof nor evidence for the statistical bounds, which follow from the analysis alone.

Step 4: authentication. Each line of Algorithm 9 is either an affine map with public coefficients (the ci are public after the opening) applied to authenticated values, which the local linear rules of Fig. 9 preserve, or a call to ΠMult . By Theorem 7, conditioned on no accepted wrong opening, every output is an authenticated sharing of the value computed in Steps 1– 3. 

Cost and privacy. Algorithm 9 uses 2n authenticated multiplications (a conservative count, since the first product of Loop 1 multiplies by a public zero), one masked opening, and 2n + 1 dependent rounds, i.e., 123 rounds for n = 61 .Independent instances share rounds by being placed in the same CheckOpen batch; batching amortizes metadata and challenge derivation but cannot shorten the two carry chains. For privacy, c = x + r with r uniform on Fp and independent of everything the corrupt host holds (its share tuples of [r]

and [ri] are uniform and independent of r by construction of 

AuthShare ), so c is uniform and independent of x. The mul-tiplications open only Beaver differences under fresh triples.

For k = 0 both prefixes are empty, so eq = 1 and lt = 0 . For the step, let i = n−k−1 and mi = eq ·xi. The new prefixes are equal iff the old ones were equal and xi = θi; this is eq xi =

After k = n iterations lt = 1[x < θ ] for every θ ∈ [0 , 2n),which Cmp returns; the flag eq = 1[x = θ] stays internal and is not an output. The endpoint thresholds give public constants for lt : no x satisfies x < 0 and every canonical x satisfies 

x < 2n. All products are of bits, so Theorem 7 preserves authentication; the comparator uses at most n authenticated multiplications. 

Sign. The centered encoding maps ¯z ∈ [0 , (p − 1) /2] to itself and ¯z ∈ [−(p − 1) /2, −1] to p + ¯ z ∈ [( p + 1) /2, p − 1] .Hence the canonical z satisfies z ≥ (p + 1) /2 iff ¯z < 0,and s = 1 − 1[z < (p + 1) /2] = 1[¯ z < 0] . The threshold 

(p + 1) /2 = 2 n−1 lies in [0 , 2n), so the comparator applies to the canonical bits returned by Theorem 8. 

Magnitude. In Fp, u = (1 − 2s)z equals z = ¯ z when s = 0 

and −z = p−z = −¯z when s = 1 . In both cases the canonical representative of u is |¯z| ∈ [0 , (p − 1) /2] , so no modular wrap occurs. By Theorem 8, (ui) are the canonical bits of |¯z|.

Shift and sign restoration. The local sum q = Pn−1 

> i=f

ui2i−f53 

equals ⌊| ¯z|/2f ⌋ as an integer, and q < 2n−f ≤ p, so it is also the canonical field element. Finally (1 − 2s)q equals q for 

¯z ≥ 0 and p − q, the encoding of −q, for ¯z < 0. Therefore the output encodes sgn(¯ z)⌊| ¯z|/2f ⌋, which is truncation toward zero; for ¯z = 0 it is 0. Every step is local affine or ΠMult , so Theorems 7 and 8 give authenticated outputs conditioned on no accepted wrong opening. 

Remarks. (i) For the particular threshold 2n−1 all lower threshold bits are zero, so lt can change only in the first iter-ation and the comparator output equals 1 − xn−1; the equality products after that iteration do not affect the result. Our cost accounting nevertheless charges the generic comparator, giving 

2n + n + 1 + 2 n + 1 = 5 n + 2 authenticated multiplications per exact truncation ( 307 for n = 61 ), all on sequential carry chains. With the two masked openings of the decompositions they occupy 5n + 4 = 309 sequential rounds (30.9 ms on LAN); this is why the compiler treats Algorithm 10 as a fallback (Section V-I). (ii) A fixed-point multiplication first computes the integer product and then applies Trunc . The declared magnitude must remain below p/ 2 before truncation; otherwise the modular product has already lost the intended signed integer. The manifest’s numeric contract states these bounds, and the proof above assumes them rather than en-forcing them.

Joint preparation. The identity uses that the authenticated bits and the DPF target are the same r. If the target were resampled as an independent uniform r′, each object would still be marginally uniform, but the output would become 

T [r′ ⊕x⊕r], which is not T [x]. The sampler therefore derives both from one logical draw, as required by Lemma 2. 

Mask privacy. For every fixed x, the map r 7 → x ⊕ r is a bijection on {0, 1}w, so u is uniform when r is. The only other material related to r that leaves the protected boundary is the corrupt party’s share tuples of [ri], which AuthShare 

samples uniformly and independently of r; the DPF keys and raw evaluations never leave the modules in Profile T. Hence u

is uniform and independent of x jointly with the corrupt party’s view, and the Beaver openings inside the w XOR products are uniform because the triples are fresh. The exported output is an epilogue tuple, which Theorem 5 shows to be uniform for either fixed party. 

D. Shifted Intervals and Piecewise Polynomials Proof of Theorem 11. Let [a, b ) ⊆ [0 , p ) be proper and nonempty, so δ := b − a ∈ [1 , p − 1] . Algorithm 6 opens 

c = ( x + r) mod p for a fresh uniform r ∈ Fp and, inside the protected sampler, sets L = ( a+r) mod p, H = ( b+r) mod p

and ω = 1[L > H], generates two conservative DCFs (Algorithm 8) with payload 1 for the thresholds L and H,both derived from the same r, and a sharing ω0 + ω1 = ω.Module T0 hands ˜s0 = ω0 + DCF H (c)0 − DCF L(c)0 to the epilogue, and T1 likewise ˜s1 (here a, b are endpoints, not party indices). 

Step 1: translation. Since c − L ≡ x − a and H − L ≡ δ

(mod p), we claim 

a ≤ x < b ⇐⇒ (c − L) mod p < (H − L) mod p = δ. 

If a ≤ x < b then (x − a) mod p = x − a < δ . If x < a then 

(x − a) mod p = p + x − a ≥ p − a ≥ b − a = δ because 

b ≤ p. If x ≥ b then x − a ≥ δ. Thus x ∈ [a, b ) iff c lies in the circular half-open interval that starts at L and has length 

δ. Moreover L̸ = H because δ / ∈ { 0, p }.

Step 2: two cases. If L < H , the circular interval is the ordinary interval [L, H ): for c < L we get (c − L) mod p =

p+c−L ≥ p−L > H −L, and for c ≥ H we get c−L ≥ H −

L. Its indicator is 1[c < H ] − 1[c < L ], which is a bit because 

L < H . If L > H , the circular interval is [L, p ) ∪ [0 , H ), a disjoint union, and its indicator is 1[c ≥ L] + 1[c < H ] = 1 + 1[c < H ] − 1[c < L ]. With ω = 1[L > H ] both cases read 

1[a ≤ x < b ] = ω + 1[c < H ] − 1[c < L ].

Step 3: shares. All of c, L, H lie in [0 , p ) ⊂ [0 , 2n), so they are valid inputs and thresholds for the n-bit DCF with the “ <”orientation. By Theorem 4, DCF H (c)0 + DCF H (c)1 = 1[c < H] and likewise for L. Summing the two raw shares gives 

˜s0 + ˜ s1 = ω + 1[c < H ] − 1[c < L ] = 1[a ≤ x < b ], and Theorem 5 yields an authenticated sharing of the indicator. Empty and full intervals are the public constants 0 and 1 and need no keys. The derivation uses one opening c for both thresholds; deriving L and H from independent masks would require two different openings and would break Step 1. 

Step 4: partitions and polynomials. Let 0 = τ0 < τ 1 <

· · · < τ m = p with m ≥ 2 define the disjoint, exhaustive partition of Algorithm 6, so every piece [τi, τ i+1 ) is proper and nonempty, and let all endpoints, wrap bits and auxiliary shares be derived jointly from one mask r and one opening c, with independent function-generation coins per DCF. By Steps 1–3 each indicator si = 1[τi ≤ x < τ i+1 ] is correct, and exactly one of them, si⋆ , equals 1. The selected coefficients [¯ aj ] = 54

Privacy. As in Theorem 8, c is a one-time pad of x over

E. Distribution-Preserving Refactoring 

The semi-honest DFA design samples a DPF target directly and recovers the mask through an inverse map [16]. Lemmas 2 and 3 state when this preserves the joint distribution with every auxiliary object derived from the mask; marginal uniformity of each object alone is not sufficient. The main proof applies them in hybrid H2 of Appendix E, only where these hypotheses hold. 

Proof of Lemma 2. Fix the already determined context Z = z.Let R be uniform on the finite set R, let gz : R → A be a bijection, let W be coins independent of R, and let J be the deterministic procedure that produces all auxiliary material. Experiment 1 outputs (R, g z (R), J (z, R, W )) ; Experiment 2 samples A uniformly on A, sets R′ = g−1 

> z

(A) and outputs 

(R′, A, J (z, R ′, W )) . Write π(ˆ ȷ | ˆr) = Pr W [J(z, ˆr, W ) = ˆ ȷ]

for the auxiliary sampler. For every (ˆ r, ˆa, ˆȷ),

Pr 1[(ˆ r, ˆa, ˆȷ)] = |R| −1 1[ˆ a = gz (ˆ r)] π(ˆ ȷ | ˆr),

Pr 2[(ˆ r, ˆa, ˆȷ)] = |A| −1 1[ˆ r = g−1 

> z

(ˆ a)] π(ˆ ȷ | ˆr).

The indicators coincide because gz is a bijection, which also gives |R| = |A| ; the conditional law of the auxiliary material given the mask is the same sampler in both experiments. Hence the two joint distributions are equal for every z, and averaging over the law of Z preserves equality. The hypothesis that R

is uniform given Z = z is essential: a mask correlated with the context would make the two sides differ. 

Joint equality. Suppose a gate needs two DPF targets α1 =

r and α2 = r + 1 derived from one mask. Resampling α1 and 

α2 as two independent uniforms keeps both marginals uniform but destroys the relation α2 = α1 + 1 , so the gate computes a different function. Lemma 2 permits instead one uniform draw A, the recovery R′ = g−1 

> z

(A), and the derivation of the second target from R′ inside J. The same reasoning covers the LUT and interval gates above, where the authenticated bits, the DPF or DCF targets, the wrap bit and all auxiliary shares are functions of one logical draw. 

Proof of Lemma 3. Let g be arbitrary and let R have any distribution on a finite set. Sample A from the law of g(R)

and then R′ from the conditional law of R given g(R) = A,which is well defined on the support of g(R). For every (ˆ r, ˆa),

Pr[ R′ = ˆ r, A = ˆ a] = Pr[ g(R) = ˆ a] Pr[ R = ˆ r | g(R) = ˆ a]= Pr[ R = ˆ r, g (R) = ˆ a].

Thus (R′, A ) has the law of (R, g (R)) . Every auxiliary object is subsequently derived from R′ with independent coins, so the argument of Lemma 2 extends the equality to the full joint distribution. 

Truncation sampler. For an unsigned right shift of a uni-form ℓ-bit mask by h bits, g(R) = ⌊R/ 2h⌋. Here A is uniform on ℓ−h bits and the fiber of each A is {2hA+U : U ∈ [0 , 2h)},so the conditional sampler is R′ = 2 hA+U with U uniform on 

h bits. Every mask then has probability 2−(ℓ−h)2−h = 2 −ℓ, as required. The sampler is specific to this case. For example, if R

is uniform on Fp with p = 2 61 − 1, the top value of ⌊R/ 2h⌋

has only 2h − 1 preimages while every other value has 2h,so A is not uniform and must be sampled with probabilities proportional to fiber sizes. Signed shifts, nonuniform masks and other moduli likewise need their own conditional sampler; Lemma 3 states what that sampler must satisfy, not that a generic one exists. 

F. Frozen-Operand Freivalds Check 

Algorithm 11 is the optional verified matrix subroutine of Section V-L: Freivalds’ randomized product check [60] run inside the protected verifier on a frozen candidate and on operands bound to their ledger records. In this subsection 

m, k, n denote matrix dimensions. The subroutine applies only where exposing the operands to that GPU is already allowed; it never authorizes exposing global secret triple masks. 

Proof of Theorem 12. Let E = C − AB , where C is the frozen candidate and A, B are the bound operands, and suppose E̸ = 0 . Because ρC is fixed before any vℓ is derived and the challenge label binds ρC , the matrix E is determined 55 

before the challenges, which are secret, uniform, and inde-pendent of E (with ideal randomness, i.e., from hybrid H2 of Appendix E on). A repetition accepts iff Cvℓ = A(Bvℓ), i.e., 

Evℓ = 0. Pick a nonzero row e of E and an index j with 

ej̸ = 0 . The event Evℓ = 0 implies ⟨e, vℓ⟩ = 0 . Conditioned on all coordinates of vℓ except vℓ,j , this linear equation has exactly one solution vℓ,j = −e−1

> j

P 

> i̸=j

eivℓ,i , which the uniform vℓ,j hits with probability exactly 1/p . Hence each repetition accepts with probability at most 1/p , and since the 

s challenge vectors are independent while E is fixed, all s

repetitions accept with probability at most p−s.The two side conditions are necessary. Without freezing, a host that observes vℓ could choose a candidate with E̸ = 0 

and Evℓ = 0 after the fact. Without operand binding, the module could verify one product and later consume a different one; the ledger check on the operand digests and the rule that consumers read only the frozen C exclude both. A check that covers only the value matrix does not authenticate a different tag matrix, hence the per-lane repetition in Algorithm 11. A union bound over at most QF invocations bounds the probability that any wrong product is accepted by QF /p s; for 

s = 3 this is QF · p−3 < Q F · 2−182 , which Theorem 1 adds to its error term (in H3) when the subroutine is enabled. 

Cost. One repetition computes g = Bv, g′ = Ag and Cv,i.e., kn + mk + mn field multiply-accumulates, and reads all three bound matrices once, compared with mkn operations for recomputing the product. For small matrices the reads dominate and the check may cost more than direct protected computation, which is why the compiler treats it as an option to be selected by modeled cost rather than as a default; it is disabled in all evaluated configurations. This appendix proves Theorem 13 for both corruption regimes and then Theorem 1 by an explicit simulator and a sequence of hybrids H0, . . . , H4. We use the real/ideal paradigm of Definition 2 [52]–[55] with Fabort  

> C,μ

and Fabort PIR 

(Appendices A-A and A-B), the leakage LC and LPIR (Defi-nition 1) and the ideal module resource FT (Appendix A-C). 

Notation. The adversary A with auxiliary input z statically corrupts the host of party Pb⋆ for some b⋆ ∈ {0, 1} (no corruption is the special case with only public traffic), and 

¯b = 1 − b⋆ is the honest party. As in Definition 2, μ,⃗x and z are fixed up front, and a PPT distinguisher D

receives the joint output (the view of A and the honest outputs). For a wire ν of the manifest’s circuit, xν is its value in C on the honest and the extracted inputs, and 

ϕν = (ˆ xb⋆,ν , ˆγb⋆,ν, 1, . . . , ˆγb⋆,ν,t ) ∈ F1+ tp is its faithful tuple 

(Appendix C): the tuple that host b⋆ would hold had it applied every prescribed local operation to what it actually received. The error terms ϵsetup , ϵ prf , ϵ channel , ϵ bind , ϵ trace are those of Section VII-A and refer to the entire admitted execution. We use one elementary fact repeatedly, the one-time-pad argument behind every masked opening. 

Lemma 8 (One-time-pad openings) . Let G be a finite abelian group ( Fp or {0, 1}w under ⊕). Let (V, S ) be arbitrary jointly distributed random variables with S ∈ G, let M be uniform on G and independent of (V, S ), and let Y be independent of 

(V, S, M ). Then (V, Y, S +M ) is distributed as (V, Y, U ) with 

U uniform on G and independent of (V, Y, S ), and conditioned on (V, Y, S, U = u) the mask equals M = u − S.Proof. For all (v, y, σ, u ), Pr[ V = v, Y = y, S = σ, S +

M = u] = Pr[ V = v, S = σ] Pr[ Y = y] Pr[ M = u −

σ] = Pr[ V = v, S = σ] Pr[ Y = y]/|G|, which is the claimed product distribution; the second statement is immediate. In our uses, Y is the corrupt party’s share tuple of the authenticated mask [M ]. AuthShare samples party 0’s value and tag shares uniformly and independently and sets party 1’s shares to the differences, so either party’s tuple is uniform on 

F1+ tp and independent of M and of the global keys; this is the hypothesis “ Y independent of M ”.

Proof of Theorem 13. We use the two regimes of Ap-pendix A-B, which are not combined: in (R1), a malicious server host and an honest client, we prove integrity and index privacy; in (R2), a malicious client only and two honest hosts, we prove database privacy. Throughout, let Good be the event that setup, protected channels and all bindings (query bindings, snapshot digest, certificates) behave ideally; 

Pr[ ¬Good ] ≤ ϵsetup + ϵchannel + ϵbind .

(R1) integrity. Let host b⋆ be corrupt and the client honest, and condition on Good .1) One legal index. The client sends (α, qid , desc v ) to both modules over authenticated confidential channels. Host b⋆

relays these messages but can only drop or delay them. The modules compare the two bindings over the protected channel and reject unless they agree and α < N . Hence, if processing continues, both modules hold the same legal 

α and the same descriptor. 2) Consistent keys. Both modules derive the DPF for (α, 1) 

under the same joint label, so they hold the two halves of 56 

one key pair. By Theorem 2, Eval (k0, i ) + Eval (k1, i ) = 

1[i = α] for all i < 2n.3) Committed bytes. Each module hashes exactly the bytes it consumes, in the public order, and releases nothing before the digest matches cm v . Under Good the consumed sequence therefore equals the committed padded snapshot 

D∗ in both modules, whatever bytes host b⋆ tries to substitute. 4) Correct reduction. Module arithmetic is ideal, so coordi-natewise 

˜y0 + ˜ y1 = X

and the fresh resharing preserves the sum: y0+y1 = ˜ y0+˜ y1.5) Release. The client accepts only a pair of valid certificates with roles 0 and 1 for its own qid and desc v . Under Good 

host b⋆ can neither forge a certificate nor splice a share from another query, version or role into the pair. Dropping or delaying a share leaves the client without a valid pair, which is an abort. Consequently the honest client outputs D∗[α] or ⊥, never another value, except with probability ϵsetup + ϵchannel + ϵbind .

(R1) index privacy. The simulator SPIR  

> b⋆

on the command sequence issued by host b⋆, the public descriptor and lengths, and the accept or abort outcomes. with a fresh pad ζ that is used nowhere else (after replacing derived tapes by random ones, ϵprf ); by Theorem 5 it is uniform and independent of α, of D∗ and of everything else in the view. ϵtrace . The index α is never used by the simulator. 

(R2) database privacy. The simulator SPIR cli has oracle access to Fabort PIR and must simulate the client’s view: the module responses (value shares and certificates) or a rejection. Its steps are: 1) Receive the client’s query messages to T0 and T1 through the emulated module interface and parse their canonical encodings. 2) If the two bindings differ, if an index is out of range, if an encoding is malformed, or if qid is replayed, output the rejection that the modules would output and submit a malformed query to Fabort PIR , which returns reject .3) Otherwise extract the agreed index α∗, submit it to Fabort PIR ,and receive D∗[α∗] ∈ Fkp .4) For each coordinate ι < k sample y0,ι 

> $

←− Fp and set y1,ι =

D∗[α∗]ι − y0,ι .5) Produce cert 0, cert 1 through the emulated module inter-face, bound to qid and desc v ; append the trace simulator’s output. In the real execution the hosts are honest, so by Steps 2– 4 of (R1) integrity the two reductions sum to D∗[α∗], and the fresh pad makes y0 uniform with y1 determined by the sum; this is exactly Step 4. Fresh pads per query make the responses to different queries independent. Since no MAC tag is delivered, the client’s view contains no relation that depends on the global keys, and desc v is part of LPIR . Hence the client learns nothing beyond the requested records and the descriptor. Collecting the terms, each regime is simulated with error at most ϵsetup + ϵprf + ϵchannel + ϵbind + ϵtrace .This is a hardware-trusted PIR statement: it does not turn untrusted two-server DPF-PIR into symmetric PIR, and a verifiable-FSS alternative with externally generated keys [33] must separately prove client key validity and server-answer integrity (Appendix A-B).

Lemma 9 (Check outcomes depend only on the deviation) .

In every hybrid below from H2 on, and in the ideal execution with S, the outcome of a CheckOpen batch that passes the metadata checks is V

(e, dj ) are the differences between the bytes frozen for host 

b⋆ and the faithful tuples, and ρj are fresh uniform challenges independent of (e, dj ).Proof. The honest host submits its prescribed tuples, so by Lemma 7 the residuals of Algorithm 4 satisfy σ0,j + σ1,j =

Both modules freeze the submitted bytes before the challenges are derived, and the challenge labels bind the frozen roots; in the ideal-randomness hybrids the challenges are therefore uniform and independent of (e, dj ).The candidate values and the challenges are never shown to the host before acceptance, so A fixes its deviation without seeing them. In the ideal execution, S evaluates the same predicate with identically distributed ∆j and ρj (Sim1, Sim9). 

C. Hybrid Argument Proof of Theorem 1. We define five hybrids. H0 is the real execution and H4 is the ideal execution with Fabort  

> C,μ

and S;

Pr[ Hi] denotes the probability that D outputs 1 on the joint output of Hi.

H0: real execution. A and the honest parties run the protocol of Section V, and each module runs the concrete module program: FT with its joint random function R instantiated by PRF K , its peer and client channels by AEAD over the hosts’ network, and OPEN_SESSION by attestation and au-thenticated key exchange. 

H1: ideal setup and channels. Replace the attestation and key exchange of every successful OPEN_SESSION by the ideal authenticated, role-bound interface of FT and simulate its public transcript; replace the AEAD traffic of the protected peer channel and of the client channels by ideal private deliveries that preserve only the permitted lengths and public schedule. A distinguisher yields an attack on the setup or on channel confidentiality or integrity, so 

| Pr[ H0] − Pr[ H1]| ≤ ϵsetup + ϵchannel .

H2: ideal randomness and bindings. Replace every output of PRF K or of the KDF by an independent uniform draw per distinct label, with identical draws for the same joint label in both modules (the random function R of FT); rejection sampling then yields exactly uniform field elements, and a bounded sampler that exhausts its retries aborts independently of all inputs. Further, let the experiment abort if any binding fails: a collision or forgery on a manifest hash, snapshot digest, frozen-page root or certificate, or a session-freshness violation. Hence 

| Pr[ H1] − Pr[ H2]| ≤ ϵprf + ϵbind .

In H2 the ledger guarantees that no randomness label is consumed by two accepted transactions, that roles cannot be swapped, and that no byte is replaced after a challenge exists. Two further replacements are exact. Wherever the implemen-tation samples a DPF or DCF target directly and recovers the mask by an inverse map, Lemmas 2 and 3 show that the joint distribution of mask, target and all auxiliary material equals that of the reference sampler; we apply them only where their joint-sampler conditions hold. Streaming generate-and-consume is replaced by materialized generation followed by evaluation, which by Theorem 3 yields identical outputs. After these replacements the analysis may treat each DPF or DCF as a key pair produced by Algorithm 1 (or Algorithm 8) and consumed by Algorithm 2. 

H3: abort on an accepted wrong value. H3 is H2 except that the experiment aborts at the first CheckOpen batch that is accepted with a nonzero value-error vector e; if Algorithm 11 is enabled, it also aborts at the first accepted candidate with 

C̸ = AB . Call these events Bad MAC and Bad F. The two hybrids are identical until a bad event occurs. Before Bad MAC 

the hypothesis of Lemma 1 holds: conditioned on host b⋆’s view and its own key shares, the honest key shares are indepen-dent and uniform, and every earlier rejection has terminated the epoch, so no adaptive key-learning sequence exists. By Theorem 6, each batch with e̸ = 0 is then accepted with probability at most (2 /p )t, and a union bound over at most 

Q batches gives Pr[ Bad MAC ] ≤ Q(2 /p )t. By Theorem 12, 

Pr[ Bad F] ≤ QF /p s. Therefore 

| Pr[ H2] − Pr[ H3]| ≤ Q(2 /p )t  + QF /p s.

Accepted tag-only deviations ( e = 0, dj̸ = 0) are not bad events: the opened values are the faithful ones, and a tag error that persists in a stored wire reappears only in the tag-error vectors of later checks, whose outcome is independent of the keys while e = 0 (Lemma 9; item (v) of the proof of Lemma 1). We do not claim that tag-only tampering is always detected. 

H4: ideal execution. The honest parties forward their inputs to Fabort  

> C,μ

, and A interacts with S. We show 

| Pr[ H3] − Pr[ H4]| ≤ ϵtrace .

We first exclude traces from both views and show by induction over the public schedule of interaction points that the joint distribution of A’s view and the honest outputs is identical in H3 and H4. The induction hypothesis after k interaction points states that (i) A’s view Vk is identically distributed in both hybrids, jointly with the global keys; (ii) the ledger state, the abort status and the faithful corrupt tuples are the same function of Vk and of the corrupt-side preprocessing tuples in both; and (iii) in H3 every mask, triple and pad that A has not seen in full is, conditioned on Vk, uniform subject only to the 59 

relations that S keeps symbolic, while every computed wire 

ν carries xν . The step distinguishes the kind of the next view element.  

> •

Preprocessing tuples (Sim5). In H3 they are produced by 

AuthShare under fresh labels, hence uniform on F1+ tp and independent of the value, the keys and Vk; S samples the same distribution.  

> •

Epilogue and direct-gate outputs (Sim11). By Theorem 5, for a fixed party the value share and the t tag shares are jointly uniform given the raw outputs and the global keys, and freshness of the pads makes this hold jointly across outputs and jointly with Vk. 

> •

Masked openings (Sim6, Sim8, Sim10, Sim11). Each opened value has the form S +M in Fp or in {0, 1}w, where 

S is a function of wire values and earlier randomness, and 

M is a fresh mask ( r of an honest owner’s input, the triple components a, b , the mask r of Algorithms 5, 6 and 9 or of a direct gate, or an output mask o) whose only other trace in Vk is the corrupt share tuple sampled independently in Sim5. By Lemma 8 the opening is uniform and independent of Vk, which is what S samples, and the hidden mask is then determined as M = u−S; this deferred definition is exactly 

S’s symbolic bookkeeping, so the joint law is unchanged although S does not know S. When the corrupt party is entitled to the complete mask (its own input mask, or the output mask of a corrupt recipient), that mask is fresh and uniform when delivered in both hybrids, and the opening is determined by A’s broadcast ε or equals υ = y + o for the output y from Fabort  

> C,μ

; S computes it in the same way. For a public output, the opened value is xν in H3 by (iii), which equals the value that Fabort  

> C,μ

returns.  

> •

Check outcomes (Sim9). By Lemma 9 the outcome is the same predicate of (e, dj , ∆j , ρj ) in both hybrids; (e, dj )

is the same function of Vk and the faithful tuples by (ii), 

ρj is fresh, and ∆j has the same joint law with Vk by (i). Rejections and the bad event lead to the same abort in both hybrids.  

> •

Metadata, ledger decisions, timeouts and ciphertexts (Sim2, Sim4). These are deterministic functions of A’s messages and of public data, or strings of public length, and are identical.  

> •

Validation and honest outputs (Sim6, Sim10). In H3 no wrong value is ever accepted, so by Theorems 7 to 11 together with Theorems 2, 4 and 5, applied inductively over the manifest’s gates, every released value equals the corresponding value of C on the honest inputs and the extracted inputs x∗; ΠInput authenticates r + ε = x∗, and the committed-input loader extracts the canonical committed input. Input-interface and validity aborts occur in H3 exactly where Sim6 emulates them from x∗ and status . An honest recipient therefore receives C(·) unless A suppresses deliv-ery, and S instructs Fabort  

> C,μ

to deliver or abort under exactly the same condition. The adversary keeps its permitted ability to abort after seeing its own output; no fairness is claimed.  

> •

Matrix verifications (Sim12). In both hybrids the outcome is that of Algorithm 11 on the same frozen candidate and the same operand records, which are the same function of Vk by (ii), with fresh secret challenges, and an accepted C̸ = AB 

is the same bad-event abort in both. After acceptance C is the faithful product, so (ii) and (iii) are preserved, and the gates that consume it fall under the epilogue case above. This completes the induction for trace-free views. Finally, in 

H3 the observable traces are produced by the ideal modules, whereas in H4 they are produced by Strace from the command sequence of host b⋆, μ, the public lengths and the accept or abort outcomes, which by the induction have the same joint law with the trace-free view in both hybrids. The trace assumption (Section VII-A and Appendix A-C) makes the real trace (lengths, timing, host-visible memory and DMA activity, power, thermal and clock telemetry) simulatable from exactly these inputs and independent of the global MAC keys, challenges and residuals given them (with DVFS isolation); this step costs at most ϵtrace over the entire admitted execution. 

Conclusion. By the triangle inequality, 

| Pr[ H0] − Pr[ H4]| ≤ ϵsetup + ϵprf + ϵchannel 

+ ϵbind + ϵtrace + Q(2 /p )t,

plus QF /p s when Algorithm 11 is enabled for at most QF

calls. This is the bound of Theorem 1; it has no DPF one-key-privacy term because no DPF key leaves the modules. With 

p = 2 61 − 1, t = 3 and Q ≤ 232 , the statistical term satisfies 

log 2 Q(2 /p )t ≈ 32+3(1 −61) = −148 , so the overall bound is dominated by the 128 -bit computational terms (Section VII-E). 

Scope. This is a stand-alone real/ideal statement with abort under sequential composition, for one admitted execution in the protected-module model. We claim no universal compos-ability, which would need UC models of the setup and of 

FT [63], [139], and concurrent sessions that share module state are not covered. S plays both modules, which see inputs under Profile T, so the theorem hides inputs from the corrupt host, its GPU and the network, not from the modules. Section VII-B lists what the bound omits, and Section VII-F what a concrete module must still establish. APPENDIX FPERFORMANCE MODEL 

The deterministic cost model used by the compiler is doc-umented in this appendix and released alongside the artifact. Built upon the published semi-honest DFA results [16], it is validated against the three experiments of Section VI-F. We have completed the specification, functional CPU reference model, GPU/RTL conformance checks, protected runtime, and deployment measurements (Section VIII-A).
