Components overlap only through the exposed fractions η of Table XV. The parties are symmetric, the query runs alone (batch size one), and the host schedules honestly; a malicious host can always delay or abort, which the security theorem permits and the model does not price. 

Peak engine rates. With one block per engine per cycle, the 

NAES = 64 engines deliver 46.08 G blocks/s ( 64 × 720 MHz) at 28 nm and 58.24 G blocks/s at 7 nm, and the field unit array delivers NFUA f7 = 116 .48 G multiply-accumulates/s (128 × 910 MHz; 92.16 G at 720 MHz). 

B. Calibration against the Semi-Honest DFA Rows 

The semi-honest trusted-DFA latencies [16] are decomposed as 

T SH = Glin + Goth + D + X + Bon 

BW + R · RTT ,X = Vcg /BW PCIe ,

where Glin is GPU time for linear algebra over Z264 , Goth 

other GPU time, D the DFA-side FSS time, Vcg the CPU-to-GPU volume printed by DFA (e.g., 2.55 GB for BERT-Base [16]), Bon the online bytes per party and R the dependent online rounds. With the values of Table XV(b), the model reproduces every trusted-DFA LAN and WAN latency to the precision DFA prints; the largest relative deviation is 0.42%, for ResNet-50 on LAN (0.7331 s against the printed 0.73 s). The two published latencies per model constrain only 

Glin +Goth +D and one linear combination of Bon and R. We fixed them so that the LAN and WAN rows are reproduced, D

is small relative to GPU time, since FSS evaluation stops being the bottleneck once keys no longer move [16], and R is of the order of the nonlinear depth of the compiled networks [10], [11]. Because the malicious factors act differently on each term, profiling this split on the A100 validates the decom-position (Section VIII). The dealer baseline’s GPU-only FSS time is tGPU FSS = D + ( T GPU LAN − T UDFA LAN ), the calibrated DFA time plus the LAN gap between DFA’s GPU and untrusted-DFA rows (for BERT-Base, 0.09 s plus 18 .8 − 18 .4 s [16]). The semi-honest bars of Fig. 5 use these terms unscaled and expose only the longer of the overlapping key distribution and host-to-GPU copy. For energy, k = ESH /(Glin + Goth + D) divides the published trusted-DFA energy [16] by the calibrated compute time (e.g., 18.2 J over 0.86 s, i.e., 21.2 J/s, for BERT-Base); it is validated against the power draw in Section VIII.

Authenticated linear algebra. Every linear layer runs on the GPU as 1 + t exact-field GEMMs of the same shape (the 61

Only 1 + t = 4 is structural; for BERT-Base, G′

1.25 × 0.52 s = 2 .60 s . ϕD covers the malicious generate-and-consume (both generator states plus the evaluator), the output conversion into Fp, the epilogue, and the gate mix of the compiler-selected default. The compiler minimizes modeled cost subject to an FSS-portability constraint: gate shapes marked FSS-portable in the manifest keep an FSS realization, so the same compiled circuit runs under Profile U; without it, the least-cost rule of Section V-J would pick the direct gate under Profile T wherever an approved direct form exists (the direct-only row). The portable shapes are small XOR LUTs and comparisons use the direct gate; ϕD = 1 .45 against 1.30 for direct-only is a lumped allowance for them (0.014 s of module time for BERT-Base), not a gate-by-gate count, and is why the default (3.83 s) is marginally slower than direct-only (3.82 s, Table XIII).

D. Baselines 

Dealer-based malicious FSS. This baseline, modeled after the protocol family of Shark [23], scales DFA’s semi-honest GPU row [16]: offline bytes and host-to-GPU volume grow by ψkey , GPU evaluation of authenticated keys takes ψev tGPU FSS ,energy grows by ψE, and linear algebra, online bytes and dou-bled rounds equal those of VIGOR-DFA; the offline material crosses the same network as the online traffic. Counting one tag tree per MAC lane (Table II) and 32-byte instead of 8-byte masks and triples would give (1 + t) = 4 × the semi-honest volume, so ψkey = 2 .2 favors the baseline; even ψkey = 1 ,i.e., no authentication overhead on keys and preprocessing, leaves 22.4 s for BERT-Base on LAN, 5.8 × the latency of VIGOR-DFA. With ψkey = 2 .2 the baseline takes 43.9 s: 37.6 s of offline distribution, 1.70 s of key copies, 0.98 s of FSS evaluation, and the GEMM, other GPU and network terms of VIGOR-DFA. Without distribution, VIGOR-DFA would be only 1.6 × faster. Unlike DFA’s GPU row, the model does not overlap key copies with distribution; doing so would give 42.2 s (11.0 × instead of 11.4 ×), well inside the slack of ψkey .Varying only BW (Section VIII-D), the baseline needs 387 s against 8.86 s for VIGOR-DFA at 0.1 GB/s (43.7 ×) and 9.49 s against 3.33 s at 10 GB/s (2.9 ×). 

PIR baselines and the power cross-check. The GPU-DPF and GPU+DFA PIR rows are DFA’s numbers [16], read as queries/s and mJ/query; their product, the implied power of Table IX, is physically plausible: 134 k×2.61 mJ ≈ 350 W for GPU-DPF and 1,125 k × 0.187 mJ ≈ 210 W for GPU+DFA at 16K entries, within 2 W of these values at 1M and 4M entries. For VIGOR-DFA PIR we achieve a PIR efficiency of 

ηPIR = 0 .93 relative to DFA throughput, a lumped allowance for the snapshot hash of the consumed bytes, fresh resharing, certificates and lockstep batching, at average power PPIR .

PPIR lies below DFA’s implied power because Algorithm 7 moves the DPF-weighted record reduction from the GPU into the protected field unit array. Unlike untrusted two-server DPF-PIR (GPU-DPF, DFA’s untrusted mode), which hides the index α without trusted hardware, both modules of VIGOR-DFA learn α, so its query privacy rests on module trust. As a capacity check, at 16K entries VIGOR-DFA evaluates 1.05 M queries/s × 214 leaves = 17 .1 G leaves/s, and three 61-bit coordinates per 128-bit record need 51 G multiply-accumulates/s, below the 92.16 G/s of the array even at 720 MHz. The authenticated GPU-DPF baseline in the style of APIR [47] is ψAPIR times GPU-DPF throughput at PGPU . For the sweep of Fig. 18(b), DFA and VIGOR-DFA throughputs 62 

scale as 1/N from DFA’s 18.4 G leaves/s [16], and GPU-DPF throughput times N is interpolated linearly in log N between DFA’s points at 214 , 220 and 222 entries and held constant beyond 222 .

E. Microbenchmark Models 

Single-path DPF gates. A malicious generate-and-consume gate of depth n performs 3n PRG expansions per module ( 2n

for the two generator states and n for the evaluator), i.e., 6n

AES blocks, plus 3 blocks for output conversion and pads. Its sustained throughput is ηpipe NAES f28 /(6 n+3) , versus 6n+1 

blocks for semi-honest trusted DFA and (1 + t)6 n + 3 with one tag tree per lane; for n = 61 this yields 106, 113 and 26.7 M gates/s (Fig. 15). The first gap stems mainly from ηpipe 

(0.85 against 0.90); the two extra blocks cost about 0.5%. One dependent gate takes (20 n+12) /f 28 , where 20 cycles per level is DFA’s pipeline depth [16], i.e., 1.71 μs for n = 61 .

Nonlinear gates. A width-w LUT costs 2(2 w + 2 w − 1) 

AES blocks (two-state generation along the target path plus full-domain evaluation) and 2w + w + 4 field multiplications. The conservative interval gate costs two DCFs of n(n + 1) /2

path levels at 6 blocks per level, 2 · 6 · n(n + 1) /2 = 22 ,692 

blocks for n = 61 ; the optimized interval gate uses 8 blocks per level for two O(n) DCFs (976 blocks) and is not covered by Theorem 4. Unlike the 6n + 3 of the single-path gate, these LUT and interval counts cover PRG expansions only: they exclude the two epilogue pad blocks and the output conversion into Fp, which in the single-path convention costs one block per converted leaf, i.e., 2w for a full-domain LUT. The direct gate is charged its two pad blocks and five multiplications, achieving 1.8 G gates/s. Bit decomposition costs 2n multiplications in 2n+1 rounds, and exact truncation 

5n + 2 multiplications in 5n + 4 sequential rounds (307 and 309 for n = 61 , counting the masked openings of its two decompositions; Appendix D). A gate’s LAN latency is its round count times 2RTT (value and residual exchange) plus its AES and multiplication time at peak rates, e.g., 30.9 ms for exact truncation; LUT and interval gates are charged one checked round, with their bit-level products batching with the preceding layer’s openings (Appendix F). These are the entries of Table VII. 

CheckOpen . For a batch of m elements, the latency sums freezing at 100 GB/s plus 0.2 μs, the value exchange (8m/ BW + RTT ), challenge derivation ( mt/ 2 AES blocks), residual computation ( 2mt multiplications), the residual ex-change ( 8t/ BW + RTT ) and a fixed 0.5 μs for the decision. On LAN with t = 3 this is 0.101 ms for m = 64 and 0.652 ms (100 M elements/s) for m = 65 ,536 ; the two round trips dominate small batches and t barely matters (Fig. 16). 

Ablations and sweeps. Each ablation of Table XIII changes one model input: tag trees replace the epilogue by scaling D′

by (1+ t) times an overlap factor 0.8; t = 1 , 2 change the lane count; FSS-only configurations set D′ = Mgate /throughput of the respective interval gate; direct-only drops the FSS-portability constraint of the compiler-selected default and scales D by 1.30 instead of ϕD; small batches add 0.41 s of per-batch overhead; the external PCIe module sets BW link to 25 GB/s with per-layer device trips; the ZCU102 configuration sets BW link to 1.6 GB/s and a triple rate of 32 lanes at 250 MHz (Table XII); and the no-check configuration keeps 

R′ = R, an insecure lower bound. 

F. Validity Limits 

Known blind spots. As a compiler cost model, it ignores contention between tag-lane GEMMs and module traffic be-yond ηNoC , scales peak engine rates by constant efficiencies, leaves out the area, power and bandwidth of the trusted on-die links from the DPF units to the VIGOR core (raw PIR leaf shares would need 137 GB/s there, so reductions stay in the units; Section VI-G), and omits network jitter, queueing and adversarial host scheduling.
